and
R={\cal F}_{\Delta}
, eq. (
3.76
) means

\displaystyle\Theta_{{\cal F}_{\Delta}\odot{\cal F}_{\Delta}}(q)=\frac{1}{2}\frac{q^{2\Delta}+q^{2\bar{\Delta}}+2q}{(1-q)^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{q^{2\Delta}+q^{2\bar{\Delta}}}{1-q^{2}}~.

(3.77)

For
\Delta_{k}=\frac{1}{2}+i\mu_{k},k=1,2
, let
{\cal K}_{\text{rel}}(\lambda)
be the relative density of principal series between
{\cal P}_{\Delta_{1}}\odot{\cal P}_{\Delta_{1}}
and
{\cal P}_{\Delta_{2}}\odot{\cal P}_{\Delta_{2}}
, and then it should satisfy

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\,d\lambda\,{\cal K}_{\text{rel}}(\lambda)\Theta_{\frac{1}{2}+i\lambda}(q)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{q^{2\Delta_{1}}+q^{2\bar{\Delta}_{1}}-q^{2\Delta_{2}}-q^{2\bar{\Delta}_{2}}}{(1-q)^{2}}+\frac{q^{2\Delta_{1}}+q^{2\bar{\Delta}_{1}}-q^{2\Delta_{2}}-q^{2\bar{\Delta}_{2}}}{1-q^{2}}\right)

(3.78)

which yields

\displaystyle{\cal K}_{\text{rel}}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\pi}\sum_{\pm\pm}\left(\psi\left(\frac{1}{2}\pm 2i\mu_{2}\pm i\lambda\right)-\psi\left(\frac{1}{2}\pm 2i\mu_{1}\pm i\lambda\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\pm}\left(\frac{1}{\cosh(\pi(\lambda\pm 2\mu_{1}))}-\frac{1}{\cosh(\pi(\lambda\pm 2\mu_{2}))}\right)~.

(3.79)

As explained in
Sun:2021thf
, the single particle states of a free massive scalar field in dS
2
can be identified with the principal series representation
{\cal P}_{\Delta}
if the mass squared
m^{2}=\Delta(1-\Delta)>\frac{1}{4}
.
The states
|n)
, introduced in section
3.1
, form an eigenbasis of
L_{0}
which is the
{\text{SO}}(2)
generator of global dS
2
.
The discussion above, implies that the two-particle Hilbert space of the same scalar field contains the discrete series representations
{\cal D}_{2}^{\pm},{\cal D}_{4}^{\pm},{\cal D}_{6}^{\pm},\cdots
.
For example,
{\cal D}_{k}^{+}
contains the normalizable lowest-weight state
|k)_{k}
, given in (
3.74
), that is annihilated by the generator
L_{-}
. One can think of such UIRs as purely right moving in dS
2
.

Similar to section
3.2.2
, for a numerical check of the analysis above, we need to construct the matrix representation of the Casimir operator restricted to the
L_{0}=0
sector of
{\cal F}_{\Delta}\odot{\cal F}_{\Delta}
. However, it does not require any extra work since the
L_{0}=0
sector of the symmetrized tensor
{\cal F}_{\Delta}\odot{\cal F}_{\Delta}
is nothing but the subspace
{\cal H}_{+}
of the usual tensor product
{\cal F}_{\Delta}\otimes{\cal F}_{\Delta}
. So the Casimir is given by
{\cal Q}^{(+)}
of
{\cal F}_{\Delta}\otimes{\cal F}_{\Delta}
, c.f. eq. (
3.2.2
). This simple observation has two immediate applications. First,
{\cal F}_{\Delta}\odot{\cal F}_{\Delta}
and
{\cal F}_{\Delta}\otimes{\cal F}_{\Delta}
contain the same complementary series representation, if exists, because we have checked numerically
{\cal Q}^{(-)}>\frac{1}{4}
. Second, the coarse-grained density of principal series in (truncated)
{\cal F}_{\Delta}\odot{\cal F}_{\Delta}
is the same as
\bar{\rho}_{{\cal N}}^{+}(\lambda)
for (truncated)
{\cal F}_{\Delta}\otimes{\cal F}_{\Delta}
, c.f. eq. (
3.44
). With the coarse-grained density known, we further define a relative density
\rho_{\rm rel}
between
{\cal F}_{\Delta_{1}}\odot{\cal F}_{\Delta_{1}}
and
{\cal F}_{\Delta_{2}}\odot{\cal F}_{\Delta_{2}}
. A comparison of
\rho_{\rm rel}
and
{\cal K}_{\text{rel}}
(c.f. eq. (
3.5.1
)) is shown in fig. (
3.8
).

Figure 3.8
:
Relative density of principal series between
{\cal P}_{\frac{1}{2}+3.5i}\odot{\cal P}_{\frac{1}{2}+3.5i}
and
{\cal P}_{\frac{1}{2}+1.5i}\odot{\cal P}_{\frac{1}{2}+1.5i}
. In the left panel, the pink line is
\rho_{\text{rel}}
and black dashed line is
{\cal K}_{\text{rel}}
, given by eq. (
3.5.1
). The right panel zooms into the local maximum and shows how
\rho_{\text{rel}}
approaches
{\cal K}_{\text{rel}}
(black dashed line) by increasing
{\cal N}
.

3.5.2
Counting complementary series in multi-particle Hilbert space

In section
3.2
, we have shown that the tensor product
\mathcal{C}_{\frac{1}{2}+\mu_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{C}_{\frac{1}{2}+\mu_{2}}
contains exactly one complementary series representation of scaling dimension
\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}
when
\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}>\frac{1}{2}
. This result also holds for symmetrized tensor product in the sense that
\mathcal{C}_{2\mu}\in\mathcal{C}_{\frac{1}{2}+\mu}\odot\mathcal{C}_{\frac{1}{2}+\mu}
when
2\mu>\frac{1}{2}
. In the QFT language, it means that given a light scalar
18
18

18

We use the word “light” for a field whose single-particle Hilbert space is described by a complementary series representation.

\phi
of scaling dimension
\Delta=\frac{1}{2}+\mu
or equivalently mass
m^{2}=\frac{1}{4}-\mu^{2}
, its two-particle Hilbert space contains an invariant subspace corresponding to a light scalar of mass
m^{2}=2\mu(1-2\mu)
when
\mu>\frac{1}{4}
. It is then natural to ask if there is any light scalar in any
M
-particle Hilbert space of
\phi
, and if yes, what is the corresponding mass. Group theoretically, the
M
-particle Hilbert space is described by the symmetrized tensor product
\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}^{\odot M}
, whose character
\Theta_{\mathcal{C}^{\odot M}_{\Delta}}(q)
can be extracted from the following generating function

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(q,t)\equiv\exp\left(\sum_{k\geq 1}\Theta_{\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}}(q^{k})\,\frac{t^{k}}{k}\right)=\sum_{M\geq 0}\Theta_{\mathcal{C}^{\odot M}_{\Delta}}(q)\,t^{M}

(3.80)

In particular, it is easy to check for
M=2
we recover eq. (
3.77
).

As discussed in section
3.2.2
, the first several terms in the small
q
expansion of
\Theta_{\mathcal{C}^{\odot M}_{\Delta}}(q)
contain all the information regarding invariant subspaces of
\mathcal{C}^{\odot M}_{\Delta}
that correspond to complement series. The generating function
\mathbb{P}(q,t)
provides a simple way to derive such expansions as follows.
In the exponent of
\mathbb{P}(q,t)
, for each fixed
k
, we expand
\Theta_{\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}}(q^{k})
as a series
(q^{k\Delta}+q^{k\bar{\Delta}})\sum_{n\geq 0}q^{nk}
. Switching the order of the two infinite sums, then the sum over
k
yields two logarithmic functions, which allows us to rewrite
\mathbb{P}(q,t)
as

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(q,t)

\displaystyle=\prod_{n\geq 0}\frac{1}{1-t\,q^{\Delta+n}}\frac{1}{1-t\,q^{\bar{\Delta}+n}}=\sum_{\bm{k},\bm{\ell}}\,q^{\alpha(\bm{k},\bm{\ell})}t^{\sum_{n}(k_{n}+\ell_{n})}

(3.81)

where
\mu=\Delta-\frac{1}{2}
and

\displaystyle\alpha(\bm{k},\bm{\ell})=\sum_{n\geq 0}\left(n+\frac{1}{2}\right)(k_{n}+\ell_{n})+\mu\sum_{n\geq 0}(k_{n}-\ell_{n})

(3.82)

Fixing the particle number
\sum_{n}(k_{n}+\ell_{n})=M
, we have

\displaystyle\alpha(\bm{k},\bm{\ell})=\Delta M+\sum_{n\geq 0}n(k_{n}+\ell_{n})-2\mu\sum_{n\geq 0}\ell_{n}

(3.83)

Noticing that
\sum_{n\geq 0}n(k_{n}+\ell_{n})\geq 0
and
\sum_{n\geq 0}\ell_{n}\leq\sum_{n\geq 0}(k_{n}+\ell_{n})=M
, where the two “=” hold if and only if
\bm{k}=0
and
\bm{\ell}=(M,0,0,\cdots)
, we find the minimal value of
\alpha(\bm{k},\bm{\ell})
to be
\bar{\Delta}M
. To obtain the second minimal value of
\alpha(\bm{k},\bm{\ell})
, which corresponds to
\ell_{0}\leq M-1
, we rewrite it as

\displaystyle\alpha(\bm{k},\bm{\ell})=\Delta M-2\mu\ell_{0}+\sum_{n\geq 1}(n(k_{n}+\ell_{n})-2\mu\ell_{n})\geq\Delta M-2\mu\ell_{0}+\sum_{n\geq 1}nk_{n}

(3.84)

where we have used
2\mu<1\leq n
in the sum over
n
. Then it is easy to see
\alpha(\bm{k},\bm{\ell})\geq M\bar{\Delta}+2\mu
when
\ell_{0}\leq M-1
. The equality holds for
\bm{k}=(1,0,0,\cdots)
and
\bm{\ell}=(M-1,0,0,\cdots)
. Altogether, we obtain the leading and subleading terms in the small
q
expansion of
\Theta_{\mathcal{C}^{\odot M}_{\Delta}}(q)

\displaystyle\Theta_{\mathcal{C}^{\odot M}_{\Delta}}(q)=q^{M\bar{\Delta}}+q^{M\bar{\Delta}+2\mu}+\text{higher order terms in}\,\,q

(3.85)

When
M\bar{\Delta}<\frac{1}{2}
, which corresponds to
\mu>\frac{M-1}{2M}
, there has to be one copy of
\mathcal{C}_{1-M\bar{\Delta}}
in the symmetrized tensor product
\mathcal{C}^{\odot M}_{\Delta}
to cancel
q^{M\bar{\Delta}}
since the latter cannot be reproduced by an integral over principal series characters. On the other hand, because of
M\bar{\Delta}+2\mu=(M-2)\bar{\Delta}+1\geq 1
for
M\geq 2
, there should not be any extra complementary series representations. It is worth mentioning that the same results also hold for the tensor product
\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}^{\otimes M}
which can be easily checked by using the rule of
\mathcal{C}_{\Delta_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{C}_{\Delta_{2}}
. A simple reason for the coincidence from the character viewpoint is that the first two terms in the small
q
expansion of
\Theta_{\mathcal{C}^{\otimes M}_{\Delta}}(q)
are
q^{M\bar{\Delta}}+Mq^{M\bar{\Delta}+2\mu}
.
Altogether, in the multi-particle Hilbert space of
\phi
, there exist
n_{\rm max}\equiv\left\lfloor{\frac{1}{1-2\mu}}\right\rfloor
complementary series representations, with scaling dimensions
1-\bar{\Delta},1-2\bar{\Delta},\cdots,1-n_{\rm max}\bar{\Delta}
.

4
Tensor products in
{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)

In this section, we generalize the character and numerical methods developed in section
3
to study tensor product decomposition of higher dimensional
{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)
, focusing on principal and complementary series of spin 0.
Let us first consider the tensor product of two scalar principal series representations
{\cal P}_{\Delta_{1}}\otimes{\cal P}_{\Delta_{2}}
with
\Delta_{k}=\frac{d}{2}+i\mu_{k}
. Each
{\cal P}_{\Delta_{k}}
consists of all the single-row representations of
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
. Using the following decomposition rule of orthogonal groups

\displaystyle{\mathbb{Y}}_{n}\otimes{\mathbb{Y}}_{\ell}=\bigoplus_{a=0}^{\text{min}(n,\ell)}\bigoplus_{b=0}^{\text{min}(n,\ell)-a}{\mathbb{Y}}_{n+\ell-2a-b,b}~,

(4.1)

we can immediately conclude that the
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
content of
{\cal P}_{\Delta_{1}}\otimes{\cal P}_{\Delta_{2}}
has at most two rows in the Young diagram language. This condition excludes a large class of
{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)
UIRs, including discrete series when
d\geq 5
, because discrete series representations should contain
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
content that has
\frac{d+1}{2}\geq 3
rows.

Thus,
{\cal P}_{\Delta_{1}}\otimes{\cal P}_{\Delta_{2}}
only contains the four types of UIRs which are reviewed in section
2
.
Among these UIRs, the exceptional series should be thought as the analogue of discrete series in the
d=1
case, since they can be characterized by certain linear relations (in terms of
{\mathfrak{so}}(1,d+1)
generators)
19
19

19

When
d=1
, these linear relations simply refer to the highest-weight or lowest-weight conditions, and for higher
d
, they are discussed in appendix
E
.
while the two continuous series can only be identified by the quadratic operator
C^{{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)}
. In appendix
E
, we show that the exceptional series cannot be present in this tensor product by directly checking the linear relations. Further assuming the absence of complementary series, which is actually proved by
Dobrev:1976vr
and will also be confirmed numerically later, the decomposition of
{\cal P}_{\Delta_{1}}\otimes{\cal P}_{\Delta_{2}}
can be written
schematically
as

\displaystyle{\cal P}_{\Delta_{1}}\otimes{\cal P}_{\Delta_{2}}=\sum_{s\geq 0}\int_{0}^{\infty}d\lambda\,{\cal K}^{(s)}(\lambda)\,{\cal P}_{\Delta_{\lambda},s},\,\,\,\,\,\Delta_{\lambda}=\frac{d}{2}+i\lambda

(4.2)

where
{\cal K}^{(s)}(\lambda)
is
formally
the density of spin
s
principal series. Proceeding with eq. (
4.2
) and replacing each UIR by the corresponding Harish-Chandra character, we would end up with a divergent
{\cal K}^{(s)}(\lambda)
. Following the strategy used in section
3
, we regularize
{\cal K}^{(s)}(\lambda)
by introducing two more principal series representation
{\cal P}_{\Delta_{3}}
and
{\cal P}_{\Delta_{4}}
as a reference, which then yields

\displaystyle\Theta_{\Delta_{1}}(q,\bm{x})\Theta_{\Delta_{2}}(q,\bm{x})-\Theta_{\Delta_{3}}(q,\bm{x})\Theta_{\Delta_{4}}(q,\bm{x})=\sum_{s\geq 0}\int_{0}^{\infty}d\lambda\,{\cal K}^{(s)}_{\rm rel}(\lambda)\,\Theta_{\lambda,s}(q,\bm{x})

(4.3)

where
\Theta_{\lambda,s}
means the character of
{\cal P}_{\Delta_{\lambda},s}
. The character equation (
4.3
) implies that
{\cal K}^{(s)}_{\rm rel}(\lambda)
should be understood as the relative density of spin
s
principal series between the two tensor products
{\cal P}_{\Delta_{1}}\otimes{\cal P}_{\Delta_{2}}
and
{\cal P}_{\Delta_{3}}\otimes{\cal P}_{\Delta_{4}}
.

4.1
The relative density

By extending
{\cal K}^{(s)}_{\rm rel}(\lambda)
to an even function of
\lambda
on the real line and using the explicit expression of Harish-Chandra characters, c.f. eq. (
2.14
), we rewrite (
4.3
) as

\displaystyle\sum_{s\geq 0}\chi^{{\text{SO}}(d)}_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{s}}(\bm{x})\int_{\mathbb{R}}d\lambda\,{\cal K}^{(s)}_{\rm rel}(\lambda)e^{i\lambda t}=\frac{4\,e^{-\frac{d}{2}|t|}}{P_{d}(e^{-|t|},\bm{x})}\left(\cos(\mu_{1}t)\cos(\mu_{2}t)-\cos(\mu_{3}t)\cos(\mu_{4}t)\right)

(4.4)

where we have made the substitution
q=e^{-|t|}
. To obtain an integral equation for each
{\cal K}^{(s)}_{\rm rel}(\lambda)
, we need to expand the R.H.S of (
4.4
) into an infinite sum of
\chi^{{\text{SO}}(d)}_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{s}}(\bm{x})
. Such an expansion exists thanks to the following relation

\displaystyle\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}\,\chi^{{\text{SO}}(d)}_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{s}}(\bm{x})\,q^{s}=\frac{1-q^{2}}{P_{d}(q,\bm{x})}

(4.5)

which is proved in appendix
C
for any dimension
d\geq 3
.
Plugging eq. (
4.5
) into eq. (
4.4
) yields

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}d\lambda\,{\cal K}^{(s)}_{\rm rel}(\lambda)e^{i\lambda t}=2\,e^{-(\frac{d}{2}+s-1)|t|}\,\frac{\cos(\mu_{1}t)\cos(\mu_{2}t)-\cos(\mu_{3}t)\cos(\mu_{4}t)}{|\sinh(t)|}~.

(4.6)

Then
{\cal K}^{(s)}_{\rm rel}(\lambda)
can be computed as an inverse Fourier transform using eq. (
A.3
)

\displaystyle{\cal K}^{(s)}_{\rm rel}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{4\pi}\sum_{\pm,\pm,\pm}\left[\psi\left(\frac{\frac{d}{2}+s\pm i\lambda\pm i\mu_{3}\pm i\mu_{4}}{2}\right)-\psi\left(\frac{\frac{d}{2}+s\pm i\lambda\pm i\mu_{1}\pm i\mu_{2}}{2}\right)\right]

(4.7)

where
\sum_{\pm,\pm,\pm}
means summing over the 8 sign combinations. Altogether, there are 16
\psi
-functions in
{\cal K}^{(s)}_{\rm rel}(\lambda)
.

4.2
Truncated model of the relative density

To ascertain it makes sense to identify
{\cal K}^{(s)}_{\rm rel}(\lambda)
as a relative density, we would like to compare this to a model with discretized spectrum of eigenvalues. In particular, we will focus on the
s=0
case, which corresponds to scalar principal series.

As reviewed in section
2
, an exclusive feature of any
{\cal F}_{\Delta,\ell}
with
\ell=0
is that it contains the trivial representation of
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
. Therefore we should be able to extract all information about
{\cal P}_{\Delta_{\lambda}}
in
{\cal P}_{\Delta_{1}}\otimes{\cal P}_{\Delta_{2}}
by only studying the
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
singlet subspace of the latter, denoted by
{\cal H}_{0}
.

{\text{SO}}(d+1)
singlets can only appear in the tensor product of two
{\mathbb{Y}}_{n}
subspaces, one in
{\cal P}_{\Delta_{1}}
and the other one in
{\cal P}_{\Delta_{2}}
. This structure yields a natural way to define a truncated model
{\cal H}^{({\cal N})}_{0}
of
{\cal H}_{0}
by simply cutting off the
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
spins in each
{\cal P}_{\Delta_{i}}
, e.g.
0\leq n\leq{\cal N}
. More explicit, let
\left\{|n,\Delta_{i}\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}},a_{j}=1,\cdots,d+1\right\}
be a basis of the
{\mathbb{Y}}_{n}
subspace in
{\cal P}_{\Delta_{i}}
, where
(a_{1}\cdots a_{n})
are symmetric and traceless. The normalization of
|n,\Delta_{i}\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}
is specified by eq. (
D.4
). We can then construct a normalized and orthonormal basis of the
({\cal N}+1)
dimensional Hilbert space
{\cal H}^{({\cal N})}_{0}
as

\displaystyle|\psi_{n}\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{D^{d+1}_{n}}}|n,\Delta_{1}\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}|n,\Delta_{2}\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}},\quad 0\leq n\leq{\cal N}

(4.8)

where
D^{d+1}_{n}=\text{dim}_{{\text{SO}}(d+1)}({\mathbb{Y}}_{n})
. Defining
\mathcal{Q}_{nm}\equiv\langle\psi_{n}|\mathcal{C}_{2}^{{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)}|\psi_{m}\rangle
, the nonvanishing entries of
\mathcal{Q}
are given by eq. (
D.23
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{nn}=\Delta_{1}\bar{\Delta}_{1}+\Delta_{2}\bar{\Delta}_{2}+2n(n+d-1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{n+1,n}=\mathcal{Q}^{*}_{n,n+1}=-\sqrt{\frac{(n+1)(n+d-1)}{(n+\frac{d-1}{2})(n+\frac{d+1}{2})}}(\Delta_{1}+n)(\Delta_{2}+n)~.

(4.9)

For large
{\cal N}
, each eigenvalue
q_{n}
of
{\cal Q}
that is larger than
\frac{d^{2}}{4}
corresponds to a scalar principal series representation with
\Delta=\frac{d}{2}+i\lambda_{n}
, where
\lambda_{n}=\sqrt{q_{n}-\frac{d^{2}}{4}}
. A coarse-grained density of scalar principal series representations in the truncated model defined by
{\cal Q}
is given by the inverse spacing of
\{\lambda_{n}\}

\displaystyle\bar{\rho}_{\cal N}(\lambda_{n})\equiv\frac{2}{\lambda_{n+1}-\lambda_{n-1}}

(4.10)

This coarse-grained density blows up when
{\cal N}\to\infty
because the matrix
\mathcal{Q}
has a continuous spectrum in
\left[\frac{d^{2}}{4},\infty\right)
in the large
{\cal N}
limit. Let
a_{n}(\lambda)
be an eigenvector of
\mathcal{Q}
with eigenvalue
\frac{d^{2}}{4}+\lambda^{2},\lambda\geq 0
, i.e.

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{n,n-1}a_{n-1}(\lambda)+\mathcal{Q}_{n,n}a_{n}(\lambda)+\mathcal{Q}_{n,n+1}a_{n+1}(\lambda)=\left(\frac{d^{2}}{4}+\lambda^{2}\right)a_{n}(\lambda)~.

(4.11)

This recursion relation leads to the following asymptotic behavior of
a_{n}(\lambda)

\displaystyle a_{n}(\lambda)=R_{+}\frac{n^{i(\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}+\lambda)}}{\sqrt{n}}\left(1+{\cal O}(1/n)\right)+R_{-}\frac{n^{i(\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}-\lambda)}}{\sqrt{n}}(\left(1+{\cal O}(1/n)\right)

(4.12)

where the coefficients
R_{\pm}
cannot be determined from an asymptotic analysis of the recursion relation. The asymptotic behavior (
4.12
) implies that the eigenvectors of
\mathcal{Q}
are
\delta
-function normalizable in the
{\cal N}\to\infty
limit.

Figure 4.1
:
Tensor product of two scalar principal series representations of
{\text{SO}}(1,4)
. Left: Low-lying eigenstates of the matrix
{\cal Q}
of the tensor product
{\cal P}_{\frac{3}{2}+i\mu}\otimes{\cal P}_{\frac{3}{2}+i\mu}
for various
\mu
. The cutoff is
{\cal N}=10^{4}
.
Right: Comparison of the relative coarse-grained density
\rho_{\text{rel}}(\lambda)
(pink solid line) given by eq. (
4.13
) to
{\cal K}^{(0)}_{\text{rel}}(\lambda)
(black dashed line) given by eq. (
4.7
) for
\Delta_{1}={\frac{3}{2}+4.3i},\Delta_{2}={\frac{3}{2}+1.3i},\Delta_{3}=\Delta_{4}=\frac{3}{2}+i\mu_{\text{rel}}=\frac{3}{2}+0.1i
, and
{\cal N}=2000
. The inset plot, zooming into the range
5.4<\lambda<6
, illustrates how
\rho_{\rm rel}
approaches
{\cal K}^{(0)}_{\text{rel}}(\lambda)
as we increase
{\cal N}
.

The difference of two coarse-grained densities, on the other hand, has a finite large
{\cal N}
limit. For example, given principal series
{\cal P}_{\Delta_{3}}
and
{\cal P}_{\Delta_{4}}
, we can construct another
({\cal N}+1)\times({\cal N}+1)
matrix like eq. (
4.2
) and extract a coarse-grained density
\bar{\rho}^{{\cal P}_{\Delta_{3}}\otimes{\cal P}_{\Delta_{4}}}_{{\cal N}}
from it. Define a relative coarse-grained density as

\displaystyle\rho_{\text{rel}}\equiv\bar{\rho}^{{\cal P}_{\Delta_{1}}\otimes{\cal P}_{\Delta_{2}}}_{{\cal N}}-\bar{\rho}^{{\cal P}_{\Delta_{3}}\otimes{\cal P}_{\Delta_{4}}}_{{\cal N}}~.

(4.13)

In fig. (
4.1
), we numerically show that
\rho_{\text{rel}}(\lambda)
approaches
{\cal K}^{(0)}_{\rm rel}(\lambda)
, c.f. eq. (
4.7
), as
{\cal N}\to\infty
.

Similar to the observation we had in section
3.2.2
, we can define a Pauli-Villars regularization of
{\cal K}^{(0)}(\lambda)
by taking
\mu_{3}=\mu_{4}=\Lambda
large in
{\cal K}^{(0)}_{\rm rel}(\lambda)

{\cal K}_{\rm PV,1}^{(0)}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi}\log\Lambda+\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{\pm}\psi\left(\frac{\frac{d}{2}\pm i\lambda}{2}\right)-\frac{1}{4\pi}\sum_{\pm,\pm,\pm}\psi\left(\frac{\frac{d}{2}\pm i\lambda\pm i\mu_{1}\pm i\mu_{2}}{2}\right)+{\cal O}(\Lambda^{-1})~.

(4.14)

We notice that for each fixed
{\cal N}
,
{\cal K}_{\rm PV,1}^{(0)}(\lambda)
matches the coarse-grained density
\bar{\rho}_{\cal N}(\lambda)
with a properly chosen
\Lambda
. In fig. (
4.2
), we show the remarkable match for the case of
{\cal P}_{\frac{3}{2}+4.3i}\otimes{\cal P}_{\frac{3}{2}+1.3i}
. In the coarse-grained density the cutoff is
{\cal N}=2000
, and in the Pauli-Villars regularization the cutoff is
\Lambda\approx 4000
.

Figure 4.2
:
Plot of the course-grained spectral density
\bar{\rho}_{\cal N}
(red dots) and its perfect match with
{\cal K}^{(s=0)}_{\rm PV,1}(\lambda)
(blue dashed line) defined in eq. (
4.14
) with
\Lambda\approx 4000
.

4.3
Analytical continuation to complementary series

Figure 4.3
:
Tensor product of two complementary series representations. Left: The first four eigenvalues of
{\cal Q}
for the tensor product
\mathcal{C}_{\frac{9}{2}+\mu}\otimes\mathcal{C}_{\frac{9}{2}+\mu}
of
{\text{SO}}(1,10)
with the cutoff being
{\cal N}=10^{4}
. The dashed line at
{\cal Q}=\frac{81}{4}
is the critical line between principal series and complementary series. The three solid lines are
{\cal Q}=(2\mu-2n)(9+2n-2\mu),n\in\{0,1,2\}
, corresponding to the
{\text{SO}}(1,10)
Casimir of
\mathcal{C}_{2\mu-2n},n\in\{0,1,2\}
. Right: The convergence of the minimal eigenvalue of
{\cal Q}
to its
{\cal N}\to\infty
limit for the tensor product
\mathcal{C}_{\frac{3}{2}+1.25}\otimes\mathcal{C}_{\frac{3}{2}+0.9}
of
{\text{SO}}(1,4)
.

While studying the tensor product of a principal series and a complementary series, e.g.
{\cal P}_{\Delta_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{C}_{\Delta_{2}}
, the whole procedure of solving
{\cal K}^{(s)}_{\rm rel}(\lambda)
above works exactly the same way up to the analytical continuation
i\mu_{2}\to\mu_{2}
. However, this simple analytical continuation prescription is not always valid in the case of
\mathcal{C}_{\Delta_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{C}_{\Delta_{2}}
with
\Delta_{1}=\frac{d}{2}+\mu_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}=\frac{d}{2}+\mu_{2}
, because as we have shown in appendix
A
, the integral (
4.6
) does not hold after the replacement
i\mu_{1}\to\mu_{1},i\mu_{2}\to\mu_{2}
if
\frac{d}{2}+s-\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}<0
. For example, assume
\frac{d}{2}+s+2N_{s}<\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}<\frac{d}{2}+s+2(N_{s}+1)
for some nonnegative integer
N_{s}
and then eq. (
A.9
) implies the following modification of eq. (
4.6
)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}d\lambda\,{\cal K}^{(s)}_{\rm rel}(\lambda)e^{i\lambda t}=

\displaystyle 2\,e^{-(\frac{d}{2}+s-1)|t|}\,\frac{\cosh(\mu_{1}t)\cosh(\mu_{2}t)-\cos(\mu_{3}t)\cos(\mu_{4}t)}{|\sinh(t)|}

\displaystyle-2\sum_{n=0}^{N_{s}}\cosh\left[\left(\frac{d}{2}+s-\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}+2n\right)t\right]

(4.15)

where

\displaystyle{\cal K}^{(s)}_{\rm rel}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{4\pi}\sum_{\pm,\pm,\pm}\left[\psi\left(\frac{\frac{d}{2}+s\pm i\lambda\pm i\mu_{3}\pm i\mu_{4}}{2}\right)-\psi\left(\frac{\frac{d}{2}+s\pm i\lambda\pm\mu_{1}\pm\mu_{2}}{2}\right)\right]

(4.16)

is a direct analytical continuation of eq. (
4.7
).
Because of the extra terms in (
4.3
) , the character equation (
4.3
) should change accordingly as (suppressing the arguments of characters)

\displaystyle\Theta_{\Delta_{1}}\Theta_{\Delta_{2}}-\Theta_{\Delta_{3}}\Theta_{\Delta_{4}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\geq 0}\int_{0}^{\infty}d\lambda\,{\cal K}^{(s)}_{\rm rel}(\lambda)\,\Theta_{\lambda,s}+\sum_{s}\sum_{n=0}^{N_{s}}\Theta_{\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}-s-2n}

(4.17)

where the sum over
s
is finite because
s<\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}-\frac{d}{2}<\frac{d}{2}
. Altogether, the character equation (
4.17
) implies that in the tensor product of complementary series representations
\mathcal{C}_{\Delta_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{C}_{\Delta_{2}}
, we get only principal series when
\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}\leq\frac{d}{2}

Dobrev:1976vr
, and we can also get additionally a finite number of complementary series representations when
\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}>\frac{d}{2}
. The scaling dimensions of these complementary series representations for each fix spin
s
are

\displaystyle\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}-s,\,\,\,\,\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}-s-2,\,\,\,\,\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}-s-4,\cdots,\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}-s-2N_{s}

(4.18)

where
N_{s}
satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{d}{2}+s+2N_{s}<\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}<\frac{d}{2}+s+2\left(N_{s}+1\right)~.

(4.19)

When
d=2
,
\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}
is bounded by
2
and hence
s=N_{s}=0
in eq. (
4.19
). In other words, there can be at most one complementary series representation, which has spin 0 and scaling dimension
\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}
, if exists.This result was first derived in
naimark1961decomposition
by Naimark. In higher
d
, the
s=0
version of eq. (
4.18
) was proved in
https://doi.org/10.48550/arxiv.1402.2950
by directly constructing the intertwining map between
\mathcal{C}_{\Delta_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{C}_{\Delta_{2}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}-2n}
. It would be interesting to generalize the intertwining map to incorporate complementary series with nonzero
s
.

Figure 4.4
:
Comparison of the relative coarse-grained density
\rho_{\text{rel}}(\lambda)
(pink solid line) given by eq. (
4.22
) to
{\cal K}^{(0)}_{\text{rel}}(\lambda)
(black dashed line) given by eq. (
4.16
) for
\mu_{1}=0.3,\mu_{2}=0.5,\mu_{3}=\mu_{4}=\mu_{\text{rel}}=0.1
, and
{\cal N}=2000
. The inset plot, zooming into the range
5.500<\lambda<5.520
, illustrates how
\rho_{\rm rel}
approaches
{\cal K}^{(0)}_{\text{rel}}(\lambda)
(black dashed line) as we increase
{\cal N}
.

The truncated model
{\cal Q}
described in the previous subsection can also be used to test these complementary series representations numerically. The nonvanishing matrix elements of
{\cal Q}
in the
\mathcal{C}_{\Delta_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{C}_{\Delta_{2}}
case are given by eq. (
D.24
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{nn}=\Delta_{1}\bar{\Delta}_{1}+\Delta_{2}\bar{\Delta}_{2}+2n(n+d-1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{n+1,n}=\mathcal{Q}_{n,n+1}=-\sqrt{\frac{(n+1)(n+d-1)(\Delta_{1}+n)(\bar{\Delta}_{1}+n)(\Delta_{2}+n)(\bar{\Delta}_{2}+n)}{(n+\frac{d-1}{2})(n+\frac{d+1}{2})}}~.

(4.20)

where
0\leq n\leq{\cal N}
.
We plot the first four eigenvalues of
{\cal Q}
in fig.(
4.3
), with
d=9,\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{2}=\frac{9}{2}+\mu
and
{\cal N}=10^{4}
. The smallest eigenvalue enters the complementary series regime around
\mu=\frac{d}{4}=2.25
and lies on the line
2\mu(9-2\mu)
, corresponding to
\mathcal{C}_{2\mu}
. The second eigenvalue starts to be smaller than
\frac{d^{2}}{4}=20.25
around
\mu=\frac{d}{4}+1=3.25
and then lies on the line
(2\mu-2)(11-2\mu)
, corresponding to
\mathcal{C}_{2\mu-2}
. The third eigenvalue becomes the Casimir of
\mathcal{C}_{2\mu-4}
roughly in the region
4.25<\mu<4.5
, since it lies on the line
(2\mu-4)(13-2\mu)
. The fourth eigenvalue is always larger than
\frac{d^{2}}{4}=20.25
and hence cannot belong to complementary series. These numerical results agree perfectly with eq. (
4.18
) and eq. (
4.19
) for
s=0
, which are derived from characters.

Apart from these discrete eigenvalues corresponding to complementary series, the matrix
\mathcal{Q}
defined by (
4.3
) also has a continuous spectrum in
[\frac{d^{2}}{4},\infty)
when
{\cal N}\to\infty
. The reason is that eigenvectors of
\mathcal{Q}
satisfying
\mathcal{Q}_{nm}a_{m}(\lambda)=(\frac{d^{2}}{4}+\lambda^{2})a_{n}(\lambda)
, have the asymptotic behavior

\displaystyle a_{n}(\lambda)=R_{+}\frac{n^{i\lambda}}{\sqrt{n}}\left(1+{\cal O}(1/n)\right)+R_{-}\frac{n^{-i\lambda}}{\sqrt{n}}(\left(1+{\cal O}(1/n)\right)

(4.21)

which implies that eigenvectors of different
\lambda
are
\delta
-function normalizable. The continuous spectrum corresponds to scalar principal series. We can define a coarse-grained density
\bar{\rho}_{\cal N}(\lambda)
of these representations, along the lines of eq. (
4.10
), by numerically diagonalizing the truncated matrix
{\cal Q}
. We also define a relative coarse-grained density

\displaystyle\rho_{\rm rel}(\lambda)=\bar{\rho}_{\cal N}^{\mathcal{C}_{\Delta_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{C}_{\Delta_{2}}}-\bar{\rho}_{{\cal N}}^{{\cal P}_{\Delta_{3}}\otimes{\cal P}_{\Delta_{4}}}

(4.22)

that has a finite large
{\cal N}
limit. In fig. (
4.4
), we illustrate the agreement between
\rho_{\rm rel}(\lambda)
and
{\cal K}^{(0)}_{\rm rel}(\lambda)

in eq. (
4.16
), using the tensor products
\mathcal{C}_{\frac{3}{2}+0.3}\otimes\mathcal{C}_{\frac{3}{2}+0.5}
and
{\cal P}_{\frac{3}{2}+0.1i}\otimes{\cal P}_{\frac{3}{2}+0.1i}
.

4.4
Photon from massless scalars

We have shown that the tensor product
{\cal F}_{\Delta_{1}}\otimes{\cal F}_{\Delta_{2}}
only contains UIRs in the continuous families, which describe massive fields in dS
d+1
. Given the similarity between scalar complementary series and type I exceptional series, which is mentioned in section
2
and described in more detail in appendix
D
, one would naively expect the same result for
{\cal V}_{p_{1},0}\otimes{\cal V}_{p_{2},0}
. However, we will show that this naive expectation is not true by using
{\cal V}_{1,0}\otimes{\cal V}_{1,0}
as an example. In particular, the tensor product
{\cal V}_{1,0}\otimes{\cal V}_{1,0}
contains a type II exceptional series representation
{\cal U}_{1,0}
. In field theory language, it means that the two-particle Hilbert space of two
different
massless particles admits an invariant subspace corresponding to a photon. To make the whole discussion very explicit, we will focus on the case of dS
4
. The same holds for any higher dimensional dS space.

Comparing
{\cal V}_{1,0}
and representations in scalar complementary series, the main difference is the absence of
{\text{SO}}(4)
singlets in
{\cal V}_{1,0}
.
Fortunately, this property does not affect almost any conclusion in appendix
E
, except for
{\cal U}_{1,0}={\cal U}^{+}_{1,0}\oplus{\cal U}^{-}_{1,0}
. To see what appends to
{\cal U}_{1,0}
, let us first recall that it is characterized by a normalizable state
|\chi\rangle_{a,b}=-|\chi\rangle_{b,a}
20
20

20

{\cal U}^{\pm}_{1,0}
corresponds to imposing (anti-)self-dual condition on this state, i.e.
\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{abcd}|\chi)_{c,d}=\pm|\chi)_{a,b}
.
that satisfies
L_{0b}|\chi\rangle_{a,b}=0
because
{\cal U}_{1,0}
does not contain spin 1 representation of
{\text{SO}}(4)
. In
{\cal V}_{1,0}\otimes{\cal V}_{1,0}
, such a state has to be a linear combination of

\displaystyle|\chi_{n})_{a,b}=|n\rangle_{aa_{2}\cdots a_{n}}|n\rangle_{ba_{2}\cdots a_{n}}-(a\leftrightarrow b),\,\,\,\,\,n\geq 1

(4.23)

where
|n\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}
is a basis of the
{\mathbb{Y}}_{n}
component in
{\cal V}_{1,0}
. Let
|\chi)_{a,b}=\sum_{n\geq 1}c_{n}|\chi_{n})_{a,b}
. In appendix
E
, we show that the condition
L_{0b}|\chi\rangle_{a,b}=0
leads to a recurrence relation of all
c_{n}
, c.f. eq. (
E
),

\displaystyle c_{n}\alpha_{n}+c_{n+1}\beta_{n+1}\frac{n+3}{n+1}=0,\,\,\,\,\,n\geq 1

(4.24)

where
\alpha_{n}
and
\beta_{n}
are given by eq. (
D.18
), which, for
\Delta=d=3,\bar{\Delta}=0
, reduces to

\displaystyle\alpha_{n}=\sqrt{\frac{n(n+1)(n+3)}{2(n+2)}},\,\,\,\,\,\beta_{n}=-\sqrt{\frac{(n-1)n(n+2)}{2(n+1)}}~.

(4.25)

Besides the recurrence relation (
4.24
), the requirement
L_{0b}|\chi\rangle_{a,b}=0
actually also implies an initial relation
c_{1}\beta_{1}=0
. For principal series or complementary series,
c_{1}\beta_{1}=0
is equivalent to
c_{1}=0
because
\beta_{1}\not=0
, which then leads to the vanishing of all
c_{n}
due to eq. (
4.24
). In the
{\cal V}_{1,0}
case, the initial relation does not impose any constraint on
c_{1}
because
\beta_{1}=0
. A general solution of all
c_{n}
is

\displaystyle c_{n}=\frac{A}{(n+1)(n+2)}

(4.26)

where
A
is an arbitrary constant. Using the inner product of
|\chi_{n})_{a,b}
, c.f. eq. (
E.16
), we find that
|\chi\rangle_{a,b}
is normalizable

{}_{a,b}\langle\chi|\chi\rangle_{a,b}=2|A|^{2}\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{1}{n(n+2)}<\infty

(4.27)

and hence
{\cal U}_{1,0}
belongs to
{\cal V}_{1,0}\otimes{\cal V}_{1,0}
. Altogether, we can conclude that
{\cal V}_{1,0}\otimes{\cal V}_{1,0}
contains exactly one exceptional series representation
{\cal U}_{1,0}
. We also want to mention that the same conclusion does not hold for the symmetrized tensor product
{\cal V}_{1,0}\odot{\cal V}_{1,0}
since the state
|\chi_{n})_{a,b}
in eq. (
4.23
) is manifestly anti-symmetric under permutation. So it is impossible to identify a photon like state in the two-particle Hilbert space of a single massless scalar field.

(a)

(b)

Figure 4.5
:
The tensor product
{\cal V}_{1,0}\otimes{\cal V}_{1,0}
of
{\text{SO}}(1,4)
. Left: Some low-lying eigenvalues of
{\cal Q}
, given by eq. (
4.31
). The dashed line is
{\cal Q}=\frac{9}{4}
. Right: Comparison of the relative coarse-grained density
\rho_{\rm rel}(\lambda)
given by eq. (
4.32
) to
{\cal K}_{\rm rel}^{(0)}(\lambda)
given by eq. (
4.4
) with
\mu_{3}=\mu_{4}=0.1
. The inset plot zooms into
5.50\leq\lambda\leq 5.52
, showing how
\rho_{\rm rel}(\lambda)
approaches
{\cal K}_{\rm rel}^{(0)}(\lambda)
(black dashed line) by increasing
{\cal N}
.

Next, we move to study the continuous part of
{\cal V}_{1,0}\otimes{\cal V}_{1,0}
, assuming that it does not support complementary series, which will be checked numerically later. Using
{\cal P}_{\Delta_{3}}\otimes{\cal P}_{\Delta_{4}}
as a regulator, the relative density of principal series
{\cal K}_{\rm rel}^{(s)}(\lambda)
between
{\cal V}_{1,0}\otimes{\cal V}_{1,0}
and
{\cal P}_{\Delta_{3}}\otimes{\cal P}_{\Delta_{4}}
is defined as

\displaystyle\Theta_{{\cal V}_{1,0}}(q,x)^{2}-\Theta_{\Delta_{3}}(q,x)\Theta_{\Delta_{4}}(q,x)=\sum_{s\geq 0}\int_{0}^{\infty}\,d\lambda\,{\cal K}_{\rm rel}^{(s)}(\lambda)\Theta_{\Delta_{\lambda}}(q,x)+\Theta_{{\cal U}_{1,0}}(q,x)

(4.28)

where
\Theta_{{\cal V}_{1,0}}(q,x)
is given by eq. (
2.17
), and
\Theta_{{\cal U}_{1,0}}(q,x)
is given by eq. (
2.19
) with
s=1,t=0
.
As we have seen in previous sections, in order to compute
{\cal K}_{\rm rel}^{(s)}(\lambda)
, we need to expand
\Theta_{{\cal V}_{1,0}}(q,x)^{2}P_{3}(q,x)
in terms of
{\text{SO}}(3)
character. In this discussion, we will focus on the
s=0
case (the rest of densities can be derived similarly), and hence it suffices to know the constant term in this expansion. Using eq. (
2.17
) and eq. (
C.1
), we find the constant term to be
\frac{4\,q^{6}}{1-q^{2}}+\frac{q^{2}(1+3q^{3})}{1+q}
. Plugging it into eq. (
4.28
) yields

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\,d\lambda\,{\cal K}_{\rm rel}^{(0)}(\lambda)e^{i\lambda t}=\left(\frac{4\,e^{-\frac{9}{2}|t|}}{1-e^{-2|t|}}-\sum_{\pm,\pm}\frac{e^{-(\frac{3}{2}\pm i\mu_{3}\pm i\mu_{4})|t|}}{1-e^{-2|t|}}\right)+\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2}|t|}+3\,e^{-\frac{7}{2}|t|}}{1+e^{-|t|}}+2\,e^{-\frac{3}{2}|t|}

(4.29)

where the last term comes from
\Theta_{{\cal U}_{1,0}}(q,x)
. Then
{\cal K}_{\rm rel}^{(0)}(\lambda)
can be computed as an inverse Fourier transformation of the R.H.S of eq.(
4.29
), term by term. For the first two terms, the inverse Fourier transformation follows from eq. (
A.3
). For the third term, one can use the integral formula,
\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\frac{e^{-zt}}{1+e^{-t}}=\frac{1}{2}\psi(\frac{1+z}{2})-\frac{1}{2}\psi(\frac{z}{2})
, where
{\rm Re\,}z>0
. For the last term, it is an elementary integral. Altogether, we obtain

\displaystyle{\cal K}_{\rm rel}^{(0)}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\pi}\sum_{\pm,\pm,\pm}\psi\left(\frac{\frac{3}{2}\pm i\lambda\pm i\mu_{3}\pm i\mu_{4}}{2}\right)-\frac{1}{\pi}\sum_{\pm}\psi\left(\frac{\frac{9}{2}\pm i\lambda}{2}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{3}{2\pi}\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}+\frac{1}{4}}-\frac{3}{2\pi}\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}+\frac{9}{4}}+\frac{15}{2\pi}\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}+\frac{25}{4}}-\frac{2}{e^{\pi\lambda}+e^{-\pi\lambda}}~.

(4.30)

which can also be recovered by taking
d=3,s=0
and
\mu_{1}=\mu_{2}=\frac{3}{2}
in eq. (
4.16
). This is not a coincidence but a result of the fact that
{\cal V}_{1,0}
of any
{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)
is the boundary point of complementary series up to a trivial representation of
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
. From the numerical side, this is also obvious because the
{\text{SO}}(1,4)
Casimir in the
{\text{SO}}(4)
singlet subspace of
{\cal V}_{1,0}\otimes{\cal V}_{1,0}
can be derived from that of
\mathcal{C}_{\Delta_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{C}_{\Delta_{2}}
case, c.f. eq. (
4.3
), by taking
\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{2}=3
and cutting off
n
at 1, i.e.

\displaystyle{\cal Q}_{n,n}=2n(n+2),\,\,\,\,\,{\cal Q}_{n+1,n}={\cal Q}_{n,n+1}=-n(n+3),\,\,\,\,\,n\geq 1~.

(4.31)

For numerical diagonalization, we impose a hard cut-off
n\leq{\cal N}
, and then
{\cal Q}
becomes an
{\cal N}\times{\cal N}
matrix. In the left panel of fig. (
4.5
), we plot the first four eigenvalues of
{\cal Q}
with the cut-off
{\cal N}
being
10^{6}
. They are all larger that
\frac{9}{4}
, implying the absence of complementary series representations in
{\cal V}_{1,0}\otimes{\cal V}_{1,0}
. Since the truncated
{\cal Q}
is larger than
\frac{9}{4}
, its eigenvalues induce a coarse-grained density of principal series representations
\bar{\rho}_{\cal N}(\lambda)
, which diverges in the
{\cal N}\to\infty
limit. We remove the divergence by introducing
{\cal P}_{\Delta_{3}}\otimes{\cal P}_{\Delta_{4}}
and considering a relative density

\displaystyle\rho_{\rm rel}(\lambda)=\bar{\rho}_{\cal N}^{{\cal V}_{1,0}\otimes{\cal V}_{1,0}}(\lambda)-\bar{\rho}_{\cal N}^{{\cal P}_{\Delta_{3}}\otimes{\cal P}_{\Delta_{4}}}(\lambda)

(4.32)

In the right panel of fig.
4.5
, we show a match between eq. (
4.32
) and eq. (
4.4
) with
\Delta_{3}=\Delta_{4}=\frac{3}{2}+0.1i
.

4.5
Some remarks on nonzero spin

For the tensor product of two spinning principal series representations, e.g.
{\cal P}_{\Delta_{1},s_{1}}\otimes{\cal P}_{\Delta_{2},s_{2}}
, it has been proved by
Dobrev:1977qv
that there can only be principal series and discrete series. Here principal series means a larger class of representations
{\cal P}_{\Delta,{\mathbb{Y}}}
that are labelled by a scaling dimension
\Delta\in\frac{d}{2}+i\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and a Young diagram
{\mathbb{Y}}
. The
{\cal P}_{\Delta,s}
representation is a special case of
{\cal P}_{\Delta,{\mathbb{Y}}}
when
{\mathbb{Y}}
has only one row, i.e.
{\mathbb{Y}}={\mathbb{Y}}_{s}
. The character of
{\cal P}_{\Delta,{\mathbb{Y}}}
is given by eq. (
2.22
).

When
d
is even,
{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)
does not admit discrete series, and hence
{\cal P}_{\Delta_{1},s_{1}}\otimes{\cal P}_{\Delta_{2},s_{2}}
is decomposed to principal series only. Based on this fact, we are going to discuss what kinds of principal series representations can appear in this tensor product by using character analysis. Since regularization is not relevant in this analysis, we just use the character relation
formally

\displaystyle\Theta_{\Delta_{1},s_{1}}(q,\bm{x})\Theta_{\Delta_{2},s_{2}}(q,\bm{x})=\sum_{{\mathbb{Y}}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\,d\lambda\,{\cal K}_{{\mathbb{Y}}}(\lambda)\Theta_{\Delta_{\lambda},{\mathbb{Y}}}(q,\bm{x})~.

(4.33)

For
\Delta_{1}=\frac{d}{2}+i\mu_{1},\Delta_{2}=\frac{d}{2}+i\mu_{2}
and
q=e^{-|t|}
, the eq. (
4.33
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\sum_{{\mathbb{Y}}}\chi_{\mathbb{Y}}^{{\text{SO}}(d)}(\bm{x})\int_{0}^{\infty}\,d\lambda\,{\cal K}_{{\mathbb{Y}}}(\lambda)e^{i\lambda t}=4\frac{\chi_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{s_{1}}}^{{\text{SO}}(d)}(\bm{x})\chi_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{s_{2}}}^{{\text{SO}}(d)}(\bm{x})}{P_{d}(e^{-|t|},\bm{x})}e^{-\frac{d}{2}|t|}\cos(\mu_{1}t)\cos(\mu_{2}t)~.

(4.34)

Then we need to expand the
\bm{x}
dependent part on the R.H.S of eq. (
4.34
) as a series summing over all
{\text{SO}}(d)
characters. It involves two steps.
First, using eq. (
4.5
), we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\chi_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{s_{1}}}^{{\text{SO}}(d)}(\bm{x})\chi_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{s_{2}}}^{{\text{SO}}(d)}(\bm{x})}{P_{d}(q,\bm{x})}=\sum_{s\geq 0}\chi_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{s_{1}}}^{{\text{SO}}(d)}(\bm{x})\chi_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{s_{2}}}^{{\text{SO}}(d)}(\bm{x})\chi_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{s}}^{{\text{SO}}(d)}(\bm{x})\frac{q^{s}}{1-q^{2}}~.

(4.35)

Next, we express the product of three
{\text{SO}}(d)
characters
\chi_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{s_{1}}}^{{\text{SO}}(d)}(\bm{x})\chi_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{s_{2}}}^{{\text{SO}}(d)}(\bm{x})\chi_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{s}}^{{\text{SO}}(d)}(\bm{x})
as a finite sum of
{\text{SO}}(d)
characters, which is equivalent to the tensor product decomposition of
{\mathbb{Y}}_{s_{1}}\otimes{\mathbb{Y}}_{s_{2}}\otimes{\mathbb{Y}}_{s}
as
{\text{SO}}(d)
representations. Thus, we can conclude that a principal series representation
{\cal P}_{\Delta,{\mathbb{Y}}}
appears in
{\cal P}_{\Delta_{1},s_{1}}\otimes{\cal P}_{\Delta_{2},s_{2}}
if and only if
{\mathbb{Y}}
belongs to
{\mathbb{Y}}_{s_{1}}\otimes{\mathbb{Y}}_{s_{2}}\otimes{\mathbb{Y}}_{s}
for some
s\geq 0
. For example, when
s_{1}=1
and
s_{2}=0
, the tensor products
{\mathbb{Y}}_{1}\otimes{\mathbb{Y}}_{0}\otimes{\mathbb{Y}}_{s}={\mathbb{Y}}_{1}\otimes{\mathbb{Y}}_{s}
yield all single row representations and all
{\mathbb{Y}}_{n,1},n\geq 1
. So the tensor product of a spin 1 and a spin 0 principal series representations contains all
{\cal P}_{\Delta,s}\,(s\geq 0)
and all
{\cal P}_{\Delta,{\mathbb{Y}}_{s,1}}\,(s\geq 1)
. For
s_{1}=s_{2}=1
, using the decomposition rule

\displaystyle{\mathbb{Y}}_{1}\otimes{\mathbb{Y}}_{1}\otimes{\mathbb{Y}}_{s}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{Y}}_{1}\otimes\left({\mathbb{Y}}_{s-1}\oplus{\mathbb{Y}}_{s+1}\oplus{\mathbb{Y}}_{s,1}\right)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{Y}}_{s-2}\oplus 3{\mathbb{Y}}_{s}\oplus{\mathbb{Y}}_{s+2}\oplus 2\left({\mathbb{Y}}_{s+1,1}\oplus{\mathbb{Y}}_{s-1,1}\right)\oplus{\mathbb{Y}}_{s,2}\oplus{\mathbb{Y}}_{s,1,1}~,

(4.36)

one can easily find that the tensor product of two spin 1 principal series representations contains all
{\cal P}_{\Delta,s}\,(s\geq 0)
,
{\cal P}_{\Delta,{\mathbb{Y}}_{s,1}}\,(s\geq 1)
,
{\cal P}_{\Delta,{\mathbb{Y}}_{s,2}}\,(s\geq 2)
and
{\cal P}_{\Delta,{\mathbb{Y}}_{s,1,1}}\,(s\geq 1)
. Although, we start with even
d
, we believe this result is valid for both even and odd
d
.

In
Dobrev:1977qv
, the possible
{\mathbb{Y}}
were found by a much more sophisticated method, that is independent of the parity of
d
. The result can be described as follows. Let
\rho
be a UIR of
{\text{SO}}(d)
contained in
{\mathbb{Y}}_{s_{1}}\otimes{\mathbb{Y}}_{s_{2}}
and
\sigma
be a UIR of
{\text{SO}}(d-1)
contained in the restriction of
\rho
to
{\text{SO}}(d-1)
. A principal series representation
{\cal P}_{\Delta,{\mathbb{Y}}}
belongs to
{\cal P}_{\Delta_{1},s_{1}}\otimes{\cal P}_{\Delta_{2},s_{2}}
if and only if some
\sigma
obtained in this way belongs to the restriction of
{\mathbb{Y}}
to
{\text{SO}}(d-1)
.
As shown in
Kravchuk:2016qvl
, this constraint on
{\mathbb{Y}}
is equivalent to saying that
{\mathbb{Y}}
belongs to
{\mathbb{Y}}_{s_{1}}\otimes{\mathbb{Y}}_{s_{2}}\otimes{\mathbb{Y}}_{s}
for some
s\geq 0
. To see this, we only need a simple fact that given two irreducible representations
{\mathbb{Y}}
and
{\mathbb{Y}}^{\prime}
of
{\text{SO}}(d)
, the tensor product
{\mathbb{Y}}\otimes{\mathbb{Y}}^{\prime}
contains the trivial representation if and only if
{\mathbb{Y}}={\mathbb{Y}}^{\prime}
. Then identifying an irreducible component
{\mathbb{Y}}
in some reducible representation
R
amounts to checking if
\bullet\in{\mathbb{Y}}\otimes R
, where
\bullet
denotes the trivial representation. Based on this fact, we can immediately see that the following equivalence

\displaystyle{\mathbb{Y}}\subset{\mathbb{Y}}_{s_{1}}\otimes{\mathbb{Y}}_{s_{2}}\otimes\sum_{s\geq 0}{\mathbb{Y}}_{s}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\exists\,s\geq 0:{\mathbb{Y}}_{s}\subset{\mathbb{Y}}\otimes{\mathbb{Y}}_{s_{1}}\otimes{\mathbb{Y}}_{s_{2}}\,.

(4.37)

as both sides mean
\bullet\in{\mathbb{Y}}_{s_{1}}\otimes{\mathbb{Y}}_{s_{2}}\otimes{\mathbb{Y}}\otimes{\mathbb{Y}}_{s}
for some
s
.
Then, reduce the last formula from
{\text{SO}}(d)
to
{\text{SO}}(d-1)
.
Since
{\mathbb{Y}}_{s}
are the only irreducible representations of
{\text{SO}}(d)
that give rise to
{\text{SO}}(d-1)
singlets, we conclude that (
4.37
) is equivalent to the statement that there is at least one
{\text{SO}}(d-1)
singlet inside the
{\text{SO}}(d)
tensor product
{\mathbb{Y}}\otimes{\mathbb{Y}}_{s_{1}}\otimes{\mathbb{Y}}_{s_{2}}
. But this is equivalent to the statement that
there is at least one irreducible representation
\sigma
of
{\text{SO}}(d-1)
that appears both inside

{\mathbb{Y}}
and
{\mathbb{Y}}_{s_{1}}\otimes{\mathbb{Y}}_{s_{2}}
.

With the possible
{\text{SO}}(d)
structures
{\mathbb{Y}}
known, we can in principle compute the relative density of each
{\cal P}_{\Delta_{\lambda},{\mathbb{Y}}}
between
{\cal P}_{\Delta_{1},s_{1}}\otimes{\cal P}_{\Delta_{2},s_{2}}
and
{\cal P}_{\Delta_{3},s_{1}}\otimes{\cal P}_{\Delta_{4},s_{2}}
. For example, in the case of
s_{1}=1,s_{2}=0
, the relative densities of
{\cal P}_{\Delta_{\lambda},{\mathbb{Y}}_{s}}
(
s\geq 0
) and
{\cal P}_{\Delta_{\lambda},{\mathbb{Y}}_{s,1}}
(
s\geq 1
) should satisfy (suppressing the arguments of characters)

\displaystyle\Theta_{\Delta_{1},1}\Theta_{\Delta_{2}}-\Theta_{\Delta_{3},1}\Theta_{\Delta_{4}}=\sum_{s\geq 0}\int_{0}^{\infty}\,d\lambda\,{\cal K}_{\rm rel}^{(s)}(\lambda)\Theta_{\Delta_{\lambda},s}+\sum_{s\geq 1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\,d\lambda\,{\cal K}_{\rm rel}^{(s,1)}(\lambda)\Theta_{\Delta_{\lambda},{\mathbb{Y}}_{s,1}}~.

(4.38)

As we have seen in eq. (
4.35
), the difference compared to the
s_{1}=s_{2}=0
case comes mainly from the following expansion

\displaystyle\frac{\chi^{{\text{SO}}(d)}_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{1}}(\bm{x})}{P_{d}(q,\bm{x})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\geq 0}\chi^{{\text{SO}}(d)}_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{1}}(\bm{x})\chi^{{\text{SO}}(d)}_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{s}}(\bm{x})\frac{q^{s}}{1-q^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\chi^{{\text{SO}}(d)}_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{1}}(\bm{x})}{1-q^{2}}+\sum_{s\geq 1}\left(\chi^{{\text{SO}}(d)}_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{s-1}}(\bm{x})+\chi^{{\text{SO}}(d)}_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{s+1}}(\bm{x})+\chi^{{\text{SO}}(d)}_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{s,1}}(\bm{x})\right)\frac{q^{s}}{1-q^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{q}{1-q^{2}}+\sum_{s\geq 1}\chi^{{\text{SO}}(d)}_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{s}}(\bm{x})\frac{q^{s-1}+q^{s+1}}{1-q^{2}}+\sum_{s\geq 1}\chi^{{\text{SO}}(d)}_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{s,1}}(\bm{x})\frac{q^{s}}{1-q^{2}}~.

(4.39)

Plugging (
4.5
) into the character relation (
4.38
), we obtain

\displaystyle{\cal K}_{\rm rel}^{(s)}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{4\pi}\begin{cases}\sum_{\pm,\pm,\pm}\left[\psi\left(\frac{\frac{d}{2}+1\pm i\lambda\pm i\mu_{3}\pm i\mu_{4}}{2}\right)-\psi\left(\frac{\frac{d}{2}+1\pm i\lambda\pm i\mu_{1}\pm i\mu_{2}}{2}\right)\right],&s=0\\
\sum_{\pm,\pm,\pm,\pm}\left[\psi\left(\frac{\frac{d}{2}+s\pm 1\pm i\lambda\pm i\mu_{3}\pm i\mu_{4}}{2}\right)-\psi\left(\frac{\frac{d}{2}+s\pm 1\pm i\lambda\pm i\mu_{1}\pm i\mu_{2}}{2}\right)\right],&s\geq 1\end{cases}

(4.40)

and

\displaystyle{\cal K}_{\rm rel}^{(s,1)}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{4\pi}\sum_{\pm,\pm,\pm}\left[\psi\left(\frac{\frac{d}{2}+s\pm i\lambda\pm i\mu_{3}\pm i\mu_{4}}{2}\right)-\psi\left(\frac{\frac{d}{2}+s\pm i\lambda\pm i\mu_{1}\pm i\mu_{2}}{2}\right)\right],\,\,\,\,s\geq 1~.

(4.41)

The analytical continuation
i\mu_{1}\to\mu_{1},i\mu_{2}\to\mu_{2}
in eq. (
4.40
) and eq. (
4.41
) leads to relative density of principal series between
\mathcal{C}_{\Delta_{1},1}\otimes\mathcal{C}_{\Delta_{2}}
and
{\cal P}_{\Delta_{3},1}\otimes{\cal P}_{\Delta_{4}}
, where
\Delta_{1}=\frac{d}{2}+\mu_{1}
and
\Delta=\frac{d}{2}+\mu_{2}
. After the analytical continuation,
\mu_{1}\in(0,\frac{d}{2}-1)
and
\mu_{2}\in(0,\frac{d}{2})
. There can be pole crossing when we vary
\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}
if
d
is large enough. For example, when
d=4
, pole crossing happens in
\psi(\frac{2-\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}\pm i\lambda}{2})
, which is contained in
{\cal K}^{(1)}_{\rm rel}
. It implies that when
\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}>2
, besides principal series representations, there also exists a spin 1 complementary series representation
\mathcal{C}_{\mu_{1}+\mu_{2},1}
. A complete analysis of complementary series for higher dimension is very similar to subsection
4.3
.

For odd
d
, discrete series representations can appear in
{\cal F}_{\Delta_{1},s_{1}}\otimes{\cal F}_{\Delta_{2},s_{2}}
, given that one of the spins is nonzero. The full spectrum of discrete series representations in such a tensor product is not known for a generic odd
d
. When
d\geq 9
, we can exclude discrete series representations in
{\cal F}_{\Delta_{1},s_{1}}\otimes{\cal F}_{\Delta_{2},s_{2}}
, by simply analyzing its
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
components. On the one hand, a discrete series of
{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)
should contain
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
UIRs corresponding to Young diagrams with
\frac{d+1}{2}\geq 5
rows. On the other hand, the
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
content of both
{\cal F}_{\Delta_{1},s_{1}}
and
{\cal F}_{\Delta_{2},s_{2}}
has at most two rows and hence their tensor product yields
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
UIRs that have at most four rows. When
d=3
, the problem is solved by Martin
Martin2
;
Martin
if at least one of the
{\cal F}_{\Delta_{i},s_{i}}
belongs to principal series. For example, according to Martin,
{\cal P}_{\Delta_{1},1}\otimes{\cal P}_{\Delta_{2}}
contains all

{\cal U}^{\pm}_{s,0},s\geq 1
, with multiplicity one for each
21
21

21

Martin used Dixmier’s notation
\pi^{\pm}_{p,q}

Dix
to denote discrete series representations, where
p\geq 1
and
q=1,2,\cdots p
. In our notation,
\pi^{\pm}_{p,q}
should be identified as
{\cal U}^{\pm}_{p,q-1}
.
. In particular,
{\cal U}^{+}_{1,0}\oplus{\cal U}^{-}_{1,0}
is isomorphic to the single-particle Hilbert space of a photon in
\text{dS}_{d+1}
.

4.5.1
The case of
SO
(1,3)

As an explicit example, let’s decompose the tensor product
{\cal P}_{\Delta,s_{1}}\otimes{\cal P}_{\Delta_{2},s_{2}}
of
{\text{SO}}(1,3)
. Due to the isomorphism
{\cal P}_{\Delta,s}\cong{\cal P}_{\bar{\Delta},-s}
, we will always consider nonnegative spins, and let
\Delta
run along the whole line
1+i\mathbb{R}
when
s\geq 1
and the half line
1+i\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
when
s=0
. Without loss of generality, we also assume
s_{1}\geq s_{2}
. Formally, the following character equation is expected to hold as a manifestation of the tensor product decomposition

\displaystyle\Theta_{\Delta_{1},s_{1}}(q,x)\Theta_{\Delta_{2},s_{2}}(q,x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\,{\cal K}^{(0)}(\lambda)\Theta_{1+i\lambda}(q,x)+\sum_{s\geq 1}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\,{\cal K}^{(s)}(\lambda)\Theta_{1+i\lambda,s}(q,x)

(4.42)

where
\Delta_{1}=1+i\mu_{1},\Delta_{2}=1+i\mu_{2}
, and
{\cal K}^{(s)}(\lambda)
is understood as the density of spin
s
principal series. Using eq. (
2.21
) for
\Theta_{\Delta,s}(q,x)
, we find that (
4.42
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\frac{q(x^{s_{1}}q^{i\mu_{1}}+x^{-s_{1}}q^{-i\mu_{1}})(x^{s_{2}}q^{i\mu_{2}}+x^{-s_{2}}q^{-i\mu_{2}})}{(1-xq)(1-x^{-1}q)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\geq 1}\int_{\mathbb{R}}d\lambda\,{\cal K}^{(s)}(\lambda)\left(x^{s}\,q^{i\lambda}+x^{-s}\,q^{-i\lambda}\right)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{\infty}d\lambda\,{\cal K}^{(0)}(\lambda)\left(q^{i\lambda}+q^{-i\lambda}\right)

(4.43)

Denote the L.H.S of eq. (
4.5.1
) by
\Phi
, the R.H.S imposes a very nontrivial constraint, namely
\Phi(q,x)=\Phi(q^{-1},x^{-1})
, or equivalently
\Phi(t,\theta)=\Phi(-t,-\theta)
if we make the substitution
q=e^{-t}
and
x=e^{i\theta}
. It easy to check that
\Phi(t,\theta)=\frac{2\cos(\mu_{1}t-s_{1}\theta)\cos(\mu_{2}t-s_{2}\theta)}{\cosh t-\cos\theta}
satisfies this condition.

To proceed, we need to match the coefficients of any
x^{\pm s}
on both sides of eq. (
4.5.1
). It requires the Fourier expansion of
(1-xq)^{-1}(1-x^{-1}q)^{-1}
, which depends on the sign of
1-q
:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(1-xq)(1-x^{-1}q)}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\,x^{n}\times\begin{cases}\frac{q^{|n|}}{1-q^{2}}&0<q<1\\
\frac{q^{-|n|}}{q^{2}-1}&q>1\end{cases}

(4.44)

Because of the pole at
q=1
for each Fourier coefficient,
{\cal K}^{(s)}(\lambda)
defined in (
4.42
) is divergent. In order to regularize it, we introduce another tensor product
{\cal P}_{\Delta_{3}}\otimes{\cal P}_{\Delta_{4}}
, and compute a relative density
{\cal K}_{\rm rel}^{(s)}(\lambda)
of principal series between between
{\cal P}_{\Delta,s_{1}}\otimes{\cal P}_{\Delta_{2},s_{2}}
and
{\cal P}_{\Delta_{3}}\otimes{\cal P}_{\Delta_{4}}
. By matching the coefficient of
x^{0}
, we find

\displaystyle{\cal K}_{\rm rel}^{(0)}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4\pi}\sum_{\pm,\pm}\left(\psi\left(\frac{s_{1}+s_{2}+1\pm i(\mu_{1}+\mu_{2})\pm i\lambda}{2}\right)+\psi\left(\frac{s_{1}-s_{2}+1\pm i(\mu_{1}-\mu_{2})\pm i\lambda}{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4\pi}\sum_{\pm,\pm,\pm}\psi\left(\frac{1\pm i\mu_{3}\pm i\mu_{4}\pm i\lambda}{2}\right)

(4.45)

When
s_{1}=s_{2}=0
, (
4.5.1
) reduces to eq. (
4.7
) with
d=2,s=0
. Pole crossing does not happen if we move
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
along the real axis, or analytically continue one of the two
{\cal P}_{\Delta_{i},s_{i}}
to a complementary series representation. It suggests the absence of complementary series in any
{\cal P}\otimes{\cal P}
or
{\cal P}\otimes\mathcal{C}
of
{\text{SO}}(1,3)
. The
\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathcal{C}
case has been studied in section
4.3
. Similarly, by matching
x^{s}
, we obtain

\displaystyle{\cal K}_{\rm rel}^{(s)}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4\pi}\sum_{\pm}\left(\psi\left(\frac{|s\!\pm\!(s_{1}\!+\!s_{2})|\!+\!1\!\mp\!i(\mu_{1}\!+\!\mu_{2})\!-\!i\lambda}{2}\right)\!-\!\psi\left(\frac{|s\!\pm\!(s_{1}\!-\!s_{2})|\!+\!1\!\mp\!i(\mu_{1}\!-\!\mu_{2})\!-\!i\lambda}{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4\pi}\sum_{\pm}\left(\psi\left(\frac{|s\!\pm\!(s_{1}\!+\!s_{2})|\!+\!1\!\pm\!i(\mu_{1}\!+\!\mu_{2})\!+\!i\lambda}{2}\right)\!-\!\psi\left(\frac{|s\!\pm\!(s_{1}\!-\!s_{2})|\!+\!1\!\pm\!i(\mu_{1}\!-\!\mu_{2})\!+\!i\lambda}{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4\pi}\sum_{\pm,\pm,\pm}\psi\left(\frac{s+1\pm i\mu_{3}\pm i\mu_{4}\pm i\lambda}{2}\right).

(4.46)

5
Conformal field theory in de Sitter

Since dS spacetime is conformally equivalent to flat spacetime, the full Hilbert space
{\cal H}_{\text{CFT}}
of a CFT in
\text{dS}_{d+1}
is a direct sum of unitary and irreducible primary representations of the Lorentzian conformal group
{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)
. (A short review of
{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)
and its representations can be found in appendix
F
).
Then understanding the representation structure of
{\cal H}_{\text{CFT}}
under the dS isometry group
{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)
amounts to decomposing primary representations of
{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)
into UIRs of
{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)
.

In representation theory of compact groups, the Weyl character is a powerful tool to decompose a UIR
R
of
G
to UIRs of its subgroup
H
. For example, let
G={\text{SO}}(4),H={\text{SO}}(3)
and
R=\tiny\hbox{}\hskip 0.0pt\vbox{\vbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt}}\hskip 0.0pt
. The
{\text{SO}}(4)
Weyl character associated to

is

\displaystyle\chi^{{\text{SO}}(4)}_{\mbox{\scriptsize\tiny\hbox{}\hskip 0.0pt{\vbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt}}\hskip 0.0pt}}(x,y)=\frac{(x+y)(xy+1)\left(x^{2}y^{2}+1\right)}{x^{2}y^{2}}~.

(5.1)

Since
G
and
H
have only one common Cartan generator (denoted by
J_{3}
), we take
y=1
in eq. (
5.1
), which effectively computes
\,{\rm tr}\,\,x^{J_{3}}
in the representation

, and then the character becomes

\displaystyle\chi^{{\text{SO}}(4)}_{\tiny\hbox{}\hskip 0.0pt\vbox{\vbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt}}\hskip 0.0pt}(x)=\left(x^{2}+x+1+x^{-1}+x^{-2}\right)+\left(x+1+x^{-1}\right)

(5.2)

which is manifestly the sum of
{\text{SO}}(3)
characters corresponding to spin 1 and spin 2 representations respectively.
Altogether, we obtain the following simple branching rule

\displaystyle\left.\hbox{}\hskip 0.0pt\vbox{\vbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=8.74669pt,width=0.3pt,depth=2.18663pt\hbox to10.93333pt{\hfil}\vrule height=8.74669pt,width=0.3pt,depth=2.18663pt\hbox to10.93333pt{\hfil}\vrule height=8.74669pt,width=0.3pt,depth=2.18663pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=8.74669pt,width=0.3pt,depth=2.18663pt\hbox to10.93333pt{\hfil}\vrule height=8.74669pt,width=0.3pt,depth=2.18663pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt}}\hskip 0.0pt\right|_{{\text{SO}}(3)}=\hbox{}\hskip 0.0pt\vbox{\vbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=8.74669pt,width=0.3pt,depth=2.18663pt\hbox to10.93333pt{\hfil}\vrule height=8.74669pt,width=0.3pt,depth=2.18663pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt}}\hskip 0.0pt\oplus\hbox{}\hskip 0.0pt\vbox{\vbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=8.74669pt,width=0.3pt,depth=2.18663pt\hbox to10.93333pt{\hfil}\vrule height=8.74669pt,width=0.3pt,depth=2.18663pt\hbox to10.93333pt{\hfil}\vrule height=8.74669pt,width=0.3pt,depth=2.18663pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt}}\hskip 0.0pt

(5.3)

More generally, let
k
be the dimension of the Cartan subalgebra of
H
. Then the branching rule
R|_{H}=\oplus_{i}R_{i}^{\oplus n_{i}}
is equivalent to the character relation

\displaystyle\chi^{G}_{R}(x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{k})=\sum_{i}n_{i}\,\chi^{H}_{R_{i}}(x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{k})

(5.4)

where
n_{i}
denotes the degeneracy of each UIR
R_{i}
of
H
.

In this section, we will generalize the character method above to the noncompact pair
(G,H)=({\text{SO}}(2,d+1),{\text{SO}}(1,d+1))
. When
d=1
, we take
R
to be any unitary primary representation of
{\text{SO}}(2,2)
, and when
d\geq 2
, we will focus on unitary
scalar
primary representations of
{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)
. Some partial results regarding the spinning primary representations will also be mentioned.

5.1
{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)
group characters and noncompact generators

Given a primary representation of
{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)
, the usual character considered in CFT is defined as the trace of
e^{-\beta H}
decorated by
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
Cartan generators, where
\beta>0
for the convergence of trace. Rigorously speaking, such a character (which will be referred to as a CFT character henceforth) is
not
a group character because
e^{-\beta H}
does not belong to the group
{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)
. For a scalar primary representation
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}
, the CFT character is given by
Basile:2016aen

\displaystyle\Theta^{\text{CFT}_{d+1}}_{\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}}(\beta,\bm{y})=\frac{e^{-\Delta\beta}}{P_{d+1}(e^{-\beta},\bm{y})}

(5.5)

where
\bm{y}=(y_{1},y_{2},\cdots,y_{r^{\prime}}),r^{\prime}=\left\lfloor\frac{d+1}{2}\right\rfloor
are fugacities of
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
rotations. For example, when
d=0
the CFT character is simply
\frac{e^{-\Delta\beta}}{1-e^{-\beta}}
, and when
d=1
it is
\frac{e^{-\Delta\beta}}{(1-ye^{-\beta})(1-y^{-1}e^{-\beta})}
.

Unfortunately, this well-known CFT character cannot be used to study the reduction from
{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)
to
{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)
in that its definition involves the
{\text{SO}}(2)
generator
H
which is not accessible to the
{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)
subgroup. Instead, we need the
{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)
character defined with respect to the
{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)
Cartan generators, namely
22
22

22

We assume
0<q<1
without loss of generality.

\displaystyle\Theta^{{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)}_{\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}}(q,\bm{x})\equiv\,{\rm tr}\,_{\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}}\left(q^{D}x_{1}^{J_{1}}\cdots x_{r}^{J_{r}}\right)

(5.6)

where
J_{1},J_{2},\cdots,J_{r}
are the Cartan generators of
{\text{SO}}(d)
, with
r=\left\lfloor\frac{d}{2}\right\rfloor
. Since
q^{D}x_{1}^{J_{1}}\cdots x_{r}^{J_{r}}
is a group element of
{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)
when
x_{j}\in\rm U(1)
, we will call
\Theta^{{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)}_{\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}}(q,\bm{x})
an
{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)
group character. It exists in the distributional sense because the trace in eq.(
5.6
) is, roughly speaking, an oscillating sum. For
{\text{SO}}(2,1)
, the trace is computed exactly in
Sun:2020ame
.
In particular, the
{\text{SO}}(2,1)
group character of
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}
is found to be
\frac{q^{\Delta}}{1-q}
, very similar to its CFT counterpart. This result will be the main building block for us to systematically compute the
{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)
group character of
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}
for any
d
. The strategy is to decompose a primary representation
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}
of
{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)
into primary representations of
{\text{SO}}(2,1)
while keeping track of the
{\text{SO}}(d)
spin of each
{\text{SO}}(2,1)
piece in the decomposition.
In other words, we need the reduction of
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}
from
{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)
to
{\text{SO}}(2,1)\times{\text{SO}}(d)
. The CFT character allows us to solve such a reduction very easily. When
d=2r
is even, we have
P_{d+1}(e^{-\beta},\bm{y})=(1-e^{-\beta})P_{d}(e^{-\beta},\bm{y})
, and using eq. (
C.1
) for
P_{d}(e^{-\beta},\bm{y})^{-1}
we obtain

\displaystyle\Theta^{\text{CFT}_{d+1}}_{\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}}(\beta,\bm{y})

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-\Delta\beta}}{1-e^{-\beta}}\,\sum_{s\geq 0}\chi^{{\text{SO}}(d)}_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{s}}(\bm{y})\frac{e^{-s\beta}}{1-e^{-2\beta}}=\sum_{n,s\geq 0}\Theta^{\text{CFT}_{1}}_{\mathcal{R}_{\Delta+2n+s}}(\beta)\chi^{{\text{SO}}(d)}_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{s}}(\bm{y})

(5.7)

where
(1-e^{-2\beta})^{-1}
is expanded into a Taylor series of
e^{-2\beta}
, and each summand for fixed
n
and
s
is expressed as the product of a
\text{CFT}_{1}
character and an
{\text{SO}}(d)
character. It implies the following decomposition

\displaystyle\left.\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}\right|^{{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)}_{{\text{SO}}(2,1)\times{\text{SO}}(d)}=\bigoplus_{n,s\geq 0}\left[\mathcal{R}_{\Delta+2n+s}\right]_{{\text{SO}}(2,1)}\otimes\left[{\mathbb{Y}}_{s}\right]_{{\text{SO}}(d)}

(5.8)

Applying the
{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)
character to this decomposition yields

\displaystyle\Theta^{{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)}_{\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}}(q,\bm{x})=\sum_{n,s\geq 0}\Theta^{{\text{SO}}(2,1)}_{\mathcal{R}_{\Delta+2n+s}}(q)\chi^{{\text{SO}}(d)}_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{s}}(\bm{x})

(5.9)

Since
{\text{SO}}(2,1)
group characters and
\text{CFT}_{1}
characters take the same form upon the replacement
q\to e^{-\beta}
, eq. (
5.7
) and eq. (
5.9
) guarantee that this identification also holds for any even
d
, namely

\displaystyle\text{Even}\,\,d:\,\,\,\,\Theta^{{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)}_{\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}}(q,\bm{x})=\frac{q^{\Delta}}{P_{d+1}(q,\bm{x})}

(5.10)

When
d=2r+1
is odd,
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
has one more Cartan generator than
{\text{SO}}(d)
. Therefore, in order to derive the reduction from
{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)
to
{\text{SO}}(2,1)\times{\text{SO}}(d)
, we should set
y_{r+1}=1
in eq. (
5.5
). Let
\hat{\bm{y}}
be the first
r
components of
\bm{y}
. Since
P_{d+1}(e^{-\beta},\bm{y})=\prod_{i=1}^{r+1}(1-y_{i}e^{-\beta})(1-y_{i}^{-1}e^{-\beta})
, taking
y_{r+1}
yields

\displaystyle P_{d+1}(e^{-\beta},\hat{\bm{y}},1)=\left(1-e^{-\beta}\right)^{2}\prod_{i=1}^{r}(1-y_{i}e^{-\beta})(1-y_{i}^{-1}e^{-\beta})=(1-e^{-\beta})P_{d}(e^{-\beta},\hat{\bm{y}})

(5.11)

Applying eq. (
C.1
) for
P_{d}(e^{-\beta},\hat{\bm{y}})^{-1}
we obtain

\displaystyle\Theta^{\text{CFT}_{d+1}}_{\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}}(\beta,\hat{\bm{y}},1)=\sum_{n,s\geq 0}\Theta^{\text{CFT}_{1}}_{\mathcal{R}_{\Delta+2n+s}}(\beta)\chi^{{\text{SO}}(d)}_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{s}}(\hat{\bm{y}})

(5.12)

which leads to the decomposition (
5.8
), and

\displaystyle\text{Odd}\,\,d:\,\,\,\,\,\Theta^{{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)}_{\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}}(q,\bm{x})=\frac{q^{\Delta}}{P_{d+1}(q,\bm{x},1)}

(5.13)

Altogether, eq. (
5.10
) and eq. (
5.13
) can be uniformly expressed as

\displaystyle\Theta^{{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)}_{\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}}(q,\bm{x})=\frac{q^{\Delta}}{(1-q)P_{d}(q,\bm{x})}

(5.14)

Similarly, for a spinning primary representation
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell}
, one can show that the corresponding
{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)
character is given by

\displaystyle\Theta^{{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)}_{\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell}}(q,\bm{x})=\frac{q^{\Delta}}{(1-q)P_{d}(q,\bm{x})}\times\begin{cases}\chi^{{\text{SO}}(d+1)}_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{\ell}}(\bm{x}),&d=2r\\
\chi^{{\text{SO}}(d+1)}_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{\ell}}(\bm{x},1),&d=2r+1\end{cases}

(5.15)

5.2
From
{\text{SO}}(2,2)
to
{\text{SO}}(1,2)

For
d=1
, the restriction of any primary representation of
{\text{SO}}(2,2)
to
{\text{SO}}(2,1)
is partially solved in
Hogervorst:2021uvp
. It is found that an
{\text{SO}}(2,2)
primary representation
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell}
of energy
\Delta
and spin
\ell
, satisfying the unitarity bound
\Delta\geq|\ell|
, contains
{\text{SO}}(1,2)
principal series
{\cal P}_{\frac{1}{2}+i\lambda}
for all
\lambda
. In addition, there is a complementary series representation
\mathcal{C}_{1-\Delta}
when
\Delta<\frac{1}{2}
and
\ell=0
, and
|\ell|
discrete series representations
{\cal D}^{\text{sign}(\ell)}_{1},{\cal D}^{\text{sign}(\ell)}_{2},\cdots,{\cal D}^{\text{sign}(\ell)}_{|\ell|}
when
\ell\not=0
. However, a properly defined density of the principal series is still missing. We will solve this problem by using Harish-Chandra characters.

5.2.1
Character analysis

Using a straightforward generalization of the derivation in subsection
5.1
, we find that the
{\text{SO}}(2,2)
group character of the primary representation
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell}
is given by

\displaystyle\Theta^{{\text{SO}}(2,2)}_{\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell}}(q)=\frac{q^{\Delta}}{(1-q)^{2}},\,\,\,\,\,0<q<1~.

(5.16)

This character is insensitive to the spin
\ell
because we have only turned on one Cartan generator while
{\text{SO}}(2,2)
has two commuting generators, one counting the energy and the other counting the spin. In the spinning case, i.e.
\Delta>|\ell|>0
, we expect the following formal expansion

\displaystyle\Theta^{{\text{SO}}(2,2)}_{\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell}}(q)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\,d\lambda\,{\cal K}(\lambda;\ell)\frac{q^{\Delta_{\lambda}}+q^{\bar{\Delta}_{\lambda}}}{1-q}+\sum_{k=1}^{|\ell|}\frac{q^{k}}{1-q}

(5.17)

where
{\cal K}(\lambda;\ell)
is a formal “density” of
{\text{SO}}(1,2)
principal series. As we have seen many times in previous sections,
{\cal K}(\lambda;\ell)
defined this way is actually divergent because
\Theta^{{\text{SO}}(2,2)}_{\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell}}(q)
has a double pole at
q=1
while
{\text{SO}}(1,2)
characters only have a single pole at
q=1
. To regularize the divergence in
{\cal K}(\lambda;\ell)
, we introduce another spinless primary representation
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta^{\prime}}
with
\Delta^{\prime}>\frac{1}{2}
such that it does not contain any complementary series representation of
{\text{SO}}(1,2)
and define a relative density
{\cal K}_{\rm rel}(\lambda;\ell)
as

\displaystyle\Theta^{{\text{SO}}(2,2)}_{\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell}}(q)-\Theta^{{\text{SO}}(2,2)}_{\mathcal{R}_{\Delta^{\prime}}}(q)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\,d\lambda\,{\cal K}_{\rm rel}(\lambda;\ell)\Theta_{\Delta_{\lambda}}(q)+\sum_{k=1}^{|\ell|}\frac{q^{k}}{1-q}\,.

(5.18)

From eq. (
5.18
), we can easily solve the relative density using (
A.3
)

\displaystyle{\cal K}_{\rm rel}(\lambda;\ell)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\,dt\,\left(\frac{e^{-(\Delta-\frac{1}{2})t}-e^{-(\Delta^{\prime}-\frac{1}{2})t}}{1-e^{-t}}-\sum_{k=1}^{|\ell|}e^{-(k-\frac{1}{2})t}\right)\cos(\lambda t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{\pm}\left[\psi\left(\Delta^{\prime}-\frac{1}{2}\pm i\lambda\right)-\psi\left(\Delta-\frac{1}{2}\pm i\lambda\right)\right]-\frac{1}{\pi}\sum_{k=1}^{|\ell|}\frac{k-\frac{1}{2}}{(k-\frac{1}{2})^{2}+\lambda^{2}}

(5.19)

where the first term is independent of
\ell
and the second term is independent of
\Delta
. Equation (
5.18
) also holds for
\ell=0
as long as
\Delta\geq\frac{1}{2}
, since
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}
does not contain complementary series of
{\text{SO}}(1,2)
in this regime, and the corresponding
{\cal K}_{\rm rel}(\lambda;0)
gives the relative density of principal series between
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta^{\prime}}
. Taking
\Delta<\frac{1}{2}
for
\ell=0
, the eq. (
5.18
) should be modified as

\displaystyle 0<\Delta<\frac{1}{2}:\,\,\,\,\,\Theta^{{\text{SO}}(2,2)}_{\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}}(q)-\Theta^{{\text{SO}}(2,2)}_{\mathcal{R}_{\Delta^{\prime}}}(q)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\,d\lambda\,{\cal K}_{\rm rel}(\lambda;0)\Theta_{\Delta_{\lambda}}(q)+\frac{q^{\Delta}+q^{1-\Delta}}{1-q}\,.

(5.20)

where we have used eq. (
A.9
) with
n=0
for the integral
\int_{0}^{\infty}\,d\lambda\,{\cal K}_{\rm rel}(\lambda;0)\Theta_{\Delta_{\lambda}}(q)
. The extra term
\frac{q^{\Delta}+q^{1-\Delta}}{1-q}
is consistent with the existence of an
{\text{SO}}(1,2)
complementary series representation
\mathcal{C}_{1-\Delta}
in the primary representation
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}
when
0<\Delta<\frac{1}{2}
.

Figure 5.1
:
Low-lying eigenvalues of
{\cal Q}
. Left: The three smallest eigenvalues for various positive
\Delta
. The dashed line is
{\cal Q}=\frac{1}{4}
and the gray line is
{\cal Q}=\Delta(1-\Delta)
. Right: The convergence of the smallest eigenvalue of
{\cal Q}
to its large
{\cal N}
asymptote
{\cal Q}_{\text{min}}(\infty)=\Delta(1-\Delta)
. Inset plot is the log-log version and dashed blue line is a power law fit.

[
new
]When
\Delta=|\ell|
, which corresponds to a chiral field in CFT
2
, the primary representation
\mathcal{R}_{|\ell|,\ell}
contains an infinite number of null states. More explicitly, we have a basis
\{|n,\bar{n}),n,\bar{n}\geq 0\}
for any
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell}
defined by eq. (
F.12
). According to eq. (
F.13
), states with
\bar{n}\not=0
are null when
\Delta=\ell
, and states with nonzero
n
are null when
\Delta=-\ell
. In addition, it is straightforward to check that the physical states form the
{\text{SO}}(1,2)
discrete series representation
{\cal D}^{\pm}_{\ell}
when
\Delta=\pm\ell
. Altogether, the
{\text{SO}}(2,2)
UIR corresponding to a chiral field only gives a discrete series representation when restricted to
{\text{SO}}(1,2)
.

5.2.2
Numerical check

The
{\text{SO}}(1,2)
Casimir restricted to the subspace spanned by scalar descendants, i.e.
{\text{SO}}(2,2)
descendants that are also
{\text{SO}}(2)
singlets, contains all the information about principal and complementary series representations in
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell}
. Denote the normalized scalar descendant states by
|n\rangle,n\geq|{\rm min}(0,\ell)|
, where each
|n\rangle
is at level
2n+\ell
. The matrix elements of
C^{{\text{SO}}(1,2)}
with respect to the
|n\rangle
basis have been derived in the appendix D of
Hogervorst:2021uvp
. Define
{\cal Q}_{n,m}=\langle n|C^{{\text{SO}}(1,2)}|m\rangle
, and the nonvanishing entries of
{\cal Q}
are

\displaystyle{\cal Q}_{n,n}=2n(n+\ell)+\Delta(\ell+2n+1)

\displaystyle{\cal Q}_{n+1,n}={\cal Q}_{n,n+1}=-\sqrt{(n+1)(n+\Delta)(n+\ell+1)(n+\Delta+\ell)}~.

(5.21)

Similar to previous sections, we can show that this implies
C^{{\text{SO}}(1,2)}
has continuous spectrum on principal series. As before, we may introduce a truncation cutoff
{\cal N}
on
n
and diagonalize the
{\cal Q}
matrix to find a finite set of eigenstates
q_{i}
. Any eigenvalue smaller than
\frac{1}{4}
for large
{\cal N}
corresponds to a complementary series representation with
{\text{SO}}(1,2)
Casimir given by this eigenvalue. The first three eigenvalues of
{\cal Q}
for scalar primary representations
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}
are plotted as a function of
\Delta
in the left panel of fig. (
5.1
), where the cutoff is chosen to be
{\cal N}=10000
. For
\Delta
smaller than roughly
\frac{1}{2}
, the smallest eigenvalue of
{\cal Q}
is below the critical line
{\cal Q}=\frac{9}{4}
and lies on the curve
\Delta(1-\Delta)
, suggesting the appearance of a single complementary series representation
\mathcal{C}_{1-\Delta}
in
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}
in the large
{\cal N}
limit. The convergence to the expected value
\Delta(1-\Delta)
for the
\Delta=0.35
case is illustrated in the right panel of fig. (
5.1
).

We parametrize the eigenstates with
q_{i}>\frac{1}{4}
corresponding to the principal series of dimensions
\Delta_{i}=\frac{1}{2}+i\lambda_{i}
with
\lambda_{i}=\sqrt{q_{i}-\frac{1}{4}}
. A coarse-grained density of states is given by the inverse of the spacing of
\{\lambda_{i}\}
:

\bar{\rho}_{\cal N}(\lambda_{i})=\frac{2}{\lambda_{i+1}-\lambda_{i-1}}~.

(5.22)

Like before, we consider a relative coarse-grained density, which has a finite
{\cal N}\to\infty
limit

\rho_{\text{rel}}=\bar{\rho}_{\cal N}^{\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell}}-\bar{\rho}^{\mathcal{R}_{\Delta^{\prime}}}_{\cal N}~.

(5.23)

Its remarkable match with the character based density
{\cal K}_{\text{rel}}(\lambda;\ell)

is shown in fig.(
5.2
).

Similar to the previous sections, we observe a match between the coarse-grained density
\bar{\rho}_{\cal N}
with Pauli-Villars type renormalized
{\cal K}(\lambda,\ell)
. For more details see appendix
B
and fig. (
B.5
).

Figure 5.2
:
Comparison of the relative coarse-grained density
\rho_{\text{rel}}(\lambda)
(pink solid line) defined in eq. (
5.23
) to
{\cal K}_{\text{rel}}(\lambda)
(black dashed line) derived in eq. (
5.2.1
), with
\ell=4,\Delta=4.5,\Delta^{\prime}=1.3
and
{\cal N}=2000
. In the inset plot, the convergence to
{\cal K}_{\text{rel}}(\lambda)
(black dashed line) in the large
{\cal N}
limit is shown where we zoomed in to the range
12.9<\lambda<13
.

5.3
From SO
(2,3)
to SO
(1,3)

Because SO
(1,3)
only has principal series and complementary series, the reduction of any unitary primary representation
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell}
of
{\text{SO}}(2,3)
to
{\text{SO}}(1,3)
is actually simpler than its lower dimensional and higher dimensional counterparts. In this subsection, we perform a character analysis of such a reduction. First, the
{\text{SO}}(2,3)
character of
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell}
is given by eq. (
5.15
):

\displaystyle\Theta^{{\text{SO}}(2,3)}_{\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell}}(q,x)=\frac{\chi^{{\text{SO}}(3)}_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{\ell}}(x)}{(1-x\,q)(1-x^{-1}q)}\frac{q^{\Delta}}{1-q}

(5.24)

where
\chi^{{\text{SO}}(3)}_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{\ell}}(x)=\sum_{m=-\ell}^{\ell}x^{m}
. Due to the singularity at
q=1
, the
{\text{SO}}(2,3)
character
\Theta^{{\text{SO}}(2,3)}_{\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell}}(q,x)
itself does not admit a well-defined decomposition into
{\text{SO}}(1,3)
characters. So we introduce another primary representation
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta^{\prime},\ell}
with
\Delta^{\prime}>1
. Then the difference
\Theta^{{\text{SO}}(2,3)}_{\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell}}(q,x)-\Theta^{{\text{SO}}(2,3)}_{\mathcal{R}_{\Delta^{\prime},\ell}}(q,x)
can be expanded in terms of
{\text{SO}}(1,3)
characters. For
\Delta>1
, using the Fourier transformation

\displaystyle\frac{q^{\Delta-1}-q^{\Delta^{\prime}-1}}{1-q}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\,d\lambda\,e^{i\lambda t}{\cal K}_{\rm rel}(\lambda),\,\,\,\,\,q=e^{-|t|}

\displaystyle{\cal K}_{\rm rel}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{\pm}\left[\psi(\Delta^{\prime}-1\pm i\lambda)-\psi(\Delta-1\pm i\lambda)\right]

(5.25)

we obtain

\displaystyle\Theta^{{\text{SO}}(2,3)}_{\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell}}(q,x)-\Theta^{{\text{SO}}(2,3)}_{\mathcal{R}_{\Delta^{\prime},\ell}}(q,x)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}d\lambda\,{\cal K}_{\rm rel}(\lambda)\Theta^{{\text{SO}}(1,3)}_{1+i\lambda}(q,x)

\displaystyle+\sum_{m=1}^{\ell}\int_{0}^{\infty}d\lambda\,{\cal K}_{\rm rel}(\lambda)\left(\Theta^{{\text{SO}}(1,3)}_{1+i\lambda,m}(q,x)+\Theta^{{\text{SO}}(1,3)}_{1+i\lambda,-m}(q,x)\right)

(5.26)

where
\Theta^{{\text{SO}}(1,3)}_{1+i\lambda,m}(q,x)+\Theta^{{\text{SO}}(1,3)}_{1+i\lambda,-m}(q,x)=\frac{(x^{m}+x^{-m})(q^{1+i\lambda}+q^{1-i\lambda})}{(1-xq)(1-x^{-1}q)}
is the SO
(1,3)
character of the reducible representation
{\cal P}_{1+i\lambda,m}\oplus{\cal P}_{1+i\lambda,-m}
, which is isomorphic to
{\cal P}_{1-i\lambda,m}\oplus{\cal P}_{1-i\lambda,-m}
because of
{\cal P}_{1+i\lambda,m}\cong{\cal P}_{1-i\lambda,-m}
. This reducible representation describes a massive field of spin
|m|
in dS
3
. When
\ell=0
, eq. (
5.3
) implies that there are only scalar principal series representations in
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}
with
\Delta>1
. Decreasing
\Delta
from
1^{+}
to the unitarity bound
\frac{1}{2}
, pole crossing happens in the integral
\int_{0}^{\infty}d\lambda\,{\cal K}_{\rm rel}(\lambda)\Theta^{{\text{SO}}(1,3)}_{1+i\lambda}(q,x)
, which signals the appearance of a complementary series representation. Indeed, for
\frac{1}{2}<\Delta<1
and
\Delta^{\prime}>1
, we find

\displaystyle\Theta^{{\text{SO}}(2,3)}_{\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}}(q,x)-\Theta^{{\text{SO}}(2,3)}_{\mathcal{R}_{\Delta^{\prime}}}(q,x)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}d\lambda\,{\cal K}_{\rm rel}(\lambda)\Theta^{{\text{SO}}(1,3)}_{1+i\lambda}(q,x)+\Theta^{{\text{SO}}(1,3)}_{2-\Delta}(q,x)

(5.27)

Therefore, in addition to the principal series
{\cal P}_{1+i\lambda}
, there is also a complementary series representation
\mathcal{C}_{2-\Delta}
in
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}
if
\Delta
is smaller than 1. For
\ell\geq 1
, unitarity requires
\Delta\geq 1+\ell\geq 2
, so there cannot be any
{\text{SO}}(1,3)
complementary series representation in
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell}
. Instead, eq. (
5.3
) implies that besides
{\cal P}_{1+i\lambda}
, there are also spinning principal series
{\cal P}_{1+i\lambda,\pm m}
with
m=1,2,\cdots,\ell
in
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell}
. Principal series of different spins share the same (relative) density
{\cal K}_{\rm rel}(\lambda)
.

5.4
From
{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)
to
{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)
: scalar primary representations

Let
|\Delta\rangle
be the primary state of
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}
. A generic descendant of
|\Delta\rangle
should be a linear combination of
L_{a_{1}}^{+}\cdots L_{a_{k}}^{+}|\Delta\rangle
, where
L^{+}_{a}
are raising operators given by eq. (
F.2
). Treating
L_{a_{1}}^{+}\cdots L_{a_{k}}^{+}
as a symmetrized tensor of
k
spin
1
representations of
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
, which can be decomposed as a direct sum of
{\mathbb{Y}}_{k},{\mathbb{Y}}_{k-2},{\mathbb{Y}}_{k-4},\cdots
, then the possible irreducible
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
structures in
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}
are all its single-row representations. According to the review in section
2
, the absence of two-row representations of
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
implies that the only possible
{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)
structure in
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}
are the spinless principal series, complementary series, and the type I exceptional series
{\cal V}_{s,0}
.

We first prove that
{\cal V}_{s,0}
is not present in
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}
. A
{\cal V}_{s,0}
representation in
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}
is characterized by a spin-
s
state
|\phi)_{a_{1}\cdots a_{s}}
satisfying
L_{a_{1}0}|\phi)_{a_{1}\cdots a_{s}}=0
because
{\cal V}_{s,0}
does not contain any
{\mathbb{Y}}_{k}
components with
k<s
while
L_{a_{1}0}|\phi)_{a_{1}\cdots a_{s}}
has the symmetry of
{\mathbb{Y}}_{s-1}
23
23

23

More rigorously,
{\cal V}_{s,0}
is characterized by a spin-
s
state
|\phi)_{a_{1}\cdots a_{s}}
such that the
{\mathbb{Y}}_{s-1}
and
{\mathbb{Y}}_{s,1}
components of
L_{a}|\phi)_{a_{1}\cdots a_{s}}
are vanishing. It is straightforward to check that a state satisfying these conditions is an eigenstate of
C^{{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)}
with the desired eigenvalue. When
|\phi)_{a_{1}\cdots a_{s}}\in\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}
, the
{\mathbb{Y}}_{s,1}
part vanishes automatically since
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}
does not contain any two-row representation of
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
.
. In the primary representation
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}
, any spin-
s
state is a linear combination of
(L^{+}\cdot L^{+})^{n}(L^{+}_{a_{1}}\cdots L^{+}_{a_{s}}-\text{trace})|\Delta\rangle,n\geq 0
, which in the index-free formalism can be written compactly as

\displaystyle|\phi_{n}^{s})\equiv\left(L^{+}\cdot L^{+}\right)^{n}\left(z\cdot L^{+}\right)^{s}|\Delta\rangle

(5.28)

where
z^{a}
is an auxiliary null vector in
\mathbb{C}^{d+1}
. Define
|\phi,z)=\sum_{n\geq 0}a_{n}|\phi^{s}_{n})
and then the analogue of

L_{a_{1}0}|\phi)_{a_{1}\cdots a_{s}}=0
in the index-free formalism is
