\displaystyle L_{a0}{\cal D}_{z^{a}}|\phi,z)=0,\,\,\,\,\,{\cal D}_{z_{a}}=\partial_{z^{a}}-\frac{1}{d+2z\cdot\partial_{z}-1}z_{a}\partial_{z}^{2}~.

(5.29)

Using eq. (
G.7
) and (
G.10
), we find

\displaystyle L^{+}_{a}{\cal D}_{z^{a}}|\phi_{n}^{s})=\frac{s(d+s-2)}{d+2s-3}|\phi^{s-1}_{n+1})

\displaystyle L^{-}_{a}{\cal D}_{z^{a}}|\phi_{n}^{s})=\frac{4s(d+s-2)(n+s+\Delta-1)(n+s+\frac{d-1}{2})}{d+2s-3}|\phi^{s-1}_{n})~.

(5.30)

Since
L_{a0}=\frac{i}{2}\left(L^{+}_{a}+L^{-}_{a}\right)
, the requirement
L_{a0}{\cal D}_{z^{a}}|\phi,z)=0
leads to a recurrence relation of the coefficients
a_{n}

\displaystyle 4(n+s+\Delta)\left(n+s+\frac{d+1}{2}\right)a_{n+1}+a_{n}=0

(5.31)

where
n\geq-1
and
a_{-1}\equiv 0
. The equation corresponding to
n=-1
holds if and only if
a_{0}=0
, which further requires all
a_{n}
to vanish because of the recurrence relation eq. (
5.31
). Altogether,
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}
does not contain any
{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)
representation belonging to the type I exceptional series, and hence the only possible
{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)
species is spinless principal or complementary series representation.

5.4.1
Character analysis

Assuming the absence of complementary series in
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}
when
\Delta
is larger than some critical value
\Delta_{c}
, which will be confirmed by numerics shortly, we expect the following character relation to hold

\displaystyle\Theta^{{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)}_{\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}}(q,\bm{x})-\Theta^{{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)}_{\mathcal{R}_{\Delta^{\prime}}}(q,\bm{x})=\int_{0}^{\infty}d\lambda\,{\cal K}_{\rm rel}(\lambda)\Theta^{{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)}_{\Delta_{\lambda}}(q,\bm{x}),\,\,\,\,\,\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}>\Delta_{c}

(5.32)

where
\Theta^{{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)}_{\Delta_{\lambda}}(q,\bm{x})
is the
{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)
character of principal series representation
{\cal P}_{\frac{d}{2}+i\lambda}
, and
{\cal K}_{\rm rel}(\lambda)
is supposed to be the relative density of
{\cal P}_{\frac{d}{2}+i\lambda}
between the two primary representations
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta^{\prime}}
. Using eq. (
5.14
) and making the change of variable
q\to e^{-|t|}
, then the character relation (
5.32
) is equivalent to the Fourier transform

\displaystyle\frac{e^{-(\Delta-\frac{d}{2})|t|}-e^{-(\Delta^{\prime}-\frac{d}{2})|t|}}{1-e^{-|t|}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}d\lambda\,{\cal K}_{\rm rel}(\lambda)e^{i\lambda t}

(5.33)

where we have extended
{\cal K}_{\rm rel}(\lambda)
to an even function on the whole real line. For
\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}>\frac{d}{2}
, the solution of eq. (
5.33
) is

\displaystyle{\cal K}_{\rm rel}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{\pm}\left[\psi\left(\Delta^{\prime}-\frac{d}{2}\pm i\lambda\right)-\psi\left(\Delta-\frac{d}{2}\pm i\lambda\right)\right]~.

(5.34)

Since the unitarity bound on
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}
only requires
\Delta>\frac{d-1}{2}
,
\Delta-\frac{d}{2}
can be negative.
In this case, eq. (
5.33
) needs a modification, which follows from the integral eq. (
A.9
) with
n=0

\displaystyle\frac{d-1}{2}<\Delta<\frac{d}{2}:\,\,\,\,\frac{e^{-(\Delta-\frac{d}{2})|t|}-e^{-(\Delta^{\prime}-\frac{d}{2})|t|}}{1-e^{-|t|}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}d\lambda\,{\cal K}_{\rm rel}(\lambda)e^{i\lambda t}+\frac{e^{-(\Delta-\frac{d}{2})t}+e^{(\Delta-\frac{d}{2})t}}{1-e^{-|t|}}

(5.35)

and then the character relation (
5.32
) should change accordingly as (suppressing the arguments of characters)

\displaystyle\Theta^{{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)}_{\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}}-\Theta^{{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)}_{\mathcal{R}_{\Delta^{\prime}}}=\int_{0}^{\infty}d\lambda\,{\cal K}_{\rm rel}(\lambda)\Theta^{{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)}_{\Delta_{\lambda}}+\Theta_{d-\Delta}^{{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)}

(5.36)

which suggests
\Delta_{c}=\frac{d}{2}
and the existence of a single complementary series representation
\mathcal{C}_{d-\Delta}
in
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}
when
\frac{d-1}{2}<\Delta<\frac{d}{2}
. The next step is to develop a numerical scheme to test these claims.

5.4.2
Numerical check

Figure 5.3
:
Low-lying eigenvalues of the
{\text{SO}}(1,4)
Casimir matrix for the primary representation
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}
of a CFT in dS
4
. Left: The first three eigenvalues of
{\cal Q}
, given by eq. (
5.4.2
), with the cut-off being
{\cal N}=10^{4}
. The gray line is
{\cal Q}=\Delta(3-\Delta)
and the dashed line is
{\cal Q}=\frac{9}{4}
. Right: The convergence of
{\cal Q}_{\rm min}({\cal N})
to its expected value
1.2\times(3-1.2)=2.16
in the
{\cal N}\to\infty
limit.

To identify those continuous families of states, we need to study the spectrum of the
{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)
Casimir in the
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
singlet subspace of
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}
, which is spanned by
|\phi_{n})=\left(L^{+}\cdot L^{+}\right)^{n}|\Delta\rangle
. The
{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)
Casimir acting on
|\phi_{n})
is given by eq. (
F.5
)

\displaystyle C^{{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)}|\phi_{n})=\frac{1}{4}|\phi_{n+1})+\frac{1}{4}L^{-}\cdot L^{-}|\phi_{n})+\left(\frac{1}{2}L^{+}\cdot L^{-}+\frac{d+1}{2}\Delta+(d+1)n\right)|\phi_{n})

(5.37)

where both
L^{-}\cdot L^{-}|\phi_{n})
and
L^{+}\cdot L^{-}|\phi_{n})
are computed in appendix
G
, c.f. eq. (
G
) and
eq. (
G.3
) respectively.
Combining all the ingredients yields

\displaystyle C^{{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)}|\phi_{n})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(4n(n+\Delta)+(d+1)\Delta\right)|\phi_{n})+\frac{1}{4}|\phi_{n+1})

\displaystyle+4n\,(n+\Delta-1)\left(n+\frac{d-1}{2}\right)\left(n+\Delta-\frac{d+1}{2}\right)|\phi_{n-1})

(5.38)

Define normalized states
|\phi_{n}\rangle\equiv\frac{1}{\sqrt{(\phi_{n}|\phi_{n})}}|\phi_{n})
, where
(\phi_{n}|\phi_{n})
is computed in eq. (
G.6
), then the nonvanishing matrix elements of
C^{{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)}
with respect to the normalized basis
|\phi_{n}\rangle
are

\displaystyle{\cal Q}_{nn}=2n(n+\Delta)+\frac{1}{2}(d+1)\Delta

\displaystyle{\cal Q}_{n+1,n}={\cal Q}_{n,n+1}=\sqrt{(n+1)(n+\Delta)\left(n+\frac{d+1}{2}\right)\left(n+\Delta-\frac{d-1}{2}\right)}

(5.39)

where
{\cal Q}_{mn}\equiv\langle\phi_{m}|C^{{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)}|\phi_{n}\rangle
.
For each
q_{\lambda}=\frac{d^{2}}{4}+\lambda^{2},\lambda\geq 0
which corresponds to a principal series representation
{\cal P}_{\frac{d}{2}+i\lambda}
, the matrix
{\cal Q}
admits an eigenvector
v_{n}(\lambda)
satisfying
{\cal Q}_{nm}v_{m}(\lambda)=q_{\lambda}v_{n}(\lambda)
, and its asymptotic behavior at large
n
is given by
v_{n}(\lambda)\sim\frac{R}{n^{\frac{1}{2}+i\lambda}}+c.c
, which implies that all
v_{n}(\lambda)
are
\delta
-function normalizable. Therefore,
C^{{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)}
has a continuous spectrum in the region
\left[\frac{d^{2}}{4},\infty\right)
. It is also possible for the infinite dimensional matrix
{\cal Q}
to have eigenvalues smaller than
\frac{d^{2}}{4}
. To see these eigenvalues intuitively, we consider a truncated version of
{\cal Q}_{nm}
by imposing a hard cut-off
{\cal N}
on
n
and
m
, which effectively cuts off the energy at
\Delta+2{\cal N}
in the primary representation
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}
. Then we can numerically diagonalize the truncation of
{\cal Q}
. In fig. (
5.3
), with
{\cal N}
chosen to be
10^{4}
, we plot the first three eigenvalues of
{\cal Q}
for primary representations
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}
of
{\text{SO}}(2,4)
. It shows that when
1<\Delta\lesssim\frac{3}{2}
, the smallest eigenvalue of
{\cal Q}
becomes smaller than
\frac{9}{4}
, lying on the line
{\cal Q}=\Delta(3-\Delta)
which is equal to the
{\text{SO}}(1,4)
Casimir of the complementary series representations
\mathcal{C}_{3-\Delta}
, and when
\Delta>\frac{3}{2}
all the eigenvalues are larger than
\frac{9}{4}
. This numerical result confirms the prediction of a complementary series representation
\mathcal{C}_{d-\Delta}
in
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}
when
\frac{d-1}{2}<\Delta<\frac{d}{2}
. The numerical diagonalization also allows us to extract information about principal series contained in
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}
. For each fixed
{\cal N}
, we can define a coarse-grained density of principal series as the inverse spacing of
\lambda_{n}=\sqrt{q_{n}-\frac{d^{2}}{4}}
, where
q_{n}
denote eigenvalues of
{\cal Q}
that are larger than
\frac{d^{2}}{4}
. Given two primary representations
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta^{\prime}}
, we obtain similarly a relative coarse-grained density
\rho_{\rm rel}
that is finite in the large
{\cal N}
limit. Fig. (
5.4
) confirms that
\rho_{\rm rel}
matches
{\cal K}_{\rm rel}
(c.f. eq. (
5.34
)), derived from charaters.

Figure 5.4
:
Comparison of the relative coarse-grained density
\rho_{\text{rel}}(\lambda)
(pink solid line) to
{\cal K}_{\text{rel}}(\lambda)
(black dashed line) given by eq. (
5.34
) for
\Delta=4.5,\Delta^{\prime}=1.7
and
{\cal N}=2000
. The inset plot shows the convergence to
{\cal K}_{\text{rel}}(\lambda)
in the large
{\cal N}
limit by zooming in the region
12.9<\lambda<13
.

5.5
From
{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)
to
{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)
: Some remarks on spinning primary representations

Let
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell}
be a primary representation of
{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)
, built from a spin-
\ell
primary state
|\Delta\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{\ell}}
. As the first step to constrain the possible
{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)
species in
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell}
, we list all
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
irreducible representations in it. Descendants of
|\Delta\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{\ell}}
are linear combinations of
L^{+}_{b_{1}}\cdots L^{+}_{b_{k}}|\Delta\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{\ell}}
. Treating
L^{+}_{b_{1}}\cdots L^{+}_{b_{k}}
as a symmetrized tensor product of
k
spin 1 representations of
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
, which can be decomposed as a direct sum
\oplus_{m}{\mathbb{Y}}_{k-2m}
, then the possible
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
structures of descendants are encoded in the tensor products
{\mathbb{Y}}_{n}\otimes{\mathbb{Y}}_{\ell},n\geq 0
, where
{\mathbb{Y}}_{\ell}
corresponds to the primary states and
{\mathbb{Y}}_{n}
corresponds to
(L^{+}_{b_{1}}\cdots L^{+}_{b_{n}}-\text{trace})
. The tensor product decomposition of
{\mathbb{Y}}_{n}\otimes{\mathbb{Y}}_{\ell}
is given by
eq. (
4.1
):

\displaystyle{\mathbb{Y}}_{n}\otimes{\mathbb{Y}}_{\ell}=\bigoplus_{a=0}^{\text{min}(n,\ell)}\bigoplus_{b=0}^{\text{min}(n,\ell)-a}{\mathbb{Y}}_{n+\ell-2a-b,b}~.

(5.40)

Some direct results of this decomposition are:

•

Since
b\leq\text{min}(n,\ell)\leq\ell
,
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell}
does not contain any
{\mathbb{Y}}_{ss}
with
s\geq\ell+1
. It further implies that spin
s
UIRs of
{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)
cannot appear in
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell}
if
s\geq\ell+1
. Therefore, the possible
{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)
UIRs in
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell}
are

\displaystyle{\cal P}_{\frac{d}{2}+i\lambda,s},\,\,\,\,\,\mathcal{C}_{\frac{d}{2}+\mu,s},\,\,\,\,\,{\cal U}_{s,t}~,

(5.41)

for
s=0,1,\cdots,\ell
, and the type I exceptional series.

•

The existence of some
{\mathbb{Y}}_{ss}
requires
n+\ell-2a-b=b
, which is equivalent to
a+b=\frac{n+\ell}{2}
. On the other hand, we have
a+b\leq\text{min}(n,\ell)\leq\frac{n+\ell}{2}
. Therefore,
n=\ell
and
a+b=\ell
. In other words,
{\mathbb{Y}}_{ss}
with
0\leq s\leq\ell
only appears in the tensor product
{\mathbb{Y}}_{\ell}\otimes{\mathbb{Y}}_{\ell}
and appears exactly once.
Altogether, the
{\mathbb{Y}}_{ss}
-type descendants of
|\Delta\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{\ell}}
are of the form

\displaystyle|s,n)_{a_{1}\cdots a_{s},b_{1}\cdots b_{s}}\equiv(L^{+}\cdot L^{+})^{n}\,\hat{\Pi}_{ss}\left(L^{+}_{a_{1}}\cdots L^{+}_{a_{s}}L^{+}_{b_{s+1}}\cdots L^{+}_{b_{\ell}}|\Delta\rangle_{b_{1}\cdots b_{\ell}}\right)

(5.42)

where
n\geq 0,0\leq s\leq\ell
and
\hat{\Pi}_{ss}
is a projector operator onto the
{\mathbb{Y}}_{ss}
part. For example, when
s=1
,
\hat{\Pi}_{11}
simply antisymmetrizes
a_{1}
and
b_{1}
. For higher
s
, it antisymmtrizes the
s
pairs of indices
[a_{1},b_{1}],\cdots,[a_{s},b_{s}]
, and then projects out all types of trace.

The goal for the remaining part of this section is to gain more intuitions of the possible
{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)
UIRs contained in
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell}
, at least numerically. For this purpose, we first study the spectrum of
C^{{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)}
restricted to the subspace spanned by all
|s,n)_{a_{1}\cdots a_{s},b_{1}\cdots b_{s}}
for any fixed
s\in\{0,1,\cdots,\ell\}
, since it encodes information of spin
s
principal and complementary series and
{\cal U}_{s,t}
in
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell}
. In appendix
H
, we have derived the matrix representation of
C^{{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)}
in this subspace for
s=0
and 1. Denote the matrix by
\mathcal{Q}^{(s)}
and the nonvanishing matrix elements are given by eq. (
H.3
):

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{(s)}_{nn}=2n(n+\Delta+\ell)+\frac{(d+2\ell+1)\Delta-(d-1)\ell}{2}+C^{{\text{SO}}(d)}({\mathbb{Y}}_{s})

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{(s)}_{n+1,n}=\mathcal{Q}^{(s)}_{n,n+1}=\sqrt{(n+1)(n+\Delta+\ell)\left(n+\Delta-\frac{d-1}{2}\right)\left(n+\ell+\frac{d+1}{2}\right)}

(5.43)

where the diagonal entries of
\mathcal{Q}^{(s)}
hold for
any

s
and the off-diagonal ones are
only
computed for
s=0
and
1
.
Surprisingly, we get the same off-diagonal entries for
s=0
and 1. Assuming that
{\cal Q}^{(s)}_{n,n+1}={\cal Q}^{(s)}_{n+1,n}
are given by eq. (
5.5
) for any
s
, then
\mathcal{Q}^{(s)}=\mathcal{Q}^{(0)}+C^{{\text{SO}}(d)}({\mathbb{Y}}_{s})
. This relation has many interesting implications. First, using the asymptotic behavior of
{\cal Q}^{(0)}
for
large
n
, one can show that it has a continuous spectrum on
[\frac{d^{2}}{4},\infty)
and hence
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell}
contains all the scalar principal series. Second, noticing that
C^{{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)}({\cal F}_{\frac{d}{2}+i\lambda,s})=\frac{d^{2}}{4}+\lambda^{2}+C^{{\text{SO}}(d)}({\mathbb{Y}}_{s})
, we can also immediately conclude the existence of all spin
s
principal series with
s\in\{0,1,\cdots,\ell\}
. Third, the existence of
{\cal U}_{s,t}
is equivalent to the existence of the eigenvalue
(1-t)(d+t-1)+C^{{\text{SO}}(d)}({\mathbb{Y}}_{s})
of
{\cal Q}^{(s)}
, which is also equivalent to the existence of the eigenvalue
(1-t)(d+t-1)
of
{\cal Q}^{(0)}
. This eigenvalue corresponds to
{\cal F}_{d+t-1}
of
{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)
which is nonunitary unless
t=0

24
24

24

Rigorously speaking,
{\cal V}_{t,0}
has the same Casimir
(1-t)(d+t-1)
. However,
{\cal Q}^{(0)}
is defined in the
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
singlet subspace and
{\cal V}_{t,0}
does not contain any
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
singlet. So
{\cal V}_{t,0}
can be easily excluded.
. Since we start with a unitary CFT in dS
d+1
, we do not expect any nonunitary representations of
{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)
. To test the
t=0
one and other complementary series numerically, we can truncate
{\cal Q}^{(0)}
by a hard cut-off
n\leq{\cal N}
and perform a diagonalization. In fig. (
5.5
), we show low-lying eigenvalues of the truncated
{\cal Q}^{(0)}
for various primary representation
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell}
in various dimensions. The absence of any eigenvalue below
\frac{d^{2}}{4}
seems to exclude complementary series and type II exceptional series in
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell}
with
\ell\geq 1
.

Figure 5.5
:
Plot of first three low-lying eigenvalues of
{\cal Q}^{(0)}
for various spacetime dimensions and spins. The dashed lines are
{\cal Q}^{(0)}=\frac{d^{2}}{4}
, with
d=3,4,6
.

The matrix
{\cal Q}^{(s)}
cannot tell us anything about the type I exceptional series representations since the latter do not contain any
{\mathbb{Y}}_{ss}
representation of
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
. Instead, as mentioned in the footnote
23
, we should study states of symmetry
{\mathbb{Y}}_{s}
. Although we have not solved this problem completely, we propose a potentially useful way to determine whether a type I exceptional series in
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell}
by using the
s=1
case as an explicit example. When
s=1
, the
{\cal V}_{1,0}
is characterized by a spin 1 state
|\psi)_{a}
satisfying

\displaystyle L_{a0}|\psi)_{a}=0,\,\,\,\,\,L_{a0}|\psi)_{b}-L_{b0}|\psi)_{a}=0

(5.44)

A generic
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
vector in
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell}
is a linear combination of

\displaystyle|\xi^{-}_{n})_{a}=(L^{+}\cdot L^{+})^{n}|\Delta\rangle_{a},\,\,\,\,\,\,|\xi^{+}_{n})_{a}=(L^{+}\cdot L^{+})^{n}L^{+}_{a}|\Delta\rangle

(5.45)

where
|\Delta\rangle_{a_{1}}=L^{+}_{a_{2}}\cdots L^{+}_{a_{\ell}}|\Delta\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{\ell}}
and
|\Delta\rangle=L^{+}_{a_{1}}\cdots L^{+}_{a_{\ell}}|\Delta\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{\ell}}
. By definition,
|\xi^{\pm}_{n})_{a}
has level
\ell+2n\pm 1
. Let
|\psi)_{c}=\sum_{n\geq 0}\left(a_{n}|\xi^{-}_{n})_{c}+b_{n}|\xi^{+}_{n})_{c}\right)
be a spin 1 state in
{\cal V}_{1,0}
.
After some lengthy computations, one can show that the requirement
L_{a0}|\psi)_{b}-L_{b0}|\psi)_{a}=0
is equivalent to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}a_{n}+2(n+1)\left(n+\Delta+\ell-\frac{d-1}{2}\right)a_{n+1}=\ell(\Delta-d)b_{n}

(5.46)

and
L_{a0}|\psi)_{a}=0
is equivalent to

\displaystyle\frac{a_{n}\!+\!b_{n-1}}{2}\!+\!2(n\!+\!1)\left(n\!+\!\Delta\!+\!\ell\!+\!\frac{d\!-\!1}{2}\right)a_{n+1}\!+\!\left(2n\left(n\!+\!\Delta\!+\!\ell\!+\!\frac{d\!+\!1}{2}\right)\!+\!(d\!+\!1)\Delta\!+\!\ell(\Delta\!+\!1)\right)b_{n}=0

(5.47)

When
d=1
, the UIR
{\cal V}_{1,0}
reduces to a discrete series representation of
{\text{SO}}(1,2)
, and in this case solving (
5.46
) and (
5.47
) reduces to
b_{n-1}+4(n+\Delta)(n+\ell+1)b_{n}=0
, which is consistent with
Hogervorst:2021uvp
. In principle, one can derive
\{a_{n},b_{n}\}
from (
5.46
) and (
5.47
), and then use them to check whether
|\psi)_{c}
is normalizable. A normalizable
|\psi)_{c}
corresponds to the existence of
{\cal V}_{1,0}
. Unfortunately, we could not solve the two couple recurrence relations for
d\geq 2
. On the other hand, we want to mention that a significant simplification happens when
\Delta
approaches the unitarity bound, i.e.
\Delta=d+\ell-1
. In this case, the primary state, which will be denoted by
|J_{\ell}\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{\ell}}
henceforth, is a spin
\ell
conserved current in the sense of
L_{a_{1}}^{+}|J_{1}\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{\ell}}=0
. Because of this property, all
|\xi^{+}_{n})_{a}
vanish for
\ell\geq 0
, and all
|\xi^{-}_{n})_{a}
vanish for
\ell\geq 1
, which means that the primary representation generated by a conserved current of spin larger than 1 cannot have
{\cal V}_{1,0}
. For
\ell=1
,
|\psi)_{a}
should be a linear combination of
|\xi^{-}_{n})_{a}
only. We can simply set all
b_{n}\equiv 0
and take
\ell=1,\Delta=d
in eq. (
5.46
) or eq. (
5.47
). It gives

\displaystyle 4n\left(n+\frac{d+1}{2}\right)a_{n}+a_{n-1}=0\rightsquigarrow a_{n}=\frac{(-)^{n}}{4^{n}n!(\frac{d+3}{2})_{n}}

(5.48)

To obtain the norm of
|\xi_{n}^{-})_{a}
, we need the following relation

\displaystyle\left(L_{-}\cdot L_{-}\right)|\xi_{n}^{-})_{a}

\displaystyle=4n\left(n+\frac{d-1}{2}\right)\left(2(d+1)H+L^{+}\cdot L^{-}\right)|\xi^{-}_{n-1})_{a}-4n\left[L^{-}_{a},L^{+}_{b}\right]|\xi^{-}_{n-1})_{b}

\displaystyle=16n\left(n+\frac{d-3}{2}\right)\left(n+\frac{d+1}{2}\right)(n+d-1)|\xi^{-}_{n-1})_{a}

(5.49)

It fixes
{}_{a}(\xi^{-}_{n}|\xi^{-}_{n})_{b}
up to an overall constant (which is suppressed here)

{}_{a}(\xi^{-}_{n}|\xi^{-}_{n})_{b}=4^{2n}n!\left(\frac{d-1}{2}\right)_{n}\left(\frac{d+3}{2}\right)_{n}(d)_{n}\delta_{ab}

(5.50)

Combining (
5.48
) and (
5.50
), we find that
|\psi)_{a}
is nonnormalizable for
d\geq 2
:

{}_{a}(\psi|\psi)_{b}=\delta_{ab}\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{\left(\frac{d-1}{2}\right)_{n}(d)_{n}}{n!(\frac{d+3}{2})_{n}}=\infty

(5.51)

It excludes the existence of
{\cal V}_{1,0}
. Moreover, in this case, we can also prove that
{\cal U}_{1,0}
cannot be present.
Recall that
{\cal U}_{1,0}
is characterized by a state
|\psi)_{a,b}=-|\psi)_{b,a}
satisfying
L_{a0}|\psi)_{a,b}=0
, because
{\cal U}_{1,0}
does not contain spin 1 representations of
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
. The state
|\psi)_{a,b}
should be a linear combination

|\psi)_{a,b}=\sum_{n\geq 0}c_{n}|1,n)_{a,b}
,
where

\displaystyle|1,n)_{a,b}=(L^{+}\cdot L^{+})^{n}\left(L^{+}_{a}|J_{1}\rangle_{b}-L^{+}_{b}|J_{1}\rangle_{a}\right)~.

(5.52)

After a small calculation, one can show

\displaystyle L_{a0}|1,n)_{a,b}

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{2}\left(L^{+}_{a}+L^{-}_{a}\right)|1,n)_{a,b}

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{2}\left(L^{+}\cdot L^{+}\right)^{n+1}|J_{1}\rangle_{b}+i(n+d)(2n+d-1)\left(L^{+}\cdot L^{+}\right)^{n}|J_{1}\rangle_{b}~.

(5.53)

Then it is clear that
L_{a0}|\psi)_{a,b}
vanishes if and only if
c_{0}=0
and

\displaystyle c_{n-1}+4(n+1)\left(n+\frac{d-1}{2}\right)c_{n}=0~.

(5.54)

For
d\geq 2
, the recurrence relation eq. (
5.54
) together with the initial condition
c_{0}=0
only has trivial solution, i.e.
c_{n}\equiv 0
. Therefore, the primary representation generated by a spin 1 conserved current does not contain any type
{\cal U}
exceptional series.

The reader may wonder where the exceptional series are hiding in the conformal multiplets.
For example, free Maxwell theory, which is a CFT in dS
4
, must contain
{\cal U}_{1,0}
which describes single photon states.
We expect that
{\cal U}_{1,0}
is contained in the conformal multiplet of the gauge invariant local operator that creates single photons, i.e. the field strength which has 2
anti-symmetric
indices.
We speculate that conformal multiplets with two row Young tableaus contain
{\cal U}_{s,t}
.

Appendix A
Fourier transform of
\psi
functions

Let
z,w\in\mathbb{C}
be two complex numbers with
positive
real parts. Consider the following integral

\displaystyle{\cal I}(z,w)\equiv\int_{0}^{\infty}\,dt\,\frac{e^{-zt}-e^{-wt}}{1-e^{-t}}

(A.1)

Using the integral representation of
\psi
function

\displaystyle\psi(z)=\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\left(\frac{e^{-t}}{t}-\frac{e^{-zt}}{1-e^{-t}}\right)

(A.2)

one can immediately find that

\displaystyle{\cal I}(z,w)=\psi(w)-\psi(z),\quad\text{when}\,\,{\rm Re\,}(z)>0,{\rm Re\,}(w)>0

(A.3)

Although the original integral definition of
{\cal I}(z,w)
only makes sense when
{\rm Re\,}(z),{\rm Re\,}(w)>0
, the
\psi
function representation provides a natural analytical continuation to
\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}
minus a discrete set of points. It allows us to consider the following Fourier transformation

\displaystyle{\cal J}(a,b)

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}d\lambda\,\left({\cal I}(a+i\lambda,b+i\lambda)+{\cal I}(a-i\lambda,b-i\lambda)\right)e^{i\lambda t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}d\lambda\,\left(\psi(b+i\lambda)+\psi(b-i\lambda)-\psi(a+i\lambda)-\psi(a-i\lambda)\right)e^{i\lambda t}

(A.4)

for any
a,b\in\mathbb{C}
. First consider
{\rm Re\,}(a),{\rm Re\,}(b)>0
. In this case, we are allowed to use the integral representation (
A.1
) of
{\cal I}
, which yields

\displaystyle{\cal I}(a+i\lambda,b+i\lambda)+{\cal I}(a-i\lambda,b-i\lambda)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}dt\,\frac{e^{-a|t|}-e^{-b|t|}}{1-e^{-|t|}}e^{-i\lambda t}

(A.5)

Combining eq. (
A
) and eq. (
A.5
), we find that

\displaystyle{\cal J}(a,b)=\frac{e^{-a|t|}-e^{-b|t|}}{1-e^{-|t|}},\quad\text{when}\,\,{\rm Re\,}(a),{\rm Re\,}(b)>0.

(A.6)

Next, consider
{\rm Re\,}(a)<0
and
{\rm Re\,}(b)>0
. Without loss of generality, assume that
{\rm Re\,}(a)
is between
-(n+1)
and
-n
for some positive integer
n
. The strategy is to find a relation between
{\cal J}(a,b)
and
{\cal J}(a+n,b)
, since the latter can be computed with eq. (
A.6
). Applying the
\psi
function recurrence relation
\psi(z+1)=\psi(z)+\frac{1}{z}
to
{\cal I}(a\pm i\lambda,b\pm i\lambda)
yields

\displaystyle{\cal I}(a\pm i\lambda,b\pm i\lambda)

\displaystyle=\psi(b\pm i\lambda)-\psi(a\pm i\lambda)

\displaystyle=\psi(b\pm i\lambda)-\psi(a+n+1\pm i\lambda)+\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{1}{a+k\pm i\lambda}

\displaystyle={\cal I}(a+n+1\pm i\lambda,b\pm i\lambda)+\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{1}{a+k\pm i\lambda}

(A.7)

Plugging eq. (
A
) into the Fourier transform eq. (
A
), we obtain

\displaystyle{\cal J}(a,b)

\displaystyle={\cal J}(a+n+1,b)+\sum_{k=0}^{n}(a+k)\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{d\lambda}{\pi}\,\frac{e^{i\lambda t}}{(a+k)^{2}+\lambda^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-(a+n+1)|t|}-e^{-b|t|}}{1-e^{-|t|}}-\sum_{k=0}^{n}e^{(a+k)|t|}

(A.8)

where we have used eq. (
A.6
) and
\int_{\mathbb{R}}dt\frac{1}{x^{2}+t^{2}}e^{it\lambda}=\frac{\pi}{|x|}e^{-|x\lambda|}
. Some further rewriting of eq. (
A
) leads to

\displaystyle{\cal J}(a,b)=\frac{e^{-a|t|}-e^{-b|t|}}{1-e^{-|t|}}-2\sum_{k=0}^{n}\cosh\left((a+k)t\right)

(A.9)

when
-(n+1)<{\rm Re\,}(a)<-n,{\rm Re\,}(b)>0
.

As both
{\rm Re\,}(a)>0
and
{\rm Re\,}(a)<0
have been discussed, the remaining case is
{\rm Re\,}(a)=0
. However, for a generic imaginary
a
, the integral (
A
) is not well-defined because
\psi(a\pm i\lambda)
have poles
\lambda=\pm ia
, lying on the integration contour. The only exception is
a=0
in that as
a
approaches
0
, the two poles
\pm ia
collide and annihilate each other. More explicitly, using the recurrence relation of
\psi
functions, we get
\psi(i\lambda)+\psi(-i\lambda)=\psi(1+i\lambda)+\psi(1-i\lambda)
, which yields

\displaystyle{\cal J}(0,b)={\cal J}(1,b)=\frac{1-e^{-b|t|}}{1-e^{-|t|}}-1,\quad\text{when}\,\,{\rm Re\,}(b)>0.

(A.10)

where we have used eq. (
A.6
) for
{\cal J}(1,b)
.

Appendix B
Numerical analysis of
L_{0}\neq 0
sector of
SO
(1,2)

In section
3.2.2
, we numerically analyzed the decomposition of tensor product
{\cal F}_{\Delta_{1}}\otimes{\cal F}_{\Delta_{2}}
by focusing on
L_{0}=0
sector. The advantage of focusing on
L_{0}=0
sector is that the discrete series
{\cal D}^{\pm}_{p}
are absent. On the other hand, the formula (
3.16
) is not written in any particular
L_{0}
eigensector. Strictly speaking, the numerical analysis through which we are finding density of states
\bar{\rho}_{\cal N}
is
L_{0}
-variant and it is not a good candidate to match with the results in
3.2.2
since we do not project to any particular
L_{0}
eigenspace in the character analysis. In this section, we show that indeed, the
L_{0}=0
sector captures all the features of
relative density
of states
\rho_{\text{rel}}
and considering it is enough to check the character analysis result.

Let
{\cal H}_{j}
be the
L_{0}=j
subspace of
{\cal F}_{\Delta_{1}}\otimes{\cal F}_{\Delta_{2}}
. Consider the basis of
|\psi_{n,j})\equiv|n,j-n),~n\in\mathbb{Z}
for
{\cal H}_{j}
25
25

25

There is no obvious parity symmetry here except for when
\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{2}
where one can define

|\psi^{(\pm)}_{n,j})=|n,j-n)\pm|j-n,n)

. Following equations (
3.6
) and (
3.1
), one finds that the matrix elements of Casimir

\displaystyle{\cal Q}_{mn}^{(j)}\equiv\frac{(\psi_{m,j}|C^{{\text{SO}}(1,2)}|\psi_{n,j})}{\sqrt{(\psi_{m,j}|\psi_{m,j})(\psi_{n,j}|\psi_{n,j})}}

(B.1)

in
{\cal H}_{j}
is given by

{\cal Q}_{nn}^{(j)}=2n(n-j)+\Delta_{1}\bar{\Delta}_{1}+\Delta_{2}\bar{\Delta}_{2}~,\qquad{\cal Q}_{n+1,n}^{(j)}=\left({\cal Q}_{n,n+1}^{(j)}\right)^{*}=\beta_{n,j}~

(B.2)

where

\displaystyle\beta_{n,j}=-\begin{cases}(n+\Delta_{1})(n-j+\Delta_{2}),&{\cal P}_{\Delta_{1}}\otimes{\cal P}_{\Delta_{2}}\\
(n+\Delta_{1})\sqrt{(n-j+\Delta_{2})(n-j+\bar{\Delta}_{2})},&{\cal P}_{\Delta_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{C}_{\Delta_{2}}\\
(n-j+\Delta_{2})\sqrt{(n+\Delta_{1})(n+\bar{\Delta}_{1})},&\mathcal{C}_{\Delta_{1}}\otimes{\cal P}_{\Delta_{2}}\\
\sqrt{(n+\Delta_{1})(n+\bar{\Delta}_{1})(n-j+\Delta_{2})(n-j+\bar{\Delta}_{2})},&\mathcal{C}_{\Delta_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{C}_{\Delta_{2}}\end{cases}

(B.3)

A similar large
n
argument as in section
3.2.2
, with the same
\alpha_{\pm}(\lambda)
in (
3.40
), shows that all the principal series representations appear in this tensor product as well. We may now diagonalize the
{\cal Q}^{(j)}
matrix introducing a cutoff
-{\cal N}\leq n\leq{\cal N}
and study the density of states corresponding to
L_{0}=j
subspace,
\bar{\rho}_{\cal N}^{(j)}
, and its relative density
\rho^{(j)}_{\text{rel}}
. Discrete series
{\cal D}^{+}_{p}
with
0<p\leq j
show up in the decomposition of subspace
{\cal H}_{j}
. This can be seen in fig. (
B.1
) where the Casimir values of discrete series
p(1-p)
are apparent.

Figure B.1
:
First a few eigenvalues of Casimir
{\cal Q}^{(j)}
. Left: tensor product of two principal series representations in the
{\cal H}_{3}
subspace. Right: tensor product of two complementary series representations in the
{\cal H}_{1}
subspace. Discrete series representations with
{\cal D}^{\pm}_{p}
with
p\in\{1,2,\cdots,j\}
also appear in the spectrum. The solid line
{\cal Q}=2\mu(1-2\mu)
in the right panel corresponds to the expected single complementary series representation
\mathcal{C}_{2\mu}
in the tensor
\mathcal{C}_{\frac{1}{2}+\mu}\otimes\mathcal{C}_{\frac{1}{2}+\mu}
when
\frac{1}{4}<\mu<\frac{1}{2}
.

Figure B.2
:
Left: Illustration of how the interpolation of
\bar{\rho}_{\cal N}^{(j)}
is derived. The raw data of
\bar{\rho}_{\cal N}^{(j)}
has oscillations (the red and blue dots). To derive a smooth
\bar{\rho}_{\cal N}^{(j)}
we first interpolate each red and blue points individually and then take an average of them to find the purple dashed line. The green line is the shifted
{\cal K}_{\text{hc}}
.
Right: A plot of
\bar{\rho}_{\cal N}^{(j)}
for various values of
j
in small
\lambda
region. The dashed black line is the
{\cal K}_{\text{hc}}
which is shifted by
\sim+6.745
in y-axis. The
\bar{\rho}_{\cal N}^{(j)}
is
j
-independent in large
\lambda
region but in small
\lambda
region they do not match as one varies
j
. Only
\bar{\rho}_{\cal N}^{(0)}
agrees with
{\cal K}_{\text{hc}}
.

In fig. (
3.4
), we observe a great agreement between
\bar{\rho}_{\cal N}^{(j)}
and hard-cutoff renormalized density of states
{\cal K}_{\text{hc}}
defined in (
3.18
) up to a shift. However, this is solely true in
L_{0}=0
sector.
For
L_{0}\neq 0
sectors, in the small
\lambda
region the numerics do not agree with
{\cal K}_{\text{hc}}
as shown in fig. (
B.2
). On the other hand, the relative density of different
L_{0}=j
sectors defined as

\rho_{\text{rel}}^{(j)}=\bar{\rho}^{(j),{\cal P}_{\Delta_{1}}\otimes{\cal P}_{\Delta_{2}}}_{{\cal N}}-\bar{\rho}^{(j),{\cal P}_{\Delta_{3}}\otimes{\cal P}_{\Delta_{4}}}_{{\cal N}}

(B.4)

converges to the
{\cal K}_{\text{rel}}
in (
3.2.2
). In addition to this numerical evidence for the claim that
\rho_{\text{rel}}
is unique in all
L_{0}
sectors and matches with
{\cal K}_{\text{rel}}
, in what follows we present an analytical argument why this is the case.

Figure B.3
:
A perfect match of
{\cal K}_{\text{rel}}
(the dashed black line) and
\rho_{\text{rel}}^{(j)}
s. The
\rho_{\text{rel}}^{(j)}
for different
j
converges to the same function in large
{\cal N}
. The agreement is good enough that puts them on top of each other in the plot and make them indistinguishable.

The tensor product character in (
3.15
) can be computed in
|n,m)
basis as

\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}(n,j-n|q^{D}|n,j-n)=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}A_{j}(q)

(B.5)

where

A_{j}(q)\equiv\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}(n|q^{D}|n)(j-n|q^{D}|j-n)

(B.6)

and its dependence on
\Delta_{1}=\frac{1}{2}+i\mu_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}=\frac{1}{2}+i\mu_{2}
is implicit. Moreover, concerning the right side of (
3.15
), each character admits a similar expression:

\frac{q^{\frac{1}{2}+i\lambda}+q^{\frac{1}{2}-i\lambda}}{1-q}=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}B_{j}(q)~,\qquad B_{j}(q)\equiv(j|q^{D}|j)~.

(B.7)

We would like to see whether, within each
j
sector, we can write an equation similar to (
3.15
):

A_{j}(q)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}d\lambda~{\cal K}_{j}(\lambda)B_{j}(q)~+\text{Discrete series}~.

(B.8)

To this end, let us write
A_{j}(q)
and
B_{j}(q)
in a
\delta
-function normalized basis
|x\rangle
of
{\cal P}_{\Delta}
mentioned in
Sun:2021thf
which satisfies the following relations:

q^{D}|x\rangle=q^{\bar{\Delta}}\bigl|qx\bigr\rangle,~\quad\langle x|n)=\frac{2^{\Delta}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\left(\frac{1-ix}{1+ix}\right)^{n}\frac{1}{(1+x^{2})^{\Delta}}~.

(B.9)

Plugging in the identity operator
\mathds{1}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}dx|x\rangle\langle x|
into definition of
B_{j}(q)
, we find

B_{j}(q)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{dx}{\sqrt{(1+x^{2})(q^{-1}+qx^{2})}}W(q,x)^{j}Q(q,x)^{i\lambda}

(B.10)

where we define

Q(q,x)\equiv\frac{1+q^{2}x^{2}}{q(1+x^{2})}~,\quad W(q,x)\equiv\frac{(1-ix)(1+iqx)}{(1+ix)(1-iqx)}~.

(B.11)

Similarly, for
A_{j}(q)
, we have

\begin{split}A_{j}(q)&=\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}dx_{1}dx_{2}~W(q,x)^{j}\left(\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\Phi(q;x_{1},x_{2})^{n}\right)\prod_{i=1,2}\frac{1}{(1+x^{2})^{\Delta_{i}}(q^{-1}+qx_{i}^{2})^{\bar{\Delta}_{i}}}\\
&=\frac{q}{2\pi(1-q)}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{dx}{|1-qx^{2}|}W(q,x)^{j}\sum_{\pm\pm}Q(q,x)^{\pm i\mu_{\pm}}\end{split}

(B.12)

where in the second line we perform the integral of the phase

\Phi(q;x_{1},x_{2})\equiv W(q,x_{1})W(q,-x_{2})=e^{i\phi(q;x_{1},x_{2})}

(B.13)

over
x_{2}
by noticing that

\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{in\phi(q;x_{1},x_{2})}=2\pi\delta\left(\phi(q;x_{1},x_{2})\right)~,

(B.14)

with support at
\Phi(q;x_{1},x_{2})=1
.

Equation (
B.8
) is formal since the integral representation for
A_{j}(q)
is divergent because of the singularity at
x=1/\sqrt{q}
. However, if we take a derivative with respect to one of the
\mu_{i}
’s (
i=1,2
), then this equation becomes well defined. Of course, in this way we will lose information about the
\mu
-independent part of
{\cal K}_{j}(\lambda)
including the discrete series.
Consider derivative of (
B.8
):

\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu_{i}}A_{j}(q)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}d\lambda~B_{j}(q)\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu_{i}}{\cal K}_{j}^{(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2})}(\lambda)~

(B.15)

where we introduce the superscript to remark the
\mu_{i}
-dependence.
Plugging in the integral representations of
A_{j}
and
B_{j}
introduced in (
B.12
) and (
B.10
), and using the fact that the integrals are well-defined, so we may interchange the integrals over
x
and
\lambda
, one finds that both equations have the same
j
dependence through
W(q,x)^{j}
and what is left is:

i\log Q\,\frac{Q^{i\mu_{+}}+Q^{i\mu_{-}}-Q^{-i\mu_{+}}-Q^{-i\mu_{-}}}{|Q^{\frac{1}{2}}-Q^{-\frac{1}{2}}|}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}d\lambda~\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu_{i}}{\cal K}_{j}^{(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2})}(\lambda)~Q^{i\lambda}~,

(B.16)

where
\mu_{\pm}=\mu_{1}\pm\mu_{2}
.
The left hand side of this equation is
j
-independent which means that
\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu_{i}}{\cal K}_{j}^{(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2})}(\lambda)
is also
j
-independent. From this, one concludes that the difference of
{\cal K}_{j}^{(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2})}(\lambda)-{\cal K}_{j}^{(\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4})}(\lambda)
is also
j
-independent.
This completes the argument that
{\cal K}_{\text{rel}}(\lambda)
within each
L_{0}=j
sector – which corresponds to the tensor product character
A_{j}(q)
and single particle character
B_{j}(q)
– has to be
j
-independent and illustrates results in fig. (
B.3
) where
\rho_{\text{rel}}
is
j
-independent in large
{\cal N}
.

CFT in dS

There is a similar story for the case of CFT in dS
2
. Given a primary representation
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell}
of
{\text{SO}}(2,2)
, let
{\cal H}^{(s)}_{\Delta,\ell}
be the subspace spanned by all descendants of spin
s\in\mathbb{Z}

26
26

26

The spin
s
is an eigenvalue of the generator
L_{0}\in{\text{SO}}(1,2)\subset{\text{SO}}(2,2)
. So
{\cal H}^{(s)}_{\Delta,\ell}
is the counterpart of
{\cal H}_{j}
in the tensor product case.
. It is shown in the appendix D of
Hogervorst:2021uvp
that the
{\text{SO}}(1,2)
Casimir restricted in the subspace
{\cal H}^{(s)}_{\Delta,\ell}
can be represented by the following matrix:

\displaystyle{\cal Q}^{(s)}_{n,n}=2n(n+\ell-s)+\Delta(\ell+2n+1)-s(\ell+\Delta)~,

\displaystyle{\cal Q}^{(s)}_{n+1,n}={\cal Q}^{(s)}_{n,n+1}=-\sqrt{(n+1)(n+\Delta-s)(n+\ell-s+1)(n+\Delta+\ell)}

(B.17)

with
n\geq\left|\text{Min}(0,\ell-s)\right|
.

Figure B.4
:
First five low-lying eigenvalues of
SO
(1,2)
Casimir matrix restricted to the subspace
s=0,1,2,3
for
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell=3}
. The points above the dashed line belong to principal series. In addition, the discrete series with Casimir
p(1-p)
where
p\in\{1,2,\cdots,s\}
show up as discussed in the text.

In section
5.2.2
,
we have studied the spectrum property of
{\cal Q}^{(0)}
numerically.
Now we show some observations for the
s\not=0
case.
As before, we truncate the size of
{\cal Q}^{(s)}
by a large number
\mathcal{N}
and diagonalize the truncated matrix numerically. As proven in
Hogervorst:2021uvp
, in decomposition of
SO
(2,2)
primary representation
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell}
, discrete series representations

\{{\cal D}^{\rm sign(\ell)}_{1},{\cal D}^{\rm sign(\ell)}_{2},\cdots,{\cal D}^{\rm sign(\ell)}_{\ell}\}
appear. In section
5.2.2
, we were blind to them in the numerics as we consider
{\text{SO}}(1,2)
Casimir in the
s=0
subspace.
In fig. (
B.4
), we identify these discrete series representations as certain eigenvalues of
{\cal Q}^{(s)}
for
s\not=0
.

Figure B.5
:
Left: Plot of
\bar{\rho}^{(s)}_{\cal N}
for different
s
. They differ in the small
\lambda
region. The dashed line is the renormalized density
{\cal K}_{\text{PV}}(\lambda;\ell)
in (
B.18
) with
\Lambda\sim 8\times 10^{3}
.
Right: The plot of
\rho_{\text{rel}}
for
\Delta=4.5
and
\Delta^{\prime}=1.3
in
s=2
subspace with truncation cutoff
{\cal N}=2000
(pink line) that matches with
{\cal K}_{\text{rel}}(\lambda,\ell)
(black dashed line). The inset plot shows the convergence to
{\cal K}_{\text{rel}}(\lambda;\ell)
(c.f. eq. (
5.2.1
)) in large
{\cal N}
limit by zooming in the region
\lambda\in(12.9,13)
.

Analogous to eq. (
3.23
) and eq. (
4.14
), we can define a regularized version of
{\cal K}(\lambda;\ell)
formally defined in (
5.17
) by taking the limit
\Delta^{\prime}=\Lambda\to\infty
in eq. (
5.2.1
):

{\cal K}_{\text{PV}}(\lambda;\ell)=\frac{\log(\Lambda)}{\pi}-\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{\pm}\psi\left(\Delta-\frac{1}{2}\pm i\lambda\right)-\frac{1}{\pi}\sum_{k=1}^{|\ell|}\frac{k-\frac{1}{2}}{(k-\frac{1}{2})^{2}+\lambda^{2}}~.

(B.18)

Again, similar to what we see for the tensor products, the coarse-grained densities
\bar{\rho}^{(s)}_{\cal N}
extracted from
{\cal Q}^{(s)}
for various
s
differ in the small
\lambda
region and
{\cal K}_{\text{PV}}(\lambda;\ell)
matches with
\bar{\rho}^{(s=0)}_{\cal N}
up to fixing a value for
\Lambda
– see the left panel of fig. (
B.5
) – similar to the observasion we had for the case of tensor products illustrated in fig. (
3.4
) and fig. (
B.2
).
However, the relative density
\rho_{\text{rel}}
has been observed to be
s
-independent in large
{\cal N}
limit and it matches perfectly with
{\cal K}_{\text{rel}}(\lambda;\ell)
(c.f. eq. (
5.2.1
)), as shown in the right panel of fig. (
B.5
).

In this section, we gave numerical and analytical evidence that for
d=1
and in the large
{\cal N}
limit, the
relative
coarse-grained density extracted from diagonalizing the truncated Casimir matrix is the same for different subsectors labeled by
L_{0}=j
(for tensor product) or
L_{0}=s
(for CFT). Therefore, for convenience, we pick the
L_{0}=0
subspace in sections
3
and
5
for our numerical investigation. We conjecture this to be true in higher dimensions, although we did not explicitly check it. The matching of the relative density from the character analysis with the numerical results in sections
4
and
5
, on the other hand, is an evidence for this conjecture.

Appendix C
Summing over
{\text{SO}}(d)
Weyl character

In this appendix, we will give a proof of the following series involving
{\text{SO}}(d)
Weyl characters

\displaystyle\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}\,\chi^{{\text{SO}}(d)}_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{s}}(\bm{x})\,q^{s}=\frac{1-q^{2}}{P_{d}(q,\bm{x})}

(C.1)

where
0<q<1
and
P_{d}(q,\bm{x})
is defined in eq. (
2.15
). First we want to argue that it suffices to prove (
C.1
) for even
d
. According to the branching rule,
{\text{SO}}(2r+1)
and
{\text{SO}}(2r)
characters are related by

\displaystyle\chi^{{\text{SO}}(2r+1)}_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{s}}(\bm{x})=\sum_{m=0}^{s}\chi^{{\text{SO}}(2r)}_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{m}}(\bm{x})

(C.2)

which implies

\displaystyle\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}\,\chi^{{\text{SO}}(2r+1)}_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{s}}(\bm{x})\,q^{s}=\sum_{m\geq 0}\chi^{{\text{SO}}(2r)}_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{m}}(\bm{x})\sum_{s\geq m}\,q^{s}=\frac{1}{1-q}\sum_{m\geq 0}\chi^{{\text{SO}}(2r)}_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{m}}(\bm{x})q^{m}

(C.3)

If (
C.1
) holds for even
d=2r
, i.e.
\sum_{m\geq 0}\chi^{{\text{SO}}(2r)}_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{m}}(\bm{x})q^{m}=\frac{1-q^{2}}{P_{2r}(q,\bm{x})}
, then it is obviously correct for
d=2r+1
since
P_{2r+1}(q,\bm{x})=(1-q)P_{2r}(q,\bm{x})
.

To prove (
C.1
) for even
d=2r
, we need an explicit expression of
\chi^{{\text{SO}}(d)}_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{s}}(\bm{x})
, which is given by the famous Weyl character formula
fulton2013representation

\displaystyle\chi^{{\text{SO}}(d)}_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{s}}(\bm{x})=\frac{|x_{i}^{\ell_{j}}+x_{i}^{-\ell_{j}}|}{|x_{i}^{n-j}+x_{i}^{-(n-j)}|}

(C.4)

Let’s briefly explain the notations in this formula:

•

\ell_{j}
is the
j
-th component of the vector
\bm{\ell}=(s+r-1,r-2,r-3,\cdots,1,0)
.

•

The numerator
|x_{i}^{\ell_{j}}+x_{i}^{-\ell_{j}}|
means the determinant of the matrix
N_{\rho}
whose
(j,i)
entry is
x_{i}^{\ell_{j}}+x_{i}^{-\ell_{j}}
.

•

The denominator
|x_{i}^{n-j}+x_{i}^{-(n-j)}|
means the determinant of the matrix
D_{\rho}
whose
(j,i)
entry is
x_{i}^{n-j}+x_{i}^{-(n-j)}
. It is well known that
D_{\rho}
can be alternatively expressed as

\displaystyle|D_{\rho}|=2\,\Psi(x_{i}+x_{i}^{-1}),\,\,\,\,\ \Psi(\xi_{i})\equiv\prod_{i<j}(\xi_{i}-\xi_{j})

(C.5)

Since only the first row of
N_{\rho}
depends on
s
, the sum
\sum_{s\geq 0}|N_{\rho}|q^{s}
effectively changes the first row in the following way:

\displaystyle x_{i}^{s+r-1}+x_{i}^{1-r-s}\to\frac{x_{i}^{r-1}}{1-x_{i}q}+\frac{x_{i}^{1-r}}{1-x_{i}^{-1}q}

(C.6)

Denote this new matrix by
N^{\prime}_{\rho}
and it is related to the series
\sum_{s\geq 0}\chi^{{\text{SO}}(d)}_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{s}}(\bm{x})q^{s}
by

\displaystyle\sum_{s\geq 0}\chi^{{\text{SO}}(d)}_{{\mathbb{Y}}_{s}}(\bm{x})q^{s}=\frac{|N^{\prime}_{\rho}|}{|D_{\rho}|}

(C.7)

Then we add the rest rows to the first row of
N^{\prime}_{\rho}
, with a weight
q^{-(j-1)}
for the
j
-th row (except the last row, for which the weight is
\frac{1}{2}q^{1-r}
), which yields

\displaystyle\frac{x_{i}^{r-1}}{1-x_{i}q}+\frac{x_{i}^{1-r}}{1-x_{i}^{-1}q}

\displaystyle\to\frac{x_{i}^{r-1}}{1-x_{i}q}+\frac{x_{i}^{1-r}}{1-x_{i}^{-1}q}+q^{-1}(x_{i}^{r-2}+x_{i}^{2-r})+\cdots+q^{-(r-1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{q^{2-r}\left(q^{-1}-q\right)}{1+q^{2}-(x_{i}+x_{i}^{-1})q}=\frac{q^{2-r}\left(q^{-1}-q\right)}{P(q,x_{i})}

(C.8)

where
P(q,x)\equiv 1-(x+x^{-1})q+q^{2}
.
Altogether, we can rewrite
|N_{\rho}^{\prime}|
as

\displaystyle|N_{\rho}^{\prime}|=2q^{2-r}\left(q^{-1}-q\right)\det\begin{pmatrix}P(q,x_{1})^{-1}&P(q,x_{2})^{-1}&\cdots&P(q,x_{r})^{-1}\\
x_{1}^{r-2}+x_{1}^{2-r}&x_{2}^{r-2}+x_{2}^{2-r}&\cdots&x_{r}^{r-2}+x_{r}^{2-r}\\
\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots\\
x_{1}+x_{1}^{-1}&x_{2}+x_{2}^{-1}&\cdots&x_{r}+x_{r}^{-1}\\
1&1&\cdots&1\end{pmatrix}

(C.9)

where the factor 2 appears because we have rescaled the last row. Now a crucial step is to replace
x_{i}^{j}+x_{i}^{-j}
by
(-q)^{-j}P(q,x_{i})^{j}
, which does not change the determinant. With this replacement, the matrix becomes essentially a Vandermonde matrix up to some reshuffling and rescaling

\displaystyle|N_{\rho}^{\prime}|

\displaystyle=\frac{2q^{2-r}\left(q^{-1}-q\right)}{(-q)^{\frac{(r-1)(r-2)}{2}}}\det\begin{pmatrix}P(q,x_{1})^{-1}&P(q,x_{2})^{-1}&\cdots&P(q,x_{r})^{-1}\\
P(q,x_{1})^{r-2}&P(q,x_{2})^{r-2}&\cdots&P(q,x_{r})^{r-2}\\
\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots\\
P(q,x_{1})&P(q,x_{2})&\cdots&P(q,x_{r})\\
1&1&\cdots&1\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\,(-)^{r-1}q^{2-r}\left(q^{-1}-q\right)}{(-q)^{\frac{(r-1)(r-2)}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{r}P(q,x_{i})}\det V(P(q,x_{i}))

(C.10)

where
V(\xi_{i})
is the
r\times r
Vandermonde matrix of
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\cdots,\xi_{r}
. Plugging in
\det V(\xi_{i})=\prod_{i<j}(\xi_{j}-\xi_{i})
yields

\displaystyle|N_{\rho}^{\prime}|

\displaystyle=\frac{2\,(-)^{r-1}q^{2-r}\left(q^{-1}-q\right)}{(-q)^{\frac{(r-1)(r-2)}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{r}P(q,x_{i})}\prod_{i<j}\left(P(q,x_{j})-P(q,x_{i})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{(-)^{r-1}(-q)^{\frac{r(r-1)}{2}}q^{2-r}\left(q^{-1}-q\right)}{(-q)^{\frac{(r-1)(r-2)}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{r}P(q,x_{i})}\left(2\Psi(x_{i}+x_{i}^{-1})\right)

(C.11)

Notice that the last term is exactly the denominator
|D_{\rho}|
and hence we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{s\geq 0}\chi^{{\text{SO}}(d)}_{s}(\bm{x})q^{s}=\frac{(-)^{r-1}(-q)^{\frac{r(r-1)}{2}}q^{2-r}\left(q^{-1}-q\right)}{(-q)^{\frac{(r-1)(r-2)}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{r}P(q,x_{i})}=\frac{1-q^{2}}{P_{d}(q,\bm{x})}

(C.12)

This finishes our proof of (
C.1
).

Appendix D
Matrix elements of the noncompact generators in
{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)

The noncompact generators of
{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)
are denoted by
L_{0a}
where
a=1,\cdots,d+1
. In particular,
L_{0,d+1}=D
is the dilatation operator.
The quadratic Casimir of
{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)
can be expressed as

\displaystyle C^{{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)}=-L^{2}_{0a}-C^{{\text{SO}}(d+1)}

(D.1)

where
C^{{\text{SO}}(d+1)}
is the usual
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
Casimir, which equals
n(n+d-1)
for a spin-
n
representation.
In this appendix, we will derive explicitly how
L_{0a}
acts on the
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
content of a continuous UIR
{\cal F}_{\Delta}
.
We will first focus on the principal series case, e.g.
\Delta=\frac{d}{2}+i\mu
, and then show how the results can be generalized to complementary series. With the action of
L_{0a}
being derived, we will use eq. (
D.1
) to compute the matrix elements of
C^{{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)}
in certain subsectors of the tensor product
{\cal F}_{\Delta_{1}}\otimes{\cal F}_{\Delta_{2}}
.

As we have reviewed in section
2
, the Hilbert space of
{\cal P}_{\Delta}
consists of all single-row representations of
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
with multiplicity one for each. An obvious orthogonal and normalizable basis is

\displaystyle|n\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}},\,\,\,\,\,n\in\mathbb{Z},\,\,\,\,\,1\leq a_{i}\leq d+1

(D.2)

The indices
(a_{1},a_{2},\cdots,a_{n})
are symmetric and traceless, and hence for a fixed
n
, the different components
|n\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}
furnish the spin-
n
representation of
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
. By construction,
|n\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}
is an eigenstate of
L_{0a}^{2}

\displaystyle L_{0a}^{2}|n\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}=-\lambda_{n}|n\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}},\,\,\,\,\,\lambda_{n}=\Delta\bar{\Delta}+n(n+d-1)

(D.3)

The normalization of inner product is chosen to be

\displaystyle\text{With summation}:\,\,_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}\langle n|n\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}=D^{d+1}_{n}

(D.4)

where
D^{d+1}_{n}=\frac{(d+2n-1)\Gamma(n+d-1)}{\Gamma(d)\Gamma(n+1)}
denotes the dimension of the spin-
n
representation of
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
. For example, when
n=2
, the normalization in (
D.4
) yields

{}_{a_{1}a_{2}}\langle 2|2\rangle_{b_{1}b_{2}}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\delta_{a_{1}b_{1}}\delta_{a_{2}b_{2}}+\delta_{a_{1}b_{2}}\delta_{a_{2}b_{1}}-\frac{2}{d+1}\delta_{a_{1}a_{2}}\delta_{b_{1}b_{2}}\right)

(D.5)

In the index free formalism, all
|n\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}
can be encoded in

\displaystyle|n,z\rangle\equiv|n\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}z^{a_{1}}\cdots z^{a_{n}}

(D.6)

where
z^{a}
is an auxiliary null vector in
\mathbb{C}^{d+1}
. Introducing a different null vector
w^{a}
, the inner product (
D.4
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\langle n,\bar{w}|m,z\rangle=\delta_{n,m}(\bar{w}\cdot z)^{n}

(D.7)

Stripping off
z^{a}
is realized by the so-called interior derivative

\displaystyle{\cal D}_{a}=\partial_{z^{a}}-\frac{1}{d-1+2\,z\cdot\partial_{z}}z_{a}\partial_{z}^{2}

(D.8)

Since
L_{0a}
transforms as a vector under
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
,
L_{0a}|n\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}
has the symmetry
{\mathbb{Y}}_{1}\otimes{\mathbb{Y}}_{n}
, which can be decomposed into three irreducible components:
{\mathbb{Y}}_{n-1}
,
{\mathbb{Y}}_{n+1}
and
{\mathbb{Y}}_{n,1}
. The
{\mathbb{Y}}_{n,1}
part should vanish identically because it does not belong to the
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
content of
{\cal P}_{\Delta}
.
27
27

27

When
d=2
,
{\mathbb{Y}}_{n,1}
is the same as
{\mathbb{Y}}_{n}
due to the totally antisymmetric tensor
\epsilon_{abc}
. However, one can still show that the
{\mathbb{Y}}_{n}
component cannot enter eq. (
D.9
) if
|n\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}
belongs to
{\cal F}_{\Delta}
. This is not true if
|n\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}\in{\cal F}_{\Delta,s}
where
s\not=0
.
Therefore, the action of
L_{0a}
on
|n\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}
should take the following form
28
28

28

More explicitly,
\text{trace}=\frac{n-1}{d+2n-3}\delta_{(a_{1}a_{2}}|n-1\rangle_{a_{3}\cdots a_{n})a}
in this equation.

\displaystyle L_{0a}|n\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}=\alpha_{n}|n+1\rangle_{aa_{1}\cdots a_{n}}+\beta_{n}\left(\delta_{a(a_{1}}|n-1\rangle_{a_{2}\cdots a_{n})}-\text{trace}\right)

(D.9)

where the coefficients
\alpha_{n}
and
\beta_{n}
are to be fixed. In the index-free formalism, eq. (
D.9
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle L_{0a}|n,z\rangle=\frac{\alpha_{n}}{n+1}{\cal D}_{a}|n+1,z\rangle+\beta_{n}z_{a}|n-1,z\rangle

(D.10)

Acting
{\cal D}_{a}
on both sides of (
D.10
) and summing over
a
yields

\displaystyle L_{0a_{1}}|n\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}=\beta_{n}\frac{(d+n-2)(d+2n-1)}{n(d+2n-3)}|n-1\rangle_{a_{2}\cdots a_{n}}

(D.11)

where we have used
{\cal D}^{2}=0
and

\displaystyle{\cal D}_{a}(z_{a}|n-1,z\rangle)=\frac{(d+n-2)(d+2n-1)}{d+2n-3}|n-1,z\rangle

(D.12)

Acting
L_{0a}
on both sides of (
D.10
), we obtain our first recurrence relation

\displaystyle\frac{(d+n-1)(d+2n+1)}{(n+1)(d+2n-1)}\alpha_{n}\beta_{n+1}+\alpha_{n-1}\beta_{n}=-\lambda_{n}

(D.13)

Imposing the initial condition
\beta_{0}=0
, we can completely fix the product
\alpha_{n}\beta_{n+1}

\displaystyle\alpha_{n}\beta_{n+1}=-\frac{(n+1)(\Delta+n)(\bar{\Delta}+n)}{d+2n+1}

(D.14)

To derive another recurrence relation, let’s compute the norm of
L_{0a}|n,z\rangle
using (
D.7
) and (
D.10
)

\displaystyle\langle n,\bar{w}|L_{0a}^{\dagger}L_{0a}|n,z\rangle=\lambda_{n}(\bar{w}\cdot z)^{n}=\frac{|\alpha_{n}|^{2}}{(n+1)^{2}}{\cal D}_{a}^{(z)}{\cal D}_{a}^{(\bar{w})}(\bar{w}\cdot z)^{n+1}+|\beta_{n}|^{2}(\bar{w}\cdot z)^{n}

(D.15)

which yields

\displaystyle|\alpha_{n}|^{2}\frac{(d+n-1)(d+2n+1)}{(n+1)(d+2n-1)}+|\beta_{n}|^{2}=\lambda_{n}

(D.16)

The two recurrence relations (
D.14
) and (
D.16
) are invariant under an
n
-dependent
\text{U}(1)
transformation:

\alpha_{n}\to e^{i\theta_{n}}\alpha_{n}
and
\beta_{n+1}\to e^{-i\theta_{n}}\beta_{n+1}
. The angle
\theta_{n}
can always be absorbed into a redefinition of
|n\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}
. We will fix this
\text{U}(1)
degree of freedom by choosing
\alpha_{n}\propto\Delta+n
and
\beta_{n+1}\propto\bar{\Delta}+n
. In particular,
\alpha_{0}=\frac{\Delta}{\sqrt{d+1}}
. Then the solution of (
D.14
) and (
D.16
) is uniquely determined

\displaystyle\alpha_{n}=\sqrt{\frac{n+1}{d+2n+1}}(\Delta+n),\,\,\,\,\,\beta_{n}=-\sqrt{\frac{n}{d+2n-1}}(\bar{\Delta}+n-1)

(D.17)

The eq. (
D.17
) requires a minor modification if we replace the principal series
{\cal P}_{\Delta}
by complementary series
\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}
with
\Delta=\frac{d}{2}+\mu
. The recurrence relations (
D.14
) and (
D.16
) still hold in this case, but
\alpha_{n}\propto\Delta+n
is not a proper choice anymore. It is easy to check that
\alpha_{0}=\frac{\Delta}{\sqrt{d+1}}
does not satisfy the
n=0
case of eq. (
D.16
) when
\Delta
is real. Instead, we will fix the
\text{U}(1)
degree of freedom differently by choosing all
\alpha_{n}
to be real and positive. In particular, it means
\alpha_{0}=\sqrt{\frac{\Delta\bar{\Delta}}{d+1}}
.
The general expressions of
\alpha_{n}
and
\beta_{n}
in eq. (
D.17
) should change accordingly as

\displaystyle\alpha_{n}=\sqrt{\frac{(n+1)(\Delta+n)(\bar{\Delta}+n)}{d+2n+1}},\,\,\,\,\,\beta_{n}=-\sqrt{\frac{n(\Delta+n-1)(\bar{\Delta}+n-1)}{d+2n-1}}

(D.18)

For principal series or complementary series, the coefficients
\alpha_{n}
and
\beta_{n}
given by eq. (
D.17
) or eq. (
D.18
) are nonvanishing, and hence these representations are irreducible. Pictorially, this property is shown in fig.(
D.1
).

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Y}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Y}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Y}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Y}_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Y}_{4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{0a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{0a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{0a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{0a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{0a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{0a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{0a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{0a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{0a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{0a}}

Figure D.1
:
The action of noncompact generators of
{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)
in scalar principal and complementary series representations.

If we formally take
\Delta=d+p-1,p\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
in eq. (
D.18
), which is clearly out of the unitarity regime of complementary series,
\beta_{p}
vanishes and all the rest
\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}
are novanishing. Using the definition of
\alpha_{n}
and
\beta_{n}
given by eq. (
D.9
), the vanishing of
\beta_{p}
implies that
\{|n\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}},n\geq p\}
span an invariant subspace of
\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}
. See fig.(
D.2
) for the
p=2
example:

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{0a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{0a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{0a}}

:
