{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)
in
{\cal V}_{2,0}
.

Indeed, this invariant subspace together with the inner product (
D.4
) is the type I exceptional series representation
{\cal V}_{p,0}
. This construction manifests the
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
content of
{\cal V}_{p,0}
given by eq. (
2.7
).

Next, we apply the results above to the tensor product of
{\cal F}_{\Delta_{1}}\otimes{\cal F}_{\Delta_{2}}
. The space of
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
singlets in
{\cal F}_{\Delta_{1}}\otimes{\cal F}_{\Delta_{2}}
is spanned by

\displaystyle|\psi_{n}\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{D^{d+1}_{n}}}|n,\Delta_{1}\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}|n,\Delta_{2}\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}},\,\,\,\,\,n\in\mathbb{N}

(D.19)

where we have introduced the factor
\frac{1}{\sqrt{D^{d+1}_{n}}}
such that
|\psi_{n}\rangle
is normalized to be 1. Since
|\psi_{n}\rangle
is an
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
singlet, the action of
C^{{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)}
on it is equivalent to
-L_{0,a}^{2}
due to eq. (
D.1
):

\displaystyle C^{{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)}|\psi_{n}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{D^{d+1}_{n}}}\left(L^{2}_{0a}|n,\Delta_{1}\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}|n,\Delta_{2}\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}+|n,\Delta_{1}\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}L^{2}_{0a}|n,\Delta_{2}\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{\sqrt{D^{d+1}_{n}}}L_{0a}|n,\Delta_{1}\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}L_{0a}|n,\Delta_{2}\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}

(D.20)

Using eq. (
D.3
), we can easily write down the diagonal entry
\langle\psi_{n}|C^{{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)}|\psi_{n}\rangle
, which corresponds to the first line of (
D
)

\displaystyle\langle\psi_{n}|C^{{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)}|\psi_{n}\rangle=\Delta_{1}\bar{\Delta}_{1}+\Delta_{2}\bar{\Delta}_{2}+2n(n+d-1)

(D.21)

Combining (
D.9
), (
D.17
) and the second line of (
D
), we obtain the off-diagonal entry

\displaystyle\langle\psi_{n+1}|C^{{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)}|\psi_{n}\rangle=-2\sqrt{\frac{D^{d+1}_{n+1}}{D^{d+1}_{n}}}\alpha_{n}(\Delta_{1})\alpha_{n}(\Delta_{2})

(D.22)

which is the complex conjugate of
\langle\psi_{n}|C^{{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)}|\psi_{n+1}\rangle
. When both
{\cal F}_{\Delta_{i}}
belong to principal series, plugging eq. (
D.17
) into eq. (
D.22
) yields

\displaystyle{\cal P}_{\Delta_{1}}\otimes{\cal P}_{\Delta_{2}}:\quad\langle\psi_{n+1}|C^{{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)}|\psi_{n}\rangle=-\sqrt{\frac{(n+1)(n+d-1)}{(n+\frac{d-1}{2})(n+\frac{d+1}{2})}}(\Delta_{1}+n)(\Delta_{2}+n)

(D.23)

and when both
{\cal F}_{\Delta_{i}}
belong to complementary series, plugging eq. (
D.18
) into eq. (
D.22
) yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\Delta_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{C}_{\Delta_{2}}:\hskip 9.24994pt\langle\psi_{n+1}|C^{{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)}|\psi_{n}\rangle=-\sqrt{\frac{(n+1)(n+d-1)(\Delta_{1}+n)(\bar{\Delta}_{1}+n)(\Delta_{2}+n)(\bar{\Delta}_{2}+n)}{(n+\frac{d-1}{2})(n+\frac{d+1}{2})}}

(D.24)

Appendix E
Absence of exceptional series in
{\cal F}_{\Delta_{1}}\otimes{\cal F}_{\Delta_{2}}

In this appendix, we will show that the tensor product
{\cal F}_{\Delta_{1}}\otimes{\cal F}_{\Delta_{2}}
does not contain any exceptional series, where
{\cal F}_{\Delta_{i}}
belong to either principal series or complementary series. The main computational tool involved in our proof is established in the previous appendix.

For the type I exceptional series, we will use
{\cal V}_{1,0}
as an example to illustrate the main idea of the proof, which can be easily generalized to any
{\cal V}_{p,0}
. Its
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
content are given by

\displaystyle\left.{\cal V}_{1,0}\right|_{{\text{SO}}(d+1)}=\bigoplus_{n\geq 1}{\mathbb{Y}}_{n}

(E.1)

As a result of (
E.1
), any state
|\psi\rangle_{a}
in the
{\mathbb{Y}}_{1}
component should satisfy

\displaystyle L_{0a}|\psi\rangle_{a}=0,\,\,\,\,\,L_{0a}|\psi\rangle_{b}-L_{0b}|\psi\rangle_{a}=0

(E.2)

We will show that such states do not exists in
{\cal F}_{\Delta_{1}}\otimes{\cal F}_{\Delta_{2}}
. First, notice that a generic state with the
{\mathbb{Y}}_{1}
symmetry takes the following form

\displaystyle|\psi\rangle_{a_{1}}=\sum_{n\geq 0}\left(c_{n}|n+1,\Delta_{1}\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n+1}}|n,\Delta_{2}\rangle_{a_{2}\cdots a_{n+1}}+d_{n}|n,\Delta_{1}\rangle_{a_{2}\cdots a_{n+1}}|n+1,\Delta_{2}\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n+1}}\right)

(E.3)

Imposing the constraint
L_{0[a_{1}}|\psi\rangle_{b_{1}]}=0
yields
c_{n}\alpha_{n}(\Delta_{2})=d_{n}\alpha_{n}(\Delta_{1})
. Imposing the other constraint
L_{a_{1}}|\psi\rangle_{a_{1}}=0
leads to
c_{0}\beta_{1}(\Delta_{1})+d_{0}\beta_{1}(\Delta_{2})=0
and

\displaystyle c_{n-1}\alpha_{n-1}(\Delta_{2})+d_{n-1}\alpha_{n-1}(\Delta_{1})+\left(c_{n}\beta_{n+1}(\Delta_{1})+d_{n}\beta_{n+1}(\Delta_{2})\right)\frac{(d+n-1)(d+2n+1)}{(n+1)(d+2n-1)}=0

(E.4)

Using (
D.14
), we find
\alpha_{0}(\Delta_{1})\beta_{1}(\Delta_{1})+\alpha_{0}(\Delta_{2})\beta_{1}(\Delta_{2})\not=0
which implies that
c_{0},d_{0}
vanish. Then it is easy to conclude that all
c_{n}
and
d_{n}
are identically zero. This excludes the exceptional series
{\cal V}_{1,0}
.

For the type II exceptional series, we will use
{\cal U}_{1,0}
as an example. As reviewed in section
2
, the
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
content of
{\cal U}_{1,0}
are

\displaystyle\left.{\cal U}_{1,0}\right|_{{\text{SO}}(d+1)}=\bigoplus_{n\geq 1}{\mathbb{Y}}_{n,1}

(E.5)

Unlike the other spin 1 UIRs, e.g.
{\cal F}_{\Delta,1}
,
{\cal U}_{1,0}
does not contain the vector representation of
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
. It implies that for any state
|\chi\rangle_{a,b}
with symmetry
{\mathbb{Y}}_{1,1}
, i.e.
|\chi\rangle_{a,b}=-|\chi\rangle_{b,a}
,
L_{0b}|\chi\rangle_{a,b}
should vanish since it carries the spin 1 representation of
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
. We will show that such
|\chi\rangle_{a,b}
does not exist in the tensor product
{\cal F}_{\Delta_{1}}\otimes{\cal F}_{\Delta_{2}}
. Let’s first write down a basis of states in
{\cal F}_{\Delta_{1}}\otimes{\cal F}_{\Delta_{2}}
that carry the two-form symmetry of
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
:

\displaystyle|\chi_{n})_{a_{1},b_{1}}=|n,\Delta_{1}\rangle_{a_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{n}}|n,\Delta_{2}\rangle_{b_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{n}}-(a_{1}\leftrightarrow b_{1}),\,\,\,\,\,n\geq 1

(E.6)

Then a generic state
|\chi\rangle_{a,b}
belonging to
{\mathbb{Y}}_{1,1}
should be a linear combination of
|\chi_{n})_{a,b}
, e.g.

\displaystyle|\chi\rangle_{a,b}=\sum_{n\geq 1}c_{n}|\chi_{n})_{a,b}

(E.7)

Computing
L_{b}|\chi\rangle_{a,b}
using eq. (
D.9
) and (
D.11
) yields

\displaystyle\sum_{n\geq 1}c_{n}\left(\alpha_{n}(\Delta_{1})|n\!+\!1,\Delta_{1}\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n+1}}|n,\Delta_{2}\rangle_{a_{2}\cdots a_{n+1}}\!+\!\frac{(n\!+\!d\!-\!1)\beta_{n}(\Delta_{2})}{n}|n,\Delta_{1}\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}|n\!-\!1,\Delta_{2}\rangle_{a_{2}\cdots a_{n}}\right)

\displaystyle-\sum_{n\geq 1}c_{n}\left(\alpha_{n}(\Delta_{2})|n,\!\Delta_{1}\rangle_{a_{2}\cdots a_{n+1}}|n\!+\!1,\!\Delta_{2}\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n+1}}\!+\!\frac{(n\!+\!d\!-\!1)\beta_{n}(\Delta_{1})}{n}|n\!-\!1,\!\Delta_{1}\rangle_{a_{2}\cdots a_{n}}|n,\!\Delta_{2}\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}\right)

(E.8)

Requiring
L_{b}|\chi\rangle_{a,b}=0
gives us two recurrence relations

\displaystyle c_{n}\alpha_{n}(\Delta_{1})+c_{n+1}\beta_{n+1}(\Delta_{2})\frac{n+d}{n+1}=0

\displaystyle c_{n}\alpha_{n}(\Delta_{2})+c_{n+1}\beta_{n+1}(\Delta_{1})\frac{n+d}{n+1}=0

(E.9)

and one initial condition
c_{1}=0
. Apparently, the only solution is
c_{n}=0
for
n\geq 1
. Therefore
{\cal F}_{\Delta_{1}}\otimes{\cal F}_{\Delta_{2}}
does not contain
{\cal U}_{1,0}
.

Although we have excluded
{\cal U}_{1,0}
in
{\cal F}_{\Delta_{1}}\otimes{\cal F}_{\Delta_{2}}
, let’s still compute the norm of all
|\chi_{n})_{a,b}
, since it is needed in subsection
4.4
for the tensor product of
{\cal V}_{1,0}\otimes{\cal V}_{1,0}
. By definition, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\,_{a_{1},b_{1}}(\chi_{n}|\chi_{n})_{a_{1},b_{1}}

\displaystyle=\,_{a_{1}c_{2}\cdots c_{n}}\langle n|n\rangle_{a_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{n}}\,{}_{b_{1}c_{2}\cdots c_{n}}\langle n|n\rangle_{b_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{n}}

\displaystyle-\,_{a_{1}c_{2}\cdots c_{n}}\langle n|n\rangle_{b_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{n}}\,{}_{b_{1}c_{2}\cdots c_{n}}\langle n|n\rangle_{a_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{n}}

(E.10)

where we have suppressed the label of scaling dimensions because they are irrelevant for the calculation here.
To compute each term in (
E
), consider the state

\displaystyle|S\rangle_{a_{1}b_{1},c_{1}\cdots c_{n}}\equiv\,_{b_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{n}}\langle n|n\rangle_{c_{1}c_{2}\cdots c_{n}}|n\rangle_{a_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{n}}

(E.11)

in terms of which, eq. (
E
) can be rewritten in the following form

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\,_{a_{1},b_{1}}(\chi_{n}|\chi_{n})_{a_{1},b_{1}}=\,_{a_{1}c_{2}\cdots c_{n}}\langle n|S\rangle_{a_{1}b_{1},b_{1}c_{2}\cdots c_{n}}-\,_{b_{1}c_{2}\cdots c_{n}}\langle n|S\rangle_{a_{1}b_{1},a_{1}c_{2}\cdots c_{n}}

(E.12)

Without doing any explicit computation, one can almost fix
|S\rangle_{a_{1}b_{1},c_{1}\cdots c_{n}}
up to two unkown coefficients
A,B

\displaystyle|S\rangle_{a_{1}b_{1},c_{1}\cdots c_{n}}=A\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}\delta_{b_{1}c_{k}}|n\rangle_{a_{1}c_{1}\cdots\hat{c}_{k}\cdots c_{n}}-B\sum_{1\leq k<\ell\leq n}\delta_{c_{k}c_{\ell}}|n\rangle_{a_{1}b_{1}c_{1}\cdots\hat{c}_{k}\cdots\hat{c}_{\ell}\cdots c_{n}}\right)

(E.13)

Contracting the indices
a_{1}
and
b_{1}
,
|S\rangle
should reduce to
|n\rangle_{c_{1}\cdots c_{n}}
because
|n\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}\,{}_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}\langle n|
is the identity operator. This condition fixes
A=\frac{1}{n}
. Next, we impose the traceless condition among the
c_{j}
indices. For instance, it we contract
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
, the first sum in eq. (
E.11
) yields
2|n\rangle_{a_{1}b_{1}c_{2}\cdots c_{n}}
and the second double sum yields
(d+2n-3)|n\rangle_{a_{1}b_{1}c_{2}\cdots c_{n}}
. Therefore
B
is equal to
\frac{2}{d+2n-3}
. With
A
and
B
known, the remaining task is to contract
(a_{1},c_{1})
and
(b_{1},c_{1})
. After a simple calculation, we find

\displaystyle|S\rangle_{a_{1}b_{1},b_{1}c_{2}\cdots c_{n}}=\frac{(n+d-2)(2n+d-1)}{n(2n+d-3)}|n\rangle_{a_{1}c_{2}\cdots c_{n}}

\displaystyle|S\rangle_{a_{1}b_{1},a_{1}c_{2}\cdots c_{n}}=\frac{d-1}{n(2n+d-3)}|n\rangle_{b_{1}c_{2}\cdots c_{n}}

(E.14)

Plugging (
E
) into (
E.12
) and using the normalization condition (
D.4
), we find

\,{}_{a_{1},b_{1}}(\chi_{n}|\chi_{n})_{a_{1},b_{1}}=\frac{2(n+d-1)}{n}D^{d+1}_{n}=4\left(1+\frac{d-1}{2n}\right)\binom{n+d-1}{d-1}

(E.15)

When
d=3
, it becomes

\displaystyle d=3:\,\,\,\,\,_{a_{1},b_{1}}(\chi_{n}|\chi_{n})_{a_{1},b_{1}}=\frac{2(n+2)(n+1)^{2}}{n}

(E.16)

Appendix F
A review of
{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)

The Lie algebra of
{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)
is generated by
L_{IJ}=-L_{IJ},I,J=0,1,\cdots,d+2
, which are anti-hermitian operators satisfying commutation relations

\displaystyle\left[L_{IJ},L_{MN}\right]=\eta_{JM}L_{IN}-\eta_{IM}L_{JN}+\eta_{IN}L_{JM}-\eta_{JN}L_{IM}

(F.1)

where
\eta_{IJ}=\text{diag}(-1,1,\cdots,1,-1)
. A convenient basis of
{\mathfrak{so}}(2,d+1)
is

\displaystyle H=-iL_{0,d+2},\,\,\,\,\,L^{\pm}_{a}=-iL_{a0}\mp L_{a,d+2},\,\,\,\,\,M_{ab}=L_{ab}

(F.2)

where
1\leq a,b\leq d+1
and
M_{ab}
generate the
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
subgroup. Some nontrivial commutation relations are

\displaystyle[H,L^{\pm}_{a}]=\pm L^{\pm}_{a},\,\,\,\,\,[M_{ab},L^{\pm}_{c}]=\delta_{bc}L^{\pm}_{a}-\delta_{ac}L^{\pm}_{b},\,\,\,\,\,[L^{-}_{a},L^{+}_{b}]=2\delta_{ab}H-2M_{ab}

(F.3)

The
{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)
quadratic Casimir is given by

\displaystyle C^{{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)}=H(H-d-1)-L^{+}\cdot L^{-}-C^{{\text{SO}}(d+1)}

(F.4)

Consider an
{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)
subgroup, that is generated by all
L_{AB}
with
0\leq A,B\leq d+1
. Following the convention used in section
2
, the
{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)
Casimir can be expressed as

\displaystyle C^{{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)}=-L_{0a}^{2}+C^{{\text{SO}}(d+1)}=\frac{1}{4}L^{+}\cdot L^{+}+\frac{1}{4}L^{-}\cdot L^{-}+\left(\frac{1}{2}L^{+}\cdot L^{-}+\frac{d+1}{2}H+C^{{\text{SO}}(d+1)}\right)

(F.5)

A primary representation
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell}
is built upon a primary state
|\Delta,\ell\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{\ell}}
satisfying

\displaystyle H|\Delta,\ell\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{\ell}}=\Delta|\Delta,\ell\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{\ell}},\,\,\,\,\,L^{-}_{a}|\Delta,\ell\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{\ell}}=0

\displaystyle C^{{\text{SO}}(d+1)}|\Delta,\ell\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{\ell}}=-\ell(\ell+d-1)|\Delta,\ell\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{\ell}}

(F.6)

where the Casimir condition of
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
implies that the indices
(a_{1}\cdots a_{\ell})
are symmetric and traceless. We choose the inner product such that
|\Delta,\ell\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{\ell}}
is normalized as follows

\displaystyle\text{With summation}:\,\,

{}_{a_{1}\cdots a_{\ell}}\langle\Delta,\ell|\Delta,\ell\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{\ell}}=D^{d+1}_{\ell}

(F.7)

where
D^{d+1}_{\ell}
is the dimension of the spin
\ell
representation of
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
.
The Hilbert space of
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell}
is spanned by the primary state and its descendants of the form

\displaystyle L_{b_{1}}^{+}\cdots L_{b_{k}}^{+}|\Delta,\ell\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{\ell}}

(F.8)

The unitarity of
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell}
requires
\Delta\geq\frac{d-1}{2}
when
\ell=0
and
\Delta\geq d+\ell-1
when
\ell\geq 1
, known as the unitarity bound. When the unitarity bound is saturated, say
\Delta=d+\ell-1
, the primary state becomes a conserved current in the sense

\displaystyle L^{+}_{a_{1}}|\Delta\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{\ell}}=0

(F.9)

Acting with
C^{{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)}
on the primary state
|\Delta,\ell\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{\ell}}
yields its value on any state in
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell}

\displaystyle C^{{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)}\left(\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell}\right)=\Delta(\Delta-d-1)+\ell(\ell+d-1)

(F.10)

When
d=1
,
\ell
is an eigenvalue of the
{\text{SO}}(2)
subgroup and hence takes values in all integers, i.e.

\displaystyle iM_{12}|\Delta,\ell\rangle=\ell|\Delta,\ell\rangle,\,\,\,\,\,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}

(F.11)

A generic descendant can be written as

\displaystyle|n,\bar{n})=\left(L_{1}^{+}-iL_{2}^{+}\right)^{n}\left(L_{1}^{+}+iL_{2}^{+}\right)^{\bar{n}}|\Delta,\ell\rangle

(F.12)

It has scaling dimension
\Delta+n+\bar{n}
and spin
\ell+n-\bar{n}
. Fixing the normalization
\langle\Delta,\ell|\Delta,\ell\rangle=1
, then the norm of
|n,\bar{n})
becomes

\displaystyle(n,\bar{n}|n,\bar{n})=4^{n+\bar{n}}n!\bar{n}!(\Delta+\ell)_{n}(\Delta-\ell)_{\bar{n}}

(F.13)

Appendix G
Various properties of
|\phi^{s}_{n})

In this appendix, we compute the action of various
{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)
generators and their products on the states
|\phi_{n}^{s})
, defined by eq. (
5.28
). Let’s first start with the
s=0
case as a simple exercise. Consider
L^{-}_{a}|\phi_{n})
and it has to take the following form, by symmetry and level counting

\displaystyle L^{-}_{a}|\phi_{n})=\alpha_{n}\,L^{+}_{a}|\phi_{n-1})

(G.1)

where
\alpha_{n}
is an unknown coefficient. To obtain
\alpha_{n}
, we act with
L_{a}^{+}
on both sides of eq. (
G.1
)

\displaystyle\left(L^{+}\cdot L^{-}\right)|\phi_{n})=\alpha_{n}|\phi_{n})

(G.2)

which implies that
\alpha_{n}
is nothing but the eigenvalue of
|\phi_{n})
with respect to
L^{+}\cdot L^{-}
. This eigenvalue can be easily derived by using eq. (
F.4
)

\displaystyle\alpha_{n}=(\Delta+2n)(\Delta+2n-d-1)-\Delta(\Delta-d-1)=4n\left(n+\Delta-\frac{d+1}{2}\right)

(G.3)

Acting with
L_{a}^{-}
on both sides of eq. (
G.1
) yields

\displaystyle\left(L^{-}\cdot L^{-}\right)|\phi_{n})=\alpha_{n}\left(L^{-}\cdot L^{+}\right)|\phi_{n-1})

(G.4)

With the commutation relation
[L^{-}_{a},L^{+}_{b}]=2\delta_{ab}H-2M_{ab}
, we are allowed to replace
L^{-}\cdot L^{+}
by
L^{+}\cdot L^{-}+2(d+1)H
and then we have

\displaystyle\left(L^{-}\cdot L^{-}\right)|\phi_{n})

\displaystyle=\alpha_{n}(\alpha_{n-1}+2(d+1)(\Delta+2n-2))|\phi_{n-1})

\displaystyle=16\,n\,(n+\Delta-1)\left(n+\frac{d-1}{2}\right)\left(n+\Delta-\frac{d+1}{2}\right)|\phi_{n-1})

(G.5)

where we have used the eigenvalue interpretation of
\alpha_{n-1}
. The eq. (
G
) together with the normalization
(\phi_{0}|\phi_{0})=\langle\Delta|\Delta)=1
, also fixes the norm of all
|\phi_{n})

\displaystyle(\phi_{n}|\phi_{n})=16^{n}\,n!\,(\Delta)_{n}\left(\frac{d+1}{2}\right)_{n}\left(\Delta-\frac{d-1}{2}\right)_{n}

(G.6)

For any
s\geq 1
, using the same symmetry argument, we can write down the most general form of
L^{-}_{a}|\phi^{s}_{n})
as

\displaystyle L^{-}_{a}|\phi^{s}_{n})=\beta_{n}^{s}\,L^{+}_{a}\,|\phi^{s}_{n-1})+\gamma_{n}^{s}\,z_{a}\,|\phi_{n}^{s-1})

(G.7)

where
\beta_{n}^{s}
and
\gamma_{n}^{s}
are coefficients to be fixed. Acting with
L_{a}^{+}
on both sides of eq.(
G.7
) yields that
|\phi_{n}^{s})
is eigenstate of
L^{+}\cdot L^{-}
with eigenvalue
\alpha_{n}^{s}\equiv\beta_{n}^{s}+\gamma_{n}^{s}
. The latter can be easily computed via eq. (
F.4
), noticing that
|\phi_{n}^{s})
has energy
\Delta+s+2n
and spin
s

\displaystyle\alpha_{n}^{s}=(\Delta+s+2n)(\Delta+s+2n-d-1)-\Delta(\Delta-d-1)+s(s+d-1)

(G.8)

In particular, when
n=0
,
\beta_{0}^{s}
vanishes and then
\gamma_{0}^{s}=\alpha_{0}^{s}=2s(s+\Delta-1)
. The vanishing of
\beta_{0}^{s}
also implies that
|\phi_{0}^{s})
is annihilated by
z\cdot L^{-}
. To obtain
\beta_{n}^{s}
and
\gamma_{n}^{s}
separately, we contract both sides of eq.(
G.7
) with
z_{a}
:
z\cdot L^{-}|\phi_{n}^{s})=\beta_{n}^{s}|\phi^{s+1}_{n-1})
.
Then the remaining task is to compute
z\cdot L^{-}|\phi_{n}^{s})
explicitly

\displaystyle z\cdot L^{-}|\phi_{n}^{s})

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{n-1}\left(L^{+}\cdot L^{+}\right)^{n-m-1}\left(4\,z\cdot L^{+}\,H-4\,z_{a}\,L^{+}_{b}M_{ab}-2(d-1)z\cdot L^{+}\right)\left(L^{+}\cdot L^{+}\right)^{m}|\phi_{0}^{s})

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{n-1}\left(4(\Delta+s+2m)-2(d-1)-4s\right)|\phi^{s+1}_{n-1})=4n\left(n+\Delta-\frac{d+1}{2}\right)|\phi^{s+1}_{n-1})

(G.9)

where we have used
z\cdot L^{-}|\phi_{0}^{s})=0
and
z_{a}M_{ab}|\phi_{0}^{s})=sz_{b}|\phi_{0}^{s})
. Altogether, we get

\displaystyle\beta_{n}^{s}=4n\left(n+\Delta-\frac{d+1}{2}\right),\quad\gamma_{n}^{s}=\alpha_{n}^{s}-\beta_{n}^{s}

(G.10)

With
\beta_{n}^{s}
and
\gamma_{n}^{s}
derived, we can proceed to compute the action of
L^{-}\cdot L^{-}
on
|\phi_{n}^{s})

\displaystyle L^{-}\cdot L^{-}|\phi_{n}^{s})

\displaystyle=\beta_{n}^{s}\left(2(d+1)H+L^{+}\cdot L^{-}\right)|\phi^{s}_{n-1})+\gamma_{n}^{s}\,z_{a}\left(\beta_{n}^{s-1}\,L^{+}_{a}\,|\phi^{s-1}_{n-1})+\gamma_{n}^{s}\,z_{a}\,|\phi_{n}^{s-2})\right)

\displaystyle=\left[\beta_{n}^{s}\left(2(d+1)(\Delta+s+2n-2)+\alpha_{n-1}^{s}\right)+\gamma_{n}^{s}\beta_{n}^{s-1}\right]|\phi_{n-1}^{s})

\displaystyle=16n(n+s+\Delta-1)\left(n+\Delta-\frac{d+1}{2}\right)\left(n+s+\frac{d-1}{2}\right)|\phi_{n-1}^{s})

(G.11)

which fixes the norm of
|\phi_{n}^{s})
up to an
n
-independent factor:

\displaystyle\frac{(\phi_{n}^{s}|\phi_{n}^{s})}{(\phi_{0}^{s}|\phi_{0}^{s})}=16^{n}n!(s+\Delta)_{n}\left(\Delta-\frac{d-1}{2}\right)_{n}\left(s+\frac{d+1}{2}\right)_{n}

(G.12)

Appendix H
Various properties of
|s,n)_{a_{1}\cdots a_{s},b_{1}\cdots b_{s}}

In this appendix, we derive the action of
C^{{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)}
on the states
|s,n)
(dropping spin indices for the simplicity of notation), defined in eq. (
5.42
), for
s=0
and
s=1
. For
C^{{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)}
, we use eq. (
F.5
)

\displaystyle C^{{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)}=\frac{1}{4}L^{+}\cdot L^{+}+\frac{1}{4}L^{-}\cdot L^{-}+\left(\frac{1}{2}L^{+}\cdot L^{-}+\frac{d+1}{2}H+C^{{\text{SO}}(d+1)}\right)

(H.1)

where the first term maps
|s,n)
to
\frac{1}{4}|s,n+1)
and the last term admits
|s,n)
as an eigenstate. To compute this eigenvalue, we can use the
{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)
Casimir, c.f. eq. (
F.4
), which yields the action of
L^{+}\cdot L^{-}
on
|s,n)

\displaystyle L^{+}\cdot L^{-}|s,n)

\displaystyle=\left(H(H-d-1)-C^{{\text{SO}}(d+1)}({\mathbb{Y}}_{ss})-C^{{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)}(\mathcal{R}_{\Delta,\ell})\right)|s,n)

\displaystyle=\left((2n+\ell)(2\Delta+2n+\ell-d-1)+C^{{\text{SO}}(d+1)}({\mathbb{Y}}_{\ell})-C^{{\text{SO}}(d+1)}({\mathbb{Y}}_{ss})\right)|s,n)

(H.2)

and hence

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{2}L^{+}\!\cdot\!L^{-}\!+\!\frac{d\!+\!1}{2}H\!+\!C^{{\text{SO}}(d\!+\!1)}\right)|s,n)\!=\!\left[2n(n\!+\!\Delta\!+\!\ell)\!+\!\frac{(d\!+\!2\ell\!+\!1)\Delta\!-\!(d\!-\!1)\ell}{2}\!+\!C^{{\text{SO}}(d)}({\mathbb{Y}}_{s})\right]|s,n)

(H.3)

where we have used
C^{{\text{SO}}(d+1)}({\mathbb{Y}}_{\ell})=-\ell(\ell+d-1)
and
\frac{1}{2}C^{{\text{SO}}(d+1)}({\mathbb{Y}}_{ss})=C^{{\text{SO}}(d)}({\mathbb{Y}}_{s})=-s(s+d-2)
. So the only nontrivial task is

L^{-}\cdot L^{-}|s,n)
. We will compute it explicitly for
s=0
and
s=1
. Before diving into the computation, we write down a commutation relation that will be used a lot

\displaystyle[L^{-}_{a},L^{+}\cdot L^{+}]

\displaystyle=2(\delta_{ab}H-M_{ab})L^{+}_{b}+2L^{+}_{b}(\delta_{ab}H-M_{ab})

\displaystyle=4L_{a}^{+}H-4L^{+}_{b}M_{ab}-2(d-1)L^{+}_{a}.

(H.4)

H.1
s=0

With eq. (
H
), we first compute the action of
L^{-}_{a}
on
|0,n)

\displaystyle L^{-}_{a}|0,n)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=0}^{n-1}(L^{+}\!\cdot\!L^{+})^{n-t-1}\left(4L_{a}^{+}H-4L^{+}_{b}M_{ab}-2(d-1)L^{+}_{a}\right)|0,t)+(L^{+}\!\cdot\!L^{+})^{n}L^{-}_{a}|0,0)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=0}^{n-1}\left(4(\Delta+\ell+2t)-2(d-1)\right)L_{a}^{+}|0,n-1)+(L^{+}\cdot L^{+})^{n}L^{-}_{a}|0,0)

\displaystyle=4n\left(n+\Delta+\ell-\frac{d+1}{2}\right)L_{a}^{+}|0,n-1)+(L^{+}\cdot L^{+})^{n}L^{-}_{a}|0,0)

(H.5)

where we have used
M_{ab}|0,t)=0
because
|0,t)
is an
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
scalar.
Further acting with another
L^{-}_{a}
yields

\displaystyle L^{-}\cdot L^{-}|0,n)

\displaystyle=4n\left(n+\Delta+\ell-\frac{d+1}{2}\right)(L^{-}\cdot L^{+})|0,n-1)

\displaystyle+4n\left(n+\Delta+\ell-1-\frac{d+1}{2}\right)(L^{+}\cdot L^{+})^{n-1}(L^{+}\cdot L^{-})|0,0)

\displaystyle-4n(L^{+}\cdot L^{+})^{n-1}L^{+}_{b}M_{ab}L^{-}_{a}|0,0)+(L^{+}\cdot L^{+})^{n}(L^{-}\cdot L^{-})|0,0)

(H.6)

In the first line of (
H.1
), we write
L^{-}\cdot L^{+}
as
2(d+1)H+L^{+}\cdot L^{-}
, and notice that
|0,n-1)
is an eigenstate of both
H
and
L^{+}\cdot L^{-}
. The eigenvalue of any
|s,n)
with respect to
L^{+}\cdot L^{-}
, denoted by
\alpha_{s,n}
, is given by eq. (
H
)

\displaystyle\alpha_{s,n}=(\ell+2n)(\ell+2n+2\Delta-d-1)+2s(s+d-2)-\ell(\ell+d-1)

(H.7)

The second line of eq. (
H.1
) is a special case of (
H.7
) corresponding to
s=n=0
. In the third term of (
H.1
), we first replace
M_{ab}L^{-}_{a}
by the commutator
[M_{ab},L^{-}_{a}]=-dL^{-}_{b}
and then it is becomes equivalent to the second line. The last term of (
H.1
) vanishes because
(L^{-}\cdot L^{-})|0,0)
is at level
\ell-2
and it is clear that such a state cannot be an
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
singlet. Altogether, we have

\displaystyle L^{-}\cdot L^{-}|0,n)

\displaystyle=4n\left(n+\Delta+\ell-\frac{d+1}{2}\right)\left(2(d+1)(\Delta+\ell+2n-2)+\alpha_{0,n-1}\right)|0,n-1)

\displaystyle+4n\left(n+\Delta+\ell+\frac{d+1}{2}-2\right)\alpha_{0,0}|0,n-1)

\displaystyle=16n(n+\Delta+\ell-1)\left(n+\Delta-\frac{d+1}{2}\right)\left(n+\ell+\frac{d-1}{2}\right)|0,n-1)

(H.8)

H.2
s=1

For the
s=1
case, we also start with computing
L_{c}^{-}|1,n)_{a,b}

\displaystyle L^{-}_{c}|1,n)_{a,b}

\displaystyle=4n\left(n+\Delta+\ell-\frac{d+1}{2}\right)L^{+}_{c}|1,n-1)_{a,b}

\displaystyle-4n(L^{+}\cdot L^{+})^{n-1}L^{+}_{c^{\prime}}M_{cc^{\prime}}|1,0)_{a,b}+(L^{+}\cdot L^{+})^{n}L^{-}_{c}|1,0)_{a,b}

(H.9)

Since
|1,0)_{a,b}
carries the 2-form representation of
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
, we can get rid of the generator
M_{cc^{\prime}}

\displaystyle L^{+}_{c^{\prime}}M_{cc^{\prime}}|1,0)_{a,b}=\left(L^{+}_{a}|1,0)_{c,b}-L^{+}_{b}|1,0)_{c,a}\right)-\left(\delta_{ac}L^{+}_{c^{\prime}}|1,0)_{c^{\prime},b}-\delta_{bc}L^{+}_{c^{\prime}}|1,0)_{c^{\prime},a}\right)

(H.10)

where the first bracket is equal to
L^{+}_{c}|1,0)_{a,b}
, due to a Bianchi identity following from the identification of
|1,0)_{a,b}=L^{+}_{a}|\Delta\rangle_{b}-L^{+}_{b}|\Delta\rangle_{a}
as a curvature and
L^{+}_{a}
as a derivative.
Combining the first term of (
H.2
) and the first term of (
H.10
) leads to

\displaystyle L^{-}_{c}|1,n)_{a,b}

\displaystyle=(L^{+}\cdot L^{+})^{n}L^{-}_{c}|1,0)_{a,b}+4n\left(n+\Delta+\ell-\frac{d+3}{2}\right)L^{+}_{c}|1,n-1)_{a,b}

\displaystyle+4n\left(\delta_{ac}L^{+}_{c^{\prime}}|1,n-1)_{c^{\prime},b}-\delta_{bc}L^{+}_{c^{\prime}}|1,n-1)_{c^{\prime},a}\right)

(H.11)

and hence the action of
L^{-}\cdot L^{-}
can be written as a sum of the following three pieces

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=4n\left(n+\Delta+\ell-\frac{d+3}{2}\right)L^{-}\cdot L^{+}|1,n-1)_{a,b}

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle=4n\left(L^{-}_{a}L^{+}_{c}|1,n-1)_{c,b}-L^{-}_{b}L^{+}_{c}|1,n-1)_{c,a}\right)

\displaystyle I_{3}

\displaystyle=L^{-}_{c}(L^{+}\cdot L^{+})^{n}L^{-}_{c}|1,0)_{a,b}.

(H.12)

For the term
I_{1}
, it suffices to use
L^{-}\cdot L^{+}=2(d+1)H+L^{+}\cdot L^{-}
and eq. (
H.7
)

\displaystyle(L^{-}\cdot L^{+})|1,n-1)_{a,b}=\left(2(d+1)(\Delta+\ell+2n-2)+\alpha_{1,n-1}\right)|1,n-1)_{a,b}

(H.13)

The term
I_{2}
can be computed as follows:

\displaystyle L^{-}_{a}L^{+}_{c}|1,n-1)_{c,b}

\displaystyle=2(\Delta+\ell+2n-2)|1,n-1)_{a,b}-2M_{ac}|1,n-1)_{c,b}+L^{+}_{c}L^{-}_{a}|1,n-1)_{c,b}

\displaystyle=2(\Delta+\ell+2n-d-1)|1,n-1)_{a,b}+L^{+}_{c}L^{-}_{a}|1,n-1)_{c,b}

(H.14)

where

\displaystyle L^{+}_{c}L^{-}_{a}|1,n-1)_{c,b}

\displaystyle=(L^{+}\cdot L^{+})^{n-1}L^{+}_{c}L^{-}_{a}|1,0)_{c,b}+4(n-1)\left(n+\Delta+\ell-\frac{d+3}{2}\right)L^{+}_{c}L^{+}_{a}|1,n-2)_{c,b}

(H.15)

because of eq. (
H.2
).
After antisymmetrizing
a
and
b
, the second term of (
H.15
) is proportional to
|1,n-1)_{a,b}
, again as a result of Bianchi identity, and hence we have

\displaystyle L^{-}_{a}L^{+}_{c}|1,n-1)_{c,b}\!-\!(a\leftrightarrow b)

\displaystyle=4\left[\Delta\!+\!\ell\!+\!2n\!-\!d\!-\!1\!+\!(n\!-\!1)\left(n\!+\!\Delta\!+\!\ell\!-\!\frac{d\!+\!3}{2}\right)\right]|1,n\!-\!1)_{a,b}

\displaystyle+(L^{+}\cdot L^{+})^{n-1}\left(L^{+}_{c}L^{-}_{a}|1,0)_{c,b}-L^{+}_{c}L^{-}_{b}|1,0)_{c,a}\right)

(H.16)

Using the notation
|\Delta\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{s}}\equiv L^{+}_{a_{s+1}}\cdots L^{+}_{a_{\ell}}|\Delta\rangle_{a_{1}\cdots a_{\ell}}
, then
L^{-}_{a}|1,0)_{c,b}
can be written as

\displaystyle L^{-}_{a}|1,0)_{c,b}=2\delta_{ac}(\Delta+\ell-2)|\Delta\rangle_{b}+L^{+}_{c}L^{-}_{a}|\Delta\rangle_{b}-(b\leftrightarrow c)

(H.17)

Now a crucial step is to compute
L^{-}_{a}|\Delta\rangle_{b}
, which can be significantly simplified with the aid of group theory. On the one side, treating
L^{-}_{a}|\Delta\rangle_{b}
as the tensor product of two fundamental representation of
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
, it should has the structure
\bullet\oplus\tiny\hbox{}\hskip 0.0pt\vbox{\vbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt}}\hskip 0.0pt\oplus\hbox{}\hskip 0.0pt\vbox{\vbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt}}\hskip 0.0pt
group theoretically. On the other side, according to the analysis in section
5
, the
{\text{SO}}(d+1)
structures
\bullet
and

exist at level
\ell
or higher. Since
L^{-}_{a}|\Delta\rangle_{b}
is of level
\ell-2
, only the

part, which is proportional to
|\Delta\rangle_{ab}
, can be nonvanishing. With that being said,
L^{-}_{a}|\Delta\rangle_{b}=\kappa|\Delta\rangle_{ab}
and the coefficient
\kappa
is the eigenvalue of
|\Delta\rangle_{a}
with respect to
L^{+}\cdot L^{-}
. We find
\kappa=2(\Delta-d-1)(\ell-1)
by using the Casimir of
{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)
and
{\text{SO}}(2,d+1)
.
Combining all the ingredients together, we obtain

\displaystyle I_{2}=16n\left[n\left(n+\Delta-\frac{d+1}{2}\right)+\ell\left(n+\frac{\Delta-d}{2}\right)\right]|1,n-1)_{a,b}

(H.18)

For
I_{3}
, we move
L^{-}_{c}
rightwards by using (
H
), and the resulting terms can be computed using (
H.17
)

\displaystyle I_{3}

\displaystyle=4n\left(n+\Delta+\ell-\frac{d+3}{2}\right)(L^{+}\cdot L^{+})^{n-1}\left(L^{+}\cdot L^{-}\right)|1,0)_{i,j}

\displaystyle-4n(L^{+}\cdot L^{+})^{n-1}L^{+}_{c^{\prime}}M_{cc^{\prime}}L^{-}_{c}|1,0)_{a,b}

\displaystyle=4\alpha_{1,0}n\left(n+\Delta+\ell-\frac{d+3}{2}\right)|n-1,0)_{i,j}

\displaystyle+8n\left(d(\ell-1)(\Delta-d-1)+\Delta+\ell-2\right)|n-1,0)_{i,j}

(H.19)

Altogether, (
H.13
), (
H.18
) and (
H.2
) yield

\displaystyle(L^{-}\cdot L^{-})|1,n)_{a,b}=16n(n+\Delta+\ell-1)\left(n+\Delta-\frac{d+1}{2}\right)\left(n+\ell+\frac{d-1}{2}\right)|1,n-1)_{a,b}

(H.20)

which takes exactly the same form as eq. (
H.1
).

H.3
Matrix elements

For
s=0
or
1
, the action of
C^{{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)}
on
|s,n)
can be summarized as (dropping the spin indices again)

\displaystyle C^{{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)}|s,n)

\displaystyle=\left[2n(n\!+\!\Delta\!+\!\ell)\!+\!\frac{(d\!+\!2\ell\!+\!1)\Delta\!-\!(d\!-\!1)\ell}{2}\!+\!C^{{\text{SO}}(d)}({\mathbb{Y}}_{s})\right]|s,n)+\frac{1}{4}|s,n+1)

\displaystyle+16n(n+\Delta+\ell-1)\left(n+\Delta-\frac{d+1}{2}\right)\left(n+\ell+\frac{d-1}{2}\right)|s,n-1)

(H.21)

Using eq. (
H.1
) and eq. (
H.20
), we find the
n
-dependent part of
(s,n|s,n)
to be

\displaystyle(s,n|s,n)\propto 16^{n}n!(\Delta+\ell)_{n}\left(\Delta-\frac{d-1}{2}\right)_{n}\left(\ell+\frac{d+1}{2}\right)_{n}

(H.22)

and hence the action
C^{{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)}
on
|s,n\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(s,n|s,n)}}|s,n)
is

\displaystyle C^{{\text{SO}}(1,d+1)}|s,n\rangle

\displaystyle=\left[2n(n\!+\!\Delta\!+\!\ell)\!+\!\frac{(d\!+\!2\ell\!+\!1)\Delta\!-\!(d\!-\!1)\ell}{2}\!+\!C^{{\text{SO}}(d)}({\mathbb{Y}}_{s})\right]|s,n)

\displaystyle+\sqrt{(n+1)(n+\Delta+\ell)\left(n+\Delta-\frac{d-1}{2}\right)\left(n+\ell+\frac{d+1}{2}\right)}|s,n+1\rangle

\displaystyle+\sqrt{n(n+\Delta+\ell-1)\left(n+\Delta-\frac{d+1}{2}\right)\left(n+\ell+\frac{d-1}{2}\right)}|s,n-1)

(H.23)
